. We observe that

\phi
is a nonzero homorphism of
\mathbb{Z}
-graded
(
\mathbb{C}[t,t^{-1}],\mathfrak{g}
)-module and that

{\mathrm{Tens}}(\lambda,\delta,u)
and
\mathcal{F}(\chi(\lambda,\delta),u)
are
\mathbb{C}[t,t^{-1}]
-modules of rank
1
. We conclude that

\phi:{\mathrm{Tens}}(\lambda,\delta,u)\to\mathcal{F}(\chi(\lambda,\delta),u)

is an isomorphism.
∎

Second example of modules
\mathcal{F}(S,u)

We now consider the Lie superalgebras

\mathfrak{G}(H):=\mathrm{K}(1)\ltimes H\otimes\mathbb{C}[t,t^{-1},\xi],

\mathfrak{G}_{NS}(H):=\mathrm{K}_{NS}(1)\ltimes H\otimes\mathbb{C}[t,t^{-1},t^{1/2}\xi],

where
H
be a finite dimensional even vector space.
We denote indifferently as
\mathrm{K}_{*}(1)

the superalgebra
\mathrm{K}(1)
or
\mathrm{K}_{NS}(1)
.
Similarly
\mathfrak{G}_{*}(H)
denotes
\mathfrak{G}(H)
or

\mathfrak{G}_{NS}(H)
. We set
\tau=0
in the Ramond
case and
\tau=1/2
in the Neveu-Schwarz case.

Recall that the superconformal algebra
K_{*}(1)
is the space of all derivations

\partial\in\mathrm{Der\,}\mathbb{C}[t,t^{-1},t^{\tau}\xi]
of the form

\partial=\Bigl(f+\frac{g\xi}{2}\Bigr)D+\Bigl(\frac{D(f)}{2}\xi+g\Bigr)\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi}

where

f\in\mathbb{C}[t,t^{-1}]
,
g\in t^{\tau}\mathbb{C}[t,t^{-1}]
.
The
contact divergence
of the derivation

\partial
is defined as

\mathrm{div}(\partial):=D(f+\xi g).

In fact the contact divergence is twice the usual divergence.
Also the contraction of
\partial
by the form

t\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}
is

\langle\partial\mid t\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\rangle:=f+\frac{g\xi}{2},

We define the
tensor density

\mathfrak{G}_{*}(H)
-module

{\mathrm{Tens}}(\lambda,\delta,u)
as one copy

\overline{\mathbb{C}[t,t^{-1},t^{\tau}\xi]}
of

\mathbb{C}[t,t^{-1},t^{\tau}\xi]
where any

\partial\in\mathrm{K}_{*}(1)
acts as

\partial+\delta\mathrm{div}(\partial)+u\langle\frac{\mathrm{d}t}{t}\mid\partial\rangle

and any
h\otimes f\in H\otimes\mathbb{C}[t,t^{-1},t^{\tau}\xi]

acts by multiplication by
\lambda(h)f
.
When
\lambda\neq 0
, these modules are irreducible.
Otherwise they are merely the
K_{*}(1)
-modules considered in
[
5
]
.

We observe that the even part of

\mathfrak{G}_{*}(H)
is the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}(H)
considered in Section
4.3
.
Using the explicit formulas, we see that the

\mathfrak{g}(H)
-module

{\mathrm{Tens}}(\lambda,\delta,u)_{\bar{0}}

is the
module denoted as
{\mathrm{Tens}}(\lambda,\delta,u)
in
Section
4.3
, which justifies the chosen normalization for the divergence.
Moreover, as a
\mathfrak{g}(H)
-module, the odd part

{\mathrm{Tens}}(\lambda,\delta,u)_{\bar{1}}

is the
\mathfrak{g}(H)
-module

{\mathrm{Tens}}(\lambda,\delta+\frac{1}{2},u+\tau)
.

We now define the subalgebras

\mathrm{K}_{*}(1)^{\bf(1)}\subset\mathrm{K}_{*}(1)
and

\mathfrak{G}_{*}^{\bf(1)}\subset\mathfrak{G}_{*}(H)
.
Let
{\bf m}\subset\mathbb{C}[t,t^{-1},t^{\tau}\xi]
be the
maximal ideal

{\bf m}:=\langle(1-t),t^{\tau}\xi\rangle

and let
\mathrm{K}_{*}(1)^{\bf(1)}
be the Lie subalgebra of derivations vanishing at
{\bf m}
.
Explicitly a derivation

\partial=\Bigl(f+\frac{g\xi}{2}\Bigr)D+\Bigl(\frac{D(f)}{2}\xi+g\Bigr)\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi}\in\mathrm{K}_{*}(1)

belongs to
K_{*}(1)^{\bf(1)}
if and only if
f(1)=g(1)=0
.

Set

\mathfrak{G}_{*}^{\bf(1)}=\mathrm{K}_{*}(1)^{\bf(1)}\ltimes\mathbb{C}[t,t^{-1},t^{\tau}\xi].

For

F(t,\xi)=f(t)+\xi g(t)\in\mathbb{C}[t,t^{-1},t^{\tau}\xi]
, set

F(1)=f(1)=F\,\mathrm{mod}\,{\bf m}
.
Define the character

\chi(\lambda,\delta):\mathfrak{G}_{*}^{\bf(1)}\to\mathbb{C}
by

\chi(\lambda,\delta)(\partial+h\otimes f)=\mathrm{div}(\partial)(1)+\lambda(h)f(1),

for any

\partial+h\otimes f\in\mathfrak{G}(H)
, where

\partial\in\mathrm{K}_{*}(1),h\in H
and
f\in\mathbb{C}[t,t^{-1},t^{\tau}\xi]
.

As before set

\mathcal{F}(\chi(\lambda,\delta),u)=F_{u}\Bigl(\mathrm{Coind}_{\mathfrak{G}^{\bf(1)}}^{\mathfrak{G}}\,\chi(\lambda,\delta)\Bigr).

Lemma 4.5
.

For an arbitrary triple
(\lambda,\delta,u)\in H^{*}\times\mathbb{C}\times\mathbb{C}
there is an isomorphism of

\mathfrak{G}_{*}(H)
-modules

\mathcal{F}(\chi(\lambda,\delta),u)\simeq{\mathrm{Tens}}(\lambda,\delta,u).

Proof.

As before there is a natural
\mathfrak{G}(H)
-equivariant map

\phi:{\mathrm{Tens}}(\lambda,\delta,u)\to\mathcal{F}(\chi(\lambda,\delta),u).

We observe that

{\mathrm{Tens}}(\lambda,\delta,u)
and
\mathcal{F}(\chi(\lambda,\delta),u)
are
\mathbb{C}[t,t^{-1},t^{\tau}]
-modules of rank
1

and
\phi
induces an isomorphism

{\mathrm{Tens}}(\lambda,\delta,u)/{\bf m}\to\mathcal{F}(\chi(\lambda,\delta),u)/{\bf m}.

We conclude that

\phi:{\mathrm{Tens}}(\lambda,\delta,u)\to\mathcal{F}(\chi(\lambda,\delta),u)

is an isomorphism.
∎

The highest weight of
\mathcal{F}(S,u)

We arrive to the main at the key result of the chapter,
which concerns cuspidal representations of

\mathrm{W}(n)
,
\mathrm{K}(n)
,
\mathrm{CK}(6)
and their central extensions. Instead of a case-by-case analysis, we will state a general result concerning Lie superalgebras

\mathrm{L}
satisfying some properties.

As before let
\mathrm{L}
be a
\mathbb{Z}
-graded Lie superalgebra endowed with a grading element
\ell_{0}
and let

\tau\in\{0,1/2\}
be its shift.

The first property is

Axiom 1
.

The superalgebra
\mathrm{L}
is endowed with
a pair
(H,F)
where
H\subset\mathrm{L}_{{\overline{0}},0}

is an abelian subalegebra and
F\in H
.
The

\mathrm{ad}(H)
-action is diagonalisable,
which gives rise to a root space decomposition

\mathrm{L}=C_{\mathrm{L}}(H)\oplus\oplus_{\alpha\in\Delta}\mathrm{L}^{(\alpha)},

where
C_{\mathrm{L}}(H)
is the centralizer of
F
. It is assumed that

(a)

the set of roots

\Delta:=\{\alpha\in H^{*}\mid\mathrm{L}^{(\alpha)}\neq 0\hbox{ and }\alpha\neq 0\}

is finite.

(b)

\alpha(F)
is an integer for all
\alpha\in\Delta
.

Thus we can define

\Delta^{+}=\{\alpha\mid\alpha(F)>0\}\hbox{ and }

\Delta^{-}=\{\alpha\mid\alpha(F)<0\},

and there is a triangular decomposition

\mathrm{L}=\mathrm{L}^{-}\oplus C_{\mathrm{L}}(F)\oplus\mathrm{L}^{+}

where

\mathrm{L}^{\pm}=\oplus_{\alpha\in\Delta^{\pm}}\,\mathrm{L}^{(\alpha)}.

In some cases there are roots
\alpha
with
\alpha(F)=0
.

The second property concerns the structure
of the centralizer
C_{\mathrm{L}}(F)
.
Recall that the algebras
\mathfrak{g}(H)
,
\mathfrak{G}(H)
or
\mathfrak{G}_{NS}(H)
are defined in Sections
4.3
and
4.4
.

Axiom 2
.

The algebra
C_{\mathrm{L}}(F)
contains a subalgebra
\mathfrak{K}
and an ideal denoted as

\mathrm{Rad}(C_{\mathrm{L}}(F))
such that

(a)

C_{\mathrm{L}}(F)=\mathfrak{K}\ltimes\mathrm{Rad}(C_{\mathrm{L}}(F))

(b)

\mathfrak{K}
is isomorphic to

\mathfrak{g}(H)
, to
\mathfrak{G}(H)
or to
\mathfrak{G}_{NS}(H)
.

Let
\mathrm{L}^{\bf(1)}
be a subalgebra. We have already
define
\mathrm{L}^{\bf(1)}
when
\mathrm{L}=\mathrm{Vir}
or
\mathrm{K}(1)
and,
in general the definition of

\mathrm{L}^{\bf(1)}
will be similar. Thus
\mathrm{L}^{\bf(1)}
is
called the
isotropy subalgebra
. However for the purpose of the chapter we continue with an axiomatic approach.

The third property concerns the structure of
\mathrm{L}^{\bf(1)}
.
Recall that
\mathfrak{K}=\mathfrak{g}(H)
or
\mathfrak{G}_{*}(H)
. Thus set

\mathfrak{K}^{\bf(1)}=\mathfrak{g}^{\bf(1)}(H)\hbox{ or }\mathfrak{G}_{*}^{\bf(1)}(H)

accordingly and set

C_{\mathrm{L}}(F)^{\bf(1)}=\mathfrak{K}^{\bf(1)}\ltimes\mathrm{Rad}(C_{\mathrm{L}}(F)).

For

(\lambda,\delta)\in H^{*}\times\mathbb{C}
we extend the character

\chi(\lambda,\delta):\mathfrak{K}^{\bf(1)}\to\mathbb{C}
to

C_{\mathrm{L}}(F)^{\bf(1)}
by requiring that it vanishes on

\mathrm{Rad}(C_{\mathrm{L}}(F))
. This character is again denoted as

\chi(\lambda,\delta)
.

We also set
T=\mathrm{L}/\mathrm{L}^{\bf(1)}
.

Axiom 3
.

We assume

(a)
\mathrm{L}^{\bf(1)}\cap C_{\mathrm{L}}(F)=C_{\mathrm{L}}(F)^{\bf(1)}

and
\dim\,T<\infty
.

Since
H
lies in
\mathrm{L}^{\bf(1)}
, the Cartan subalgebra

H
acts on
T
and let

T=\oplus_{\alpha}T^{(\alpha)}

be its weight decomposition. Set

\Delta^{-}(T)=\{\alpha\in\Delta^{-}\mid T^{(\alpha)}\neq 0\}.

(b) The set
\Delta^{-}(T)
consists of
m
linearly
independent roots
\beta_{1},\cdots,\beta_{m}
.

(c) For any
\beta\in\Delta^{-}(T)
the space

T^{(\beta)}
has dimension
1
and

C_{\mathrm{L}}(F)^{\bf(1)}
acts by multiplication by

\chi(\beta,\delta^{-})
, for some
\delta^{-}\in\mathbb{C}
.

Let
(\mu,\delta)\in H^{*}\times\mathbb{C}
.
By Axiom 3(a) we have

C_{\mathrm{L}}(F)^{\bf(1)}=C_{\mathrm{L}^{\bf(1)}}(F)
.
Hence there exist a unique simple

\mathrm{L}^{\bf(1)}
-module
L(\mu,\delta)

with highest weight
\mu
such that

C_{\mathrm{L}}(F)^{\bf(1)}
acts on

L(\mu,\delta)^{(\mu)}

by the character
\chi(\mu,\delta)
.
We also assume that the
1
-dimensional space

\mathrm{L}(\mu,\delta)^{(\mu)}
has parity
m\hbox{ modulo }2
.

We now assume that
Axioms (1-3) are satisfied and we set

\omega=-\sum_{i=1}^{m}\,\beta_{i}.

Lemma 4.6
.

Assume that

L(\mu,\delta)
is finite dimensional and set

M:=\mathrm{Ind}_{\mathrm{L}^{\bf(1)}}^{\mathrm{L}}\,L(\mu,\delta)^{*}.

The lowest weight of the
\mathrm{L}
-module
M

is
-\mu-\omega.

Moreover there is an isomorphism of
C_{\mathrm{L}}(F)
-modules

M^{(-\mu-\omega)}\simeq\mathrm{Ind}_{C_{\mathrm{L}}(F)^{\bf(1)}}^{C_{\mathrm{L}}(F)}\,\,\chi(-\omega-\mu,m\delta^{-}-\delta).

Proof.

Set
S=L(\mu,\delta)^{*}
.
Choose a nonzero
v\in S^{(-\mu)}
of weight
-\mu
and
some elements

\eta_{1}\in\mathrm{L}^{(\beta_{1})}\setminus(\mathrm{L}^{\bf(1)})^{(\beta_{1})},\cdots,\eta_{m}\in\mathrm{L}^{(\beta_{m})}\setminus(\mathrm{L}^{\bf(1)})^{(\beta_{m})}.

We claim that

a.\eta_{1}\cdots\eta_{m}v=\chi(-\omega-\mu,m\delta^{-}-\delta)(a)\eta_{1}\cdots\eta_{m}v,

for any
a\in C_{\mathrm{L}}(F)^{\bf(1)}
.

Let
G\subset\mathrm{L}
be the subalgebra generated by
the subspaces
\mathrm{L}^{(\beta_{i})}
for
1\leq i\leq m

and set
G^{\bf(1)}=G\cap\mathrm{L}^{\bf(1)}
. Since the roots

\beta_{1},\cdots,\cdots\beta_{m}
are linearly independent,
we have

\mathrm{ad}(\mathrm{L}^{(\beta_{i_{1}})})\circ\mathrm{ad}(\mathrm{L}^{(\beta_{i_{2}})})\circ\cdots\circ\mathrm{ad}(\mathrm{L}^{(\beta_{i_{k}})})(\mathrm{L}^{(\beta_{i_{k+1}})})\subset\mathrm{L}^{\bf(1)}

for any
k\geq 1
. It follows that
G^{\bf(1)}
is an ideal
of
\mathrm{G}
and that

G/G^{\bf(1)}
is an abelian Lie superalgebra.

Set
\chi_{i}=\chi(\beta_{i},\delta^{-})
,

\chi_{0}=\chi(-\mu,-\delta)
and

\chi=\chi(-\omega-\mu,m\delta^{-}-\delta)
.
Let
a\in C^{\bf(1)}
. By definition we have

a.v=\chi_{0}(a)v

and by
Axiom (
AX3)
(c), we have

[a,\eta_{i}]=\chi_{i}(a)\eta_{i}+a_{i},

for some
a_{i}\in G^{\bf(1)}
.
Since
\chi(a)=\chi_{0}(a)+\sum_{i=1}^{m}\chi_{i}(a)
,
we get

a.\eta_{1}\cdots\eta_{m}v=\chi(a)\eta_{1}\cdots\eta_{m}v+\sum_{i=1}^{m}\eta_{1}\cdots\eta_{i-1}a_{i}\eta_{i+1}\cdots\eta_{m}.v

Since
G^{\bf(1)}
is an ideal,

\eta_{1}\cdots\eta_{i-1}a_{i}\eta_{i+1}\cdots\eta_{m}\in U(G)G^{\bf(1)}U(G)=U(G)G^{\bf(1)}.

However any element in
G^{\bf(1)}
is a sum of
negative root vectors. Thus we have
G^{\bf(1)}.v
.
It follows that

a.\eta_{1}\cdots\eta_{m}v=\chi(a)\eta_{1}\cdots\eta_{m}v,

which proves the claim.

Set

T^{\pm}=\oplus_{\alpha\in\Delta^{\pm}}T^{(\alpha)}.

The hypotheses imply that

T^{(0)}\simeq W/W^{\bf(1)}
.

First consider the case
W=\mathrm{Vir}
.
In that case
T^{(0)}=\mathbb{C}\ell_{0}\hbox{ modulo }\mathrm{L}^{\bf(1)}
.
Thus by Poincaré-Birkhoff-Witt there is an isomorphism
of
H
-modules

M\simeq\mathbb{C}[\ell_{0}]\otimes\wedge T^{+}\otimes\wedge T^{-}\otimes S.

Therefore the lowest weight of
M
is

-\omega-\mu
and we have

M^{(-\omega-\mu)}\simeq\mathbb{C}[\ell_{0}]\otimes\eta_{1}\cdots\eta_{m}\,v.

We observe that
\eta_{1}\cdots\eta_{m}\,v
is
an even vector. Since
C_{\mathrm{L}}(H)/C_{\mathrm{L}}(F)^{\bf(1)}\simeq\mathbb{C}\ell_{0}
it follows that

M^{(-\mu-\omega)}\simeq\mathrm{Ind}_{C_{\mathrm{L}}(F)^{\bf(1)}}^{C_{\mathrm{L}}(F)}\,\chi.

Next assume that
W=\mathrm{K}_{*}(1)
and choose

\xi\in\mathrm{K}_{*}(1)_{\overline{1}}\setminus\mathrm{L}^{(\bf 1)}_{\overline{1}}.

We have

T^{(0)}=\mathbb{C}\ell_{0}\oplus\mathbb{C}\xi\hbox{ modulo }\mathrm{L}^{\bf(1)}.

As before, we show that

M^{(-\omega-\mu)}=\mathbb{C}[\ell_{0}]\otimes\eta_{1}\cdots\eta_{m}\,v\oplus\mathbb{C}[\ell_{0}]\,\xi\otimes\eta_{1}\cdots\eta_{m}\,v

and that
\mathbb{C}\eta_{1}\cdots\eta_{m}v
is a one dimensional
C_{\mathrm{L}}(F)^{\bf(1)}
-module of character
\chi
.
It follows that

M^{(-\mu-\omega)}\simeq\mathrm{Ind}_{C_{\mathrm{L}}(F)^{\bf(1)}}^{C_{\mathrm{L}}(F)}\,\chi.

∎

We can now state the main result of the chapter.
Consider an arbitrary triple

(\lambda,\delta,u)\in H^{*}\times\mathbb{C}\times\mathbb{C}

such that the
\mathrm{L}^{\bf(1)}
-module

\mathrm{L}(\lambda,\delta)
is finite dimensional.

Recall that the datum

(\omega,m,\delta^{-})

is defined by

\omega=-\sum_{\beta\in T^{-}}\,\beta
,

m=\dim\,T^{-}
and
C_{\mathrm{L}}(F)^{\bf(1)}
acts on
each
T^{(\beta)}
with the character

\chi(\beta,\delta^{-})
.

Proposition 4.7
.

The weight
\mu+\omega
is the highest weight of the
\mathrm{L}
-module

\mathcal{F}(L(\mu,\delta),u)
. Moreover
there is an isomorphism of
C_{\mathrm{L}}(F)
-modules

\mathcal{F}(L(\mu,\delta),u)^{(\mu+\omega)}\simeq{\mathrm{Tens}}(\mu+\omega,\delta-m\delta^{-},u).

Proof.

By Lemma
4.2

the module

M:=\mathrm{Coind}_{\mathrm{L}^{\bf(1)}}^{\mathrm{L}}\,L(\mu,\delta)

is the dual of the module

\mathrm{Ind}_{\mathrm{L}^{\bf(1)}}^{\mathrm{L}}\,L(\mu,\delta)^{*}.

Thus it follows from Lemma
4.6
that

\mu+\omega
is the highest weight of
M
and that

M^{(\mu+\omega)}\simeq\mathrm{Coind}_{C_{\mathrm{L}}(F)^{\bf(1)}}^{C_{\mathrm{L}}(F)}\chi(\mu+\omega,\delta-m\delta^{-}).

Thus it follows from Lemmas
4.4
and

4.5
that

F_{u}M^{(\mu+\omega)}=\mathcal{F}(L(\mu,\delta),u)^{(\mu+\omega)}\simeq{\mathrm{Tens}}(\mu+\omega,\delta-m\delta,u).

∎

5
Representations of split extensions

Let
\mathfrak{g}
be the semi-direct product

\mathfrak{g}:=\mathrm{Vir}\ltimes H\otimes\mathbb{C}[t,t^{-1}],

where
H
denotes a finite dimensional even vector
space.

In Section
4.3
we have defined the
\mathfrak{g}
-module

{\mathrm{Tens}}(\lambda,\delta,u)

for an arbitrary triple

(\lambda,\delta,u)\in H^{*}\times\mathbb{C}\times\mathbb{C}
. When

\lambda\neq 0
it is a obviously simple
\mathfrak{g}
-module.
In this chapter we prove the following
result:

Theorem 3
.

Let
V=\oplus_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}V_{i}
be a simple
\mathbb{Z}
-graded

\mathfrak{g}
-module of growth one. Then

(a)

Either

V\simeq{\mathrm{Tens}}(\lambda,\delta,u)

for some
\delta,u\in\mathbb{C}
and
some
\lambda\in H^{*}\setminus 0
,

(b)

(H\otimes\mathbb{C}[t,t^{-1}])V=0
.

Generalities.

We summarize some easy statements that are repeatedly used throughout the paper. Let
A=A_{0}\oplus A_{1}
be a commutative superalgebra. By a derivation of

A
we mean a superderivation.

Lemma 5.1
.

Let
a\in A_{0}
be an even element with
a^{n}=0

and let
\partial_{1},\dots,\partial_{2n-1}
be derivations of
A
.
Then

(\partial_{1}a)(\partial_{2}a)\cdots(\partial_{2n-1}a)=0.

Consequently, if
A=A_{0}
its radical
\mathrm{Rad}(A)
is stable by any derivation.

Proof.

For
x\in A
we write
L_{x}\in\mathrm{End}(A)
for the left multiplication by
x
.
For an arbitrary linear transformation
\Delta:A\to A
,
set
\mathrm{ad}(a)(\Delta)=L_{a}\Delta-\Delta L_{a}
.
Since

\mathrm{ad}(a)^{2n-1}(\Delta)=\sum_{i+j=2n-1}\,(-1)^{i}{\binom{2n-1}{i}}L_{a}^{i}\Delta L_{a}^{j}

we have
\mathrm{ad}^{2n-1}(a)(\Delta)=0
.

Furthermore if
\partial
is a derivation of
A
, we have

\mathrm{ad}(a)(\partial)=-L_{\partial a}
and
\mathrm{ad}^{2}(a)(\partial)=0
.
It follows that

0=\mathrm{ad}(a)^{2n-1}(\partial_{1}\partial_{2}\cdots\cdots\partial_{2n-1})=-(2n-1)!\,L_{\partial_{1}a}L_{\partial_{2}a}\cdots L_{\partial_{2n-1}a}

which shows the formula.
∎

Let
V=\oplus_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}V_{i}
be a
\mathbb{Z}
-graded

\mathfrak{g}
-module
with all dimensions
\dim\,V_{i}
uniformly bounded by some integer
d
. (Here we do not assume that
V
is irreducible.) Abusing notation we use the same symbol
for an element
a\in\mathfrak{g}
and the associated operator on
V
.

Lemma 5.2
.

Let
h\in H
. If the operator

h(t)h(t^{-1})
is nilpotent, then

h(t)^{N(d)}V=0\hbox{ and }\hskip 2.84526pt(h\otimes\mathbb{C}[t,t^{-1}])^{N^{\prime}(d)}V=0

where
N(d)=9d-6
and
N^{\prime}(d)=18d-13
.

Proof.

Set
a=h(t)h(t^{-1})
and
\partial=t^{3}\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}
.
As
\dim\,V_{i}
is uniformly bounded by
d
, the operator
a
is nilpotent of index
d
. By Lemma
5.1
, the operator

h(t)[\partial,a]
is nilpotent of index
2d-1
.

Since

h(t)[\partial,a]=h(t)h(t^{3})h(t^{-1})-h(t)^{3}=h(t^{3})a-h(t)^{3}

h(t)^{3}
is the difference of two nilpotent elements
of indices
2d-1
and
d
. Hence
h(t)^{3}
is nilpotent of index
3d-2
, thus

h(t)^{N(d)}=0,

for
N(d)=9d-6
. Since

h\otimes\mathbb{C}[t,t^{-1}]=[\mathrm{Vir},h(t)]

Lemma
5.1
implies

(h\otimes\mathbb{C}[t,t^{-1}])^{2N(d)-1}V=0.

∎

Reduction to
\dim\,H=1

Assume now that
V
is a simple
\mathbb{Z}
-graded
\mathfrak{g}
-moduleof growth one.
In what follows we assume that

(H\otimes\mathbb{C}[t,t^{-1}])V\neq 0.

Let
R
be the commutative subalgebra of

\mathrm{End}_{\mathbb{C}}(V)
generated by
H\otimes\mathbb{C}[t,t^{-1}]
. The algebra
R
admits a
\mathbb{Z}
-grading
R=\oplus_{i}\,R_{i}
, where
R_{i}V_{j}\subset V_{i+j}
.

Lemma 5.3
.

Let
h\in H
. If the operator

h(t)h(t^{-1})
is nilpotent, then

(h\otimes\mathbb{C}[t,t^{-1}])V=0
.

Proof.

By Lemma
5.2

(h\otimes\mathbb{C}[t,t^{-1}])^{N}V=0
,

for some
N>0
. Since

\mathrm{Ker}(h\otimes\mathbb{C}[t,t^{-1}])
is a nonzero
\mathfrak{g}
-submodule
we conclude that

(h\otimes\mathbb{C}[t,t^{-1}])V=0
.

∎

We say
that an element
a\in R
is
locally nilpotent
if for an abitrary
v\in V
we have
a^{n(v)}v=0
for some
n(v)\geq 1
.
For an arbitrary
a\in R
the subspace

\{v\in V\mid a^{n(v)}v=0
for some
n(v)\geq 1\}

is a
\mathfrak{g}
-submodule of
V
. Hence the operator
a
is either locally nilpotent or injective. Moreover, since

\dim\,V_{i}\leq d
for any
i\in\mathbb{Z}
, any operator
a\in R_{0}
is either nilpotent of index
d
or is invertible.

Lemma 5.4
.

There exists a codimension one subspace

H^{\prime}\subset H
such that

(H^{\prime}\otimes\mathbb{C}[t,t^{-1}])V=0.

Moreover for
h\notin H^{\prime}
, the operator

h(t)h(t^{-1})
is invertible.

Proof.

Set

H^{\prime}=\{k\in H\mid(k\otimes\mathbb{C}[t,t^{-1}])V=0\}

and let
h\notin H^{\prime}
. By Lemma
5.3

a:=h(t)h(t^{-1})\in R_{0}
is not nilpotent. Hence
h(t)h(t^{-1})
is invertible.

Let
h^{\prime}\in H
. Since the operator
h(t):V_{0}\to V_{1}
is bijective, there is
\lambda\in\mathbb{C}
and a nonzero
v\in V_{0}
such that

(h^{\prime}(t)-\lambda h(t))v=0
. It follows
that

(h^{\prime}(t)-\lambda h(t))\,(h^{\prime}(t^{-1})-\lambda h(t^{-1}))

is nilpotent. Thus by Lemma
5.3

(h^{\prime}-\lambda h)\otimes\mathbb{C}[t,t^{-1}]
acts trivially on
V
, i.e.

h^{\prime}-\lambda h
belongs to
H^{\prime}
. Hence
H=H^{\prime}\oplus\mathbb{C}h
and

H^{\prime}
has codimension one.
∎

The algebra
R

In view of the previous considerations,
we can assume without loss of generality that

H=\mathbb{C}h
has dimension one.
Since the operator
h(t)
is invertible we have

\dim\,V_{i}=d
for some integer
d
independent of
i
.

Lemma 5.5
.

(a)

The operator
h(t)^{-1}
belongs to
R
.

(b)

The algebra
R
is isomorphic to

\mathbb{C}[h(t),h(t)^{-1}]
.

Proof.

Let
a=h(t)h(t^{-1})
and

b=a|_{V_{0}}
.
Since
b
is an invertible endomorphism of
V_{0}
,
we have

bf(b)=\mathrm{id}_{V_{0}}

for some polynomial
f
. Since
V_{i}=h(t)^{i}V_{0}
for any
i\in\mathbb{Z}
, it follows that
af(a)=\mathrm{id}
, which shows that

h(t)^{-1}
belongs to
R
.

It follows that

R=R_{0}[h(t),h(t)^{-1}].

We claim that
\mathrm{Rad}(R_{0})=0
.
Since

\mathrm{Rad}(R_{0})^{d}=0
and

\mathrm{Rad}(R)=\mathrm{Rad}(R_{0})[h(t),h(t)^{-1}],

we also have

\mathrm{Rad}(R)^{d}=0
.
By Lemma
5.1
the radical
\mathrm{Rad}(R)
is stable by
\mathrm{Vir}
, hence
\mathrm{Ker}\,\mathrm{Rad}(R)
is a
\mathfrak{g}
-submodule.
Since
\mathrm{Ker}\,\mathrm{Rad}(R)\neq 0
we conclude that

V=\mathrm{Ker}\,\mathrm{Rad}(R)
, that is
\mathrm{Rad}(R_{0})=0
, which proves the claim.

We also claim that any idempotent
a\in R_{0}
is trivial.
Let
\partial\in\mathrm{Vir}
. We have

0=\partial(a^{2}-a)=(2a-1)\partial a.

Since
(2a-1)
is invertible it follows that
\partial a=0
, that is
a

commutes with
\mathfrak{g}
. Since
V
is irreducible, we conclude that

a=0
or
1
.

The previous two claims implies that
R_{0}=\mathbb{C}
, which completes the proof.
∎

By definition the algebra
R
is generated by

h\otimes\mathbb{C}[t,t^{-1}]
and let

\rho:\mathbb{C}[t,t^{-1}]\to R

be the corresponding map.
Set
T=\rho(h(t)
. By Lemma
5.5
, we have

R=\mathbb{C}[T,T^{-1}]
.

Lemma 5.6
.

The operator
\rho(h)
is nonzero.

Proof.

Assume otherwise. Since
[e_{-1},h(t)]=-h
,
we have
[e_{-1},T]=0
. Since
e{-1}
acts by derivation on
R
, we have
[e_{-1},R]=0
. Since

h\otimes\mathbb{C}[t,t^{-1}]=\mathbb{C}h(t^{-1})\oplus[e_{-1},h\otimes\mathbb{C}[t,t^{-1}]]

we conclude that

\rho(h\otimes\mathbb{C}[t,t^{-1}])\subset\mathbb{C}T^{-1}

which contradicts that
\rho(h\otimes\mathbb{C}[t,t^{-1}])
generates the algebra
R
.
∎

By the previous lemma we can also assume that
\rho(h)=1
. As before set
T=\rho(h(t))
.

Lemma 5.7
.

For any
n\in\mathbb{Z}
we have

(a)

\rho(h(t^{n}))=T^{n}

(b)

[e_{n},f(T)]=-T^{n+1}\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}}Tf(T)

Proof.

Since
\rho(t^{n})
is homogenous
of degree
n
, we have
\rho(t^{n})=\lambda_{n}T^{n}

for some
\lambda_{n}\in\mathbb{C}
.

We prove by induction that
\lambda_{n}=1
for all
n\geq 0
. By hypothesis, we have
\lambda_{0}=\lambda_{1}=1
.
We have
[e_{-1},T]=-h=-1
hence

e_{-1}
acts over
R
as
-\mathrm{d}/\mathrm{d}T
. Thus for any

n>1
we have

n\lambda_{n-1}T^{n-1}=\rho(nh(t^{n-1}))=\rho([-e_{-1},h(t^{n})])=\lambda_{n}\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}T}T^{n}=n\lambda_{n}T^{n-1}.

Hence
\lambda_{n}=\lambda_{n-1}
which proves the claim by induction.

For an arbitrary
n,m\in\mathbb{Z}
with
m\geq 0
and
n+m\geq 0

we have

\hskip 11.38109pt[e_{n},T^{m}]=\rho([e_{n},h(t^{m})])

=(n-m)\rho(h(t^{n+m})

=(n-m)T^{n+m}
,

hence
e_{n}
acts over
R
as

-T^{n+1}\mathrm{d}/\mathrm{d}T
which shows Assertion (b).

Moreover
h(t^{n})=-[e_{n-1},h(t)]

so

\rho(h(t^{n}))=T^{n}
for any integer
n\in\mathbb{Z}

which proves Assertion (a).
∎

The Maurer-Cartan equation

Let
A
be a commutative algebra and let
\mathfrak{s}

be a Lie algebra of derivations of
A
.
By a
(\mathfrak{s},A)
-module we mean a
A
-module
V

endowed with an action of the Lie algebra

\mathfrak{s}
such that

\partial(av)=(\partial f)v+f(\partial v)

for any
\partial\in\mathfrak{s}
,
f\in A
and
v\in V
.

Furthermore assume that
V
is free as an
A
-module that is
V=A\otimes W
for some vector space
W
. For any
\partial\in\mathfrak{s}
define an endomorphism
c(\partial)\in A\otimes\mathrm{End}(W)
by the formula

c(\partial)(f\otimes w)=f\partial w

for any
f\in A
and
w\in W
.
Then the hypothesis that
V
is a
\mathfrak{s}
-module is equivalent to

[c(\partial),c(\partial^{\prime})]=c([\partial,\partial^{\prime}])-\partial c(\partial^{\prime})+\partial^{\prime}c(\partial),

which is called the
Maurer-Cartan equation
.

Let
V
be a
\mathbb{Z}
-graded
(\mathrm{Vir},\mathbb{C}[t,t^{-1}])
-module. Hence

V=\mathbb{C}[t,t^{-1}]\otimes V_{0}

and we can define the cocycle

c:\mathrm{Vir}\to\mathbb{C}[t,t^{-1}]\otimes\mathrm{End}(V_{0})
as before.
Since
c(e_{n})
is homogenous of degree
n
, we have

c(E_{n})=t^{n}\otimes a_{n}

for some
a_{n}\in\mathrm{End}(V_{0})
.
Then the Maurer-Cartan equation is equivalent to

[a_{n},a_{m}]=(n-m)a_{m+n}-na_{n}+ma_{m}.

(5)

for all
n,m\in\mathbb{Z}
.

Consider now the algebra
\mathcal{G}

with a basis

(a_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
with a bracket defined by
Equation
5
. It is easy to check that
\mathcal{G}
is a Lie algebra. We have just proved the following result.

Lemma 5.8
.

The functor
V\mapsto V_{0}
is a bijective correspondence from the class of

\mathbb{Z}
-graded
(\mathrm{Vir},\mathbb{C}[t,t^{-1}])
-modules and the class of
\mathcal{G}
-modules.

The Maurer-Cartan Lie algebra
\mathcal{G}

Let
\mathcal{G}^{+}
(respectively
\mathcal{G}^{-}
)
be the span of
(a_{n})_{n\geq-1}
(respectively
the span of
(a_{n})_{n\leq 1}
). It is easy to see
that
\mathcal{G}^{+}
and
\mathcal{G}^{-}
are Lie subalgebras
in
\mathcal{G}
.

For
n>0
, set

\Delta(n)=\sum_{k=-1}^{n}\,(-1)^{k}\binom{n+1}{k+1}\,a_{k}

\Delta(-n)=\sum_{k=-1}^{n}\,(-1)^{k}\binom{n+1}{k+1}\,a_{-k},

and let
B^{\pm}
be the sets

B^{\pm}=\{\Delta(\pm(n)\mid n>0\}.

Lemma 5.9
.

(a)

Let
n>0
. We have

[a_{-1},\Delta(n)]=n\Delta(n)

(6)

[a_{1},\Delta(-n)]=-n\Delta(-n).

(7)

(b)

An element
g=\sum_{k}x_{k}a_{k}
belongs to
[\mathcal{G},\mathcal{G}]
if and only if

\sum_{k}x_{k}=0\hbox{ and }\sum_{k}kx_{k}=0

(8)

(c)

The set
B^{+}
is a basis of

[\mathcal{G}^{+},\mathcal{G}^{+}]
and
B^{-}
is a basis of

[\mathcal{G}^{-},\mathcal{G}^{-}]
.

(d)

We have

[\mathcal{G},\mathcal{G}]=[\mathcal{G}^{+},\mathcal{G}^{+}]+[\mathcal{G}^{-},\mathcal{G}^{-}].

Proof.

The Assertions (a) is easy to prove.

Set
X:=\mathbb{C}a_{0}\oplus\mathbb{C}(a_{1}-a_{-1})
. For

g=\sum_{k}x_{k}a_{k}\in[\mathcal{G},\mathcal{G}]
it is clear that

\sum_{k}x_{k}=0\hbox{ and }\sum_{k}kx_{k}=0

(9)

It follows that

X\cap[\mathcal{G},\mathcal{G}]=0.

(10)

It is easy to see that the set

\{a_{0},a_{1}-a_{-1}\}\cup B^{+}

is a basis of
\mathcal{G}^{+}
.
By Equation (
6
)
B^{+}
lies in

[\mathcal{G}^{+},\mathcal{G}^{+}]
and by Equation (
10
)
we have

\dim\,\mathcal{G}^{+}/[\mathcal{G}^{+},\mathcal{G}^{+}]\geq 2.

Thus it follows that
\mathcal{G}^{+}/[\mathcal{G}^{+},\mathcal{G}^{+}]
has dimension exactly two and that
B^{+}
is a basis of

[\mathcal{G}^{+},\mathcal{G}^{+}]
.
The proof that
B^{-}
is a basis of

[\mathcal{G}^{-},\mathcal{G}^{-}]
is identical. Thus we have proved Assertion (c).

Equation
10
shows that

\dim\,\mathcal{G}/[\mathcal{G},\mathcal{G}]\geq 2
.
Note that

\{a_{0},a_{1}-a_{-1}\}\cup B^{+}\cup B^{-}

is a basis of
\mathcal{G}^{+}
. (Observe that

\Delta(1)=\Delta(-1)
.) Since each element

\Delta(n)
lies in
[\mathcal{G},\mathcal{G}]
we deduce that

[\mathcal{G},\mathcal{G}]
has codimension exactly two.
It has been shown that any element

g=\sum_{k}x_{k}a_{k}\hbox{ in }[\mathcal{G},\mathcal{G}]

satisfies Equation (
9
). Hence the codimension two subspace defined by
this equation is precisely
[\mathcal{G},\mathcal{G}]
which proves
Assertion (b).

It follows that
B^{+}\cup B^{-}
is a basis of

[\mathcal{G},\mathcal{G}]
. Since each basis element
\Delta(n)

belongs to
[\mathcal{G}^{+},\mathcal{G}^{+}]
or
[\mathcal{G}^{-},\mathcal{G}^{-}]
,
Assertion (d) follows.
∎

Lemma 5.10
.

Let
W
be a finite dimension Lie vector space and let

\rho:\mathcal{G}\rightarrow\mathfrak{gl}(W)

be a Lie algebra morphism.
Then
\rho(\mathcal{G})
is a solvable Lie algebra.

Proof.

By Equation
6
we have

[\rho(a_{-1}),\rho(\Delta(n))]=n\rho(\Delta(n))\hbox{ for any }n\geq 1.

Since
\mathrm{ad}(\rho(a_{-1}))
has a finite spectrum,
the nonunital algebra generated by all
\rho(\Delta(n))

for
n\geq 1
is nilpotent. By
Lemma
5.9
(c) the set
\{\Delta(n)\mid n>0\}
is a basis of
[\mathcal{G}^{+},\mathcal{G}^{+}]
, thus
the Lie algebra

\rho([\mathcal{G}^{+},\mathcal{G}^{+}])
is nilpotent. Similarly the
Lie algebra
\rho([\mathcal{G}^{-},\mathcal{G}^{-}])
is nilpotent.

By Lemma
5.9
(d) we have

\rho([\mathcal{G},\mathcal{G}]=\rho([\mathcal{G}^{+},\mathcal{G}^{+}])+\rho([\mathcal{G}^{-},\mathcal{G}^{-}]).

However it is shown in
[
21
]
that a
finite dimensional Lie
algebra which is sum, as a vector space, of two nilpotent subalgebras is solvable. Hence

\rho([\mathcal{G},\mathcal{G}])
is solvable, which implies that

\rho(\mathcal{G})
is solvable.
∎

Lemma 5.11
.

Let
\rho:\mathcal{G}\rightarrow\mathfrak{gl}(W)
be a simple
finite dimensional representation of
\mathcal{G}
. Then
V
has
dimension
1
.
Moreover there are
\delta,u\in\mathbb{C}
such that

\rho(a_{n})=-u-n\delta\hbox{ for any }n\in\mathbb{Z}.

Proof.

The first assertion follows
immediately from Lemma
5.10
.
Thus
\rho
can be identified with a linear form

\rho:\mathcal{G}\to\mathbb{C}
with
\rho([\mathcal{G},\mathcal{G}])=0
.
For any
\delta,u\in\mathbb{C}
it is easy to check
that the linear form
\rho_{\delta,u}
with

\rho_{\delta,u}(a_{n})=-u-n\delta
vanishes on

[\mathcal{G},\mathcal{G}]
.

By Lemma
5.9
(b)
\mathcal{G}/[\mathcal{G},\mathcal{G}]
has dimension two, so any linear form on
\mathcal{G}/[\mathcal{G},\mathcal{G}]
is one of the
\rho_{\delta,u}
.
∎

Proof of Theorem
3

Proof.

Recall that
\mathfrak{g}=\mathrm{Vir}\ltimes H\otimes\mathbb{C}[t,t^{-1}]
.
Let
V=\oplus_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}V_{i}
be an irreducible

\mathbb{Z}
-graded
\mathfrak{g}
-module of growth one. We assume that

(H\otimes\mathbb{C}[t,t^{-1}])V\neq 0,

otherwise the Assertion follows from Theorem
3
.

Furtermore by Lemma
5.4
we can assume that

H=\mathbb{C}h
is one dimensional. By Lemma
5.6

the operator
h|_{V}
is a nonzero scalar
so we can assume that
h|_{V}=1
.
Lemma
5.7
means that
V
is a
(\mathrm{Vir},\mathbb{C}[t,t^{-1}])
-module.

By Lemma
5.8
,
V_{0}
is an irreducible
\mathcal{G}
-module. Thus by Lemma

5.11

V_{0}
has dimension 1. In addition
Lemma
5.11
shows that each
a_{n}
acts as

-u-n\delta
for some
\delta,u\in\mathbb{C}
.
It follows easily that

V\simeq{\mathrm{Tens}}(\lambda,\delta,u)

where

\lambda(h)=1
.
This completes the proof of Theorem
3
.
∎

Three corollaries

We now state corollaries, which are used
in the next chapters.
As before, let

\mathfrak{g}=\mathrm{Vir}\ltimes(H\otimes\mathbb{C}[t,t^{-1}])

where
H=\mathbb{C}h
is one-dimensional. Let
V
be a graded

\mathfrak{g}
-module of growth one.
Here we drop the assumption that
V
is irreducible.
Set

d=\mathrm{Max}_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\,\dim\,V_{n}.

Corollary 5.12
.

Assume that
h|_{V}=0
.
Then

h(t)^{N(d)}V=0.

Proof.

We claim that
h(t)h(t^{-1})
is nilpotent.
Assume otherwise. Then there is a simple subquotient of
V

for which
h(t)h(t^{-1})
is not nilpotent, which contradicts
Lemma
5.6
which ensures that
h\neq 0
.

Therefore
h(t)h(t^{-1})
is nilpotent. The corollary follows from Lemma
5.2
.

∎

Corollary 5.13
.

Assume that
(h-1)|_{V}

is nilpotent.
Let
I\subset U(h\otimes\mathbb{C}[t,t^{-1}])
be the linear span of
the set
\{h(t^{n+m})-h(t^{m})h(t^{n})\mid n,m\in\mathbb{Z}\}
.

(a)

The operator
h(t)
is invertible.

(b)

We have
I^{d}V=0.

Proof.

Let
V^{\prime}
be an arbitrary simple subquotient of
V
. Lemma
5.3
implies that
the operator
h(t)|_{V^{\prime}}
is invertible and therefore

h(t)|_{V}
is also invertible which proves
Assertion (a).

It follows that all homogenous components
V_{i}^{\prime}
have the same dimension. Hence
the
\mathfrak{g}
-module
V
has length
\leq d
.
By Lemma
5.7

the elements

h(t^{n+m})-h(t^{m})h(t^{n})
act trivially on
V^{\prime}
. Therefore
I^{d}V=0
.
∎

Corollary 5.14
.

The operator
h|_{V}
admits only finitely many
generalized eigenvalues.

Proof.

Let
x
be a nonzero generalized eigenvalue
of the operator
h|_{V}
and let
V^{(x)}
be the corresponding generalized eigenspace. By
Corollary
5.13
, the operator
h(t)
is invertible.
Therefore
\dim\,V^{(x)}_{n}\geq 1
for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}
.
Thus
h|_{V}
admits at most
d
nonzero generalized eigenvalues, which proves the corollary.
∎

6
Representations of split extensions II

Let
\mathfrak{G}_{*}
be the semi-direct product

\mathfrak{G}:=\mathrm{K}_{*}(1)\ltimes H\otimes\mathbb{C}[t,t^{-1},t^{\tau}\xi],

where
H
denotes a finite dimensional even vector
space and where
\tau=0
if
\mathrm{K}_{*}(1)=\mathrm{K}(1)
and

\tau=\frac{1}{2}
if
\mathrm{K}_{*}(1)=\mathrm{K}_{NS}(1)
.
In Section
4.4
we have defined the
\mathfrak{G}_{*}
-module

{\mathrm{Tens}}(\lambda,\delta,u)

for an arbitrary triple

(\lambda,\delta,u)\in H^{*}\times\mathbb{C}\times\mathbb{C}
. When

\lambda\neq 0
it is a obviously simple
\mathfrak{G}_{*}
-module.
In this chapter we prove the following
result:

Theorem 4
.

Let
V=\oplus_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}V_{i}
be a simple
\mathbb{Z}
-graded

\mathfrak{G}_{*}
-module of growth one. Then

(a)

Either

V\simeq{\mathrm{Tens}}(\lambda,\delta,u)

for some
\delta,u\in\mathbb{C}
and
some
\lambda\in H^{*}\setminus 0
,

(b)

(H\otimes\mathbb{C}[t,t^{-1},t^{\tau}\xi])V=0
.

The proof follows the same scheme as the proof of
Theorem
3
with the addition of
the universal property stated in Lemma
4.5
.

Reduction to
\dim\,H=1

Let
V
be a
\mathfrak{G}
-module as before and let

d
be the upper bound of

\dim\,V_{i}:=\dim\,V_{i,\overline{0}}+\dim\,V_{i,\overline{0}}
, when
i
runs over
(1/2)\mathbb{Z}
.
In what follows we assume that

(H\otimes\mathbb{C}[t,t^{-1},t^{\tau}\xi])V\neq 0.

Lemma 6.1
.

Let
h\in H
. If the operator

h(t)h(t^{-1})
is nilpotent, then

(h\otimes\mathbb{C}[t,t^{-1},t^{\tau}\xi])V=0
.

Proof.

By Lemma
5.2

(h(t))^{N(d)}V=0
,
for some
N(d)>0
. We have

h\otimes\mathbb{C}[t,t^{-1},t^{\tau}\xi]=[\mathfrak{G},h(t)]

We deduce from Lemma
5.2
that

(h\otimes\mathbb{C}[t,t^{-1},t^{\tau}\xi])^{2N(d)-1}V=0

Since

\mathrm{Ker}\,(h\otimes\mathbb{C}[t,t^{-1},t^{\tau}\xi])
is a nonzero
\mathfrak{G}
-submodule
we conclude that

(h\otimes\mathbb{C}[t,t^{-1},t^{\tau}\xi])V=0.

∎

Lemma 6.2
.

There exists a codimension one subspace

H^{\prime}\subset H
such that
(H^{\prime}\otimes\mathbb{C}[t,t^{-1},t^{\tau}\xi])V=0
.

Moreover for
h\notin H^{\prime}
, the operator

h(t)h(t^{-1})
is invertible.

Except that we use Lemma
6.1
instead of Lemma
5.3
, the proof is the same as the proof of Lemma
5.4
.

The algebra
R

Without loss of generality, we can assume that

H=\mathbb{C}h
has dimension one. Set

\mathfrak{m}:=h\otimes\mathbb{C}[t,t^{-1},t^{\tau}\xi]

and let
R
be the
subalgebra of
\mathrm{End}_{\mathbb{C}}(V)
generated by
\mathfrak{m}
.
The superalgebra
R
is commutative and admits a
\mathbb{Z}
-grading
R=\oplus_{i}\,R_{i}
, where
R_{i}V_{j}\subset V_{i+j}
.

Since the operator
h(t)
is invertible we have

\dim\,V_{i}=d
for all
i\in\mathbb{Z}
, for some
d
.

Lemma 6.3
.

(a)

The operator
h(t)^{-1}
belongs to
R
.

(b)

The radical
\mathrm{Rad}(R)
of the algebra
R
is nilpotent of index
d
.

(c)

The operator
h|_{V}
acts as some scalar
c\neq 0
.

Proof.

The proof of Assertion (a) is the same as the proof of
Lemma
5.5
(a).

It follows that
R=R_{0}[h(t),h(t)^{-1}]

and
\mathrm{Rad}(R)=\mathrm{Rad}(R_{0})[h(t),h(t)^{-1}]
.
Since
\mathrm{Rad}(R_{0})^{d}=0
, the radical
of
R
is nilpotent of index
d
.

Since
h
lies in the center of
\mathfrak{G}
, it
acts on
V
by some scalar
c
. By lemma

6.2
the operator
h(t)h(t^{-1})
is invertible. Hence by Corollary
5.12

we have
c\neq 0
which completes the proof.
∎

By definition the action of

\mathfrak{m}=h\otimes\mathbb{C}[h(t),h(t)^{-1},t^{\tau}\xi]
provides an algebra homorphism

\rho:U(\mathfrak{m})\to R
.
By the previous lemma we can assume that
\rho(h)=1
.

Lemma 6.4
.

The algebra
R
is isomorphic to
\mathbb{C}[t,t^{-1},t^{\tau}\xi]
.
Relative to this isomorphism we have

\rho(h(t^{m}))=t^{m}\hbox{ for }m\in\mathbb{Z}\hbox{ and }

\rho(h(t^{m}\xi))=t^{m}\xi{for}m\in\tau+\mathbb{Z}.

Proof.

Consider the linear subspaces

A,B,C
in
U(\mathfrak{m})

which are defined by

A:=\mathbb{C}(h-1)

B:=\langle h(t^{n+m})-h(t^{n})h(t^{m})\mid n,m\in\mathbb{Z}\rangle

C:=\langle h(t^{n+m}\xi)-h(t^{n})h(t^{m}\xi)\mid n\in\mathbb{Z}\hbox{ and }m\in\tau+\mathbb{Z}\rangle

D:=\langle h(t^{n}\xi)h(t^{m}\xi)\mid n,m\in\tau+\mathbb{Z}\rangle,

where the notation

\langle X\rangle

stands for the linear span of an arbitrary set
X
. Set
T=A\oplus B\oplus C\oplus D
.

We show that
T
is a
\mathrm{K}(1)
-module. Let

\rho^{\prime}:U(\mathfrak{m})\to\mathbb{C}[h(t),h(t)^{-1},t^{\tau}\xi])

be the algebra morphism defined by

\rho^{\prime}(h(t^{m}))=t^{m}
and
\rho^{\prime}(h(t^{n}\xi))=t^{n}\xi

for arbitraries
m\in\mathbb{Z}
and
n\in\tau+\mathbb{Z}
. Also set

U_{2}(\mathfrak{m})=\mathbb{C}\oplus\mathfrak{m}\oplus S^{2}(\mathfrak{m}).

Since obviously

T=\mathrm{Ker}\,\rho^{\prime}\cap U_{2}(\mathfrak{m})

it follows that
T
is a
\mathrm{K}(1)
-module.

We claim that
\rho(T)=0
. Obviously
\rho(A)=0
.
It follows from Corollary
5.13

that
\rho(B)
lies in
\mathrm{Rad}(A)
. Moreover

\rho(C\oplus D)
lies in

R_{\overline{1}}R\subset\mathrm{Rad}(A)
which
shows that
\rho(T)
lies in the radical of
R
.
Hence by Lemma
6.3
we have

\rho(T)^{d}=0
. Since
\mathrm{Ker}\,\rho(T)
is a nonzero

\mathfrak{G}
-submodule, we have
\rho(T)=0
which proves the claim.

It is clear that

\mathbb{C}[h(t),h(t)^{-1},t^{\tau}\xi]=U(\mathfrak{m})/TU(\mathfrak{m})

therefore the morphism
\rho
factors through
\mathbb{C}[h(t),h(t)^{-1},t^{\tau}\xi]
. Since
\rho(h)=1
, it follows that the morphism

\mathbb{C}[h(t),h(t)^{-1},t^{\tau}\xi]\to R

is injective. We conclude that
R=\mathbb{C}[t,t^{-1},t^{\tau}\xi]
.

The second assertion follows from the proof.
∎

The subalgebra
\mathrm{K}_{*}(1)^{({\bf 1})}

Recall that
\mathrm{K}_{*}(1)
is the Ramond or the Neveu-Schwarz
superalgebra and that
\tau=0
in the first case and
\tau=1/2
in the second case.

Let
{\bf m}\subset\mathbb{C}[t,t^{-1},\xi]
be the
codimension one ideal generated by
(1-t)
and
\xi
.
We observe that

\mathrm{K}_{*}(1)^{\bf(1)}:=\{\partial\in\mathrm{K}_{*}(1)\mid\partial(\mathbb{C}[t,t^{-1},\xi])\subset{\bf m}\}

is the subalgebra of derivations
vanishing at the point
\bf m
. We now determine the
simple finite dimensional
\mathrm{K}_{*}(1)^{\bf(1)}
-modules.

Let

\mathcal{G}
be the Maurer-Cartan Lie algebra
defined in Section
5.5
and set

\mathcal{G}^{\prime}=\{g=\sum_{n}x_{n}a_{n}\mid\sum x_{n}=0\}.

Let
\psi:\mathcal{G}\to\mathrm{K}(1)
be the linear map
defined by
\psi(a_{n})=E_{n}
for any
n\in\mathbb{Z}
.

Lemma 6.5
.

(a)

An element

g=\sum_{n}x_{n}E_{n}+\sum_{m}y_{m}G_{m}

belongs to

\mathrm{K}_{*}(1)^{{\bf(1)}}
if and only if

\sum_{n}\,x_{n}=0\hbox{ and }\sum_{m}\,y_{m}=0.

(b)

The linear map
\psi
is not an algebra homomorphism, but its restriction

\psi:\mathcal{G}^{\prime}\to\mathrm{K}_{*}(1)^{\bf(1)}_{\overline{0}}

is a Lie algebra isomorphism.

(c)

The element
a_{0}
is central and

\mathcal{G}=\mathcal{G}^{\prime}\oplus\mathbb{C}a_{0}
as a Lie algebra.
Consequently

[\mathcal{G}^{\prime},\mathcal{G}^{\prime}]=[\mathcal{G},\mathcal{G}].

(d)

We have

[\mathrm{K}_{*}(1)^{\bf(1)}_{\overline{1}},\mathrm{K}_{*}(1)^{\bf(1)}_{\overline{1}}]\subset[\mathrm{K}_{*}(1)^{\bf(1)}_{\overline{0}},\mathrm{K}_{*}(1)^{\bf(1)}_{\overline{0}}].

Proof.

Assertion (a) follows from the explicit formulas of Section
4.4
.

By definition
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
is spanned by the vectors

a_{n}-a_{0}
and by Assertion (a)

\mathrm{K}_{*}(1)^{\bf(1)}_{\overline{0}}
is spanned by the vectors

E_{n}-E_{0}
. For any
n,m\in\mathbb{Z}
we have

[a_{n}-a_{0},a_{m}-a_{0}]=[a_{n},a_{m}]

=(n-m)a_{n+m}-na_{n}+ma_{m}

[E_{n}-E_{0},E_{m}-E_{0}]=[E_{n},E_{m}]+[E_{0},E_{n}]-E_{0},E_{m}]

=(n-m)E_{n+m}-nE_{n}+mE_{m}

which proves Assertion (b).

Since Assertion (c) is obvious, we skip its proof.

By Assertion (a)

\mathrm{K}_{*}(1)^{(1)}_{\overline{1}}
is spanned by the vectors

G_{i}-G_{j}
,
i,j\in\tau+\mathbb{Z}
. For any

i,j,k,l\in\tau+\mathbb{Z}
we have

[G_{i}-G_{j},G_{k}-G_{l}]=E_{i+k}+E_{j+l}-E_{i+l}-E_{j+k}.

By Lemma
5.9
(b) the element

[G_{i}-G_{j},G_{k}-G_{l}]
belongs to

\psi([\mathcal{G},\mathcal{G}])
. By Assertion (c)

[\mathcal{G},\mathcal{G}]=[\mathcal{G}^{\prime},\mathcal{G}^{\prime}]
hence

[G_{i}-G_{j},G_{k}-G_{l}]
belongs to

\psi([\mathcal{G}^{\prime},\mathcal{G}^{\prime}])=[\psi(\mathcal{G}^{\prime}),\psi(\mathcal{G}^{\prime})]=[\mathrm{K}_{*}(1)^{\bf(1)}_{\overline{0}},\mathrm{K}_{*}(1)^{\bf(1)}_{\overline{0}}]

which completes the proof of the Lemma.
∎

For
\delta\in\mathbb{C}
, let
S_{\delta}
be the one dimensional
\mathrm{K}_{*}(1)^{\bf(1)}
-module such that
the elements
E_{n}-E_{m}
act as
(m-n)\delta

and
\mathrm{K}_{*}(1)^{\bf(1)}_{\overline{1}}
acts trivially.

Lemma 6.6
.

Let
S
be a simple
finite dimensional
\mathrm{K}_{*}(1)^{\bf(1)}
-module. Then

S\simeq S_{\delta}

for some
\delta\in\mathbb{C}
.

Proof.

Let
\mathfrak{p}
be the image of
\mathrm{K}_{*}(1)^{\bf(1)}

in
\mathfrak{gl}(S)
.
By Lemma
6.5
(c),

\mathrm{K}_{*}(1)^{\bf(1)}_{\overline{0}}\oplus\mathbb{C}a_{0}\simeq\mathcal{G},

hence
\mathfrak{p}_{\overline{0}}
can be realized as a quotient of
\mathcal{G}
. Thus by Lemma
5.10
the
Lie algebra
\mathfrak{p}_{\overline{0}}
is solvable. By Kac classification of finite dimensional
simple superalgebras
[
22
]
, the
superalgebra
\mathfrak{p}
is solvable.

By Lemma
6.5
(d), we have

[\mathfrak{p}_{\overline{1}},\mathfrak{p}_{\overline{1}}]\subset[\mathfrak{p}_{\overline{0}},\mathfrak{p}_{\overline{0}}].

Thus by
[
22
]
any simple
\mathfrak{p}
-module

S
has dimension one.

In particular

\mathfrak{p}_{\overline{1}}
acts trivially on
S
and
the element
E_{1}-E_{0}
acts by some scalar
\delta
.
Thus it follows from Lemma
5.9
(b) that the elements
E_{n}-E_{m}
act as
(n-m)\delta
. Therefore

S\simeq S_{\delta}.

∎

Proof of Theorem
4

Proof.

Let
V=\oplus_{i\in(1/2)\mathbb{Z}}V_{i}
be an irreducible

\mathfrak{G}
-module of growth one.

We can assume that
(H\otimes\mathbb{C}[t,t^{-1},\xi])V\neq 0
.
By Lemma
6.2
we can assume that

H=\mathbb{C}h
has dimension one and that
h
acts as one.
By Lemma
6.4
, the algebra

R
generated by
h\otimes\mathbb{C}[t,t^{-1},\xi])
is isomorphic to
\mathbb{C}[t,t^{-1},\xi]
.

Since
V
is a free module
\mathbb{C}[t,t^{-1}]
-module of finite rank, we conclude that

S:=V/{\bf m}V
is finite dimensional. Moreover since

\xi^{2}=0
, the space
S
is not zero.

For
\delta\in\mathbb{C}
, we consider the
\mathrm{K}_{*}(1)^{\bf 1)}
-module

S_{\delta}
as a
\mathfrak{G}_{*}^{({\bf(1)})}
on which
each
h(t^{m})
acts as
1
.
Observe that
h(t^{m})
acts by
1
on
S
. Hence
by Lemma
6.6
, the

\mathfrak{G}_{*}^{({\bf(1)})}
-module
S
has a quotient isomorphic to

S_{\delta}
for some
\delta\in\mathbb{C}
. Up to
parity-change, we can assume that this quotient is even.

Thus there is an embedding

V\subset\mathrm{Coind}_{\mathfrak{G}_{*}^{\bf(1)}}^{\mathfrak{G}_{*}}\,S_{\delta}.

Let
u
be one eigenvalue of
-E_{0}
on

V_{\overline{0}}
. Since
V
is simple, the eigenvalues of
-E_{0}|_{V_{\overline{0}}}
lie in

u+\mathbb{Z}
and the eigenvalues of

-E_{0}|_{V_{\overline{1}}}
lie
u+\tau+\mathbb{Z}
.
Therefore

V\subset F_{u}\mathrm{Coind}_{\mathfrak{G}_{*}^{\bf(1)}}^{\mathfrak{G}_{*}}\,S_{\delta}.

Thus by Lemma
4.5
there is an embedding

V\subset{\mathrm{Tens}}(\lambda,\delta,u)

from which we deduce Theorem
4
.
∎

7
Projective cuspidal modules

Let
\mathrm{L}
be a superconformal algebra
and let

0\to\mathbb{C}c\to\hat{\mathrm{L}}\to\mathrm{L}\to 0

be a nonsplit central extension. By definition
a
\hat{\mathrm{L}}
-module
M
has
central charge

x
if

c|_{M}=x
. Also by a

projective

\mathrm{L}
-module we mean
an
\hat{\mathrm{L}}
-module with a non-trivial central charge, for some nonsplit central extension
\hat{\mathrm{L}}
of
\mathrm{L}
.

The nonsplit central extensions of
\mathrm{L}
are
classified by

\mathbb{P}H^{2}(\mathrm{L}):=(H^{2}(\mathrm{L})\setminus 0)/\mathbb{C}^{*}.

For
\mathrm{L}\neq\mathrm{K}(4)
,
V.G. Kac and J.W. van de Leur have shown that

\dim\,H^{2}(\mathrm{L})\leq 1,

thus
\mathrm{L}
admits at most one central extension. In the
3
-dimensional space

H^{2}(\mathrm{K}(4))
we select one specific cohomology
class
\psi\neq 0
and we define

\widehat{\mathrm{K}(4)}
as the corresponding central extension of
\mathrm{K}(4)
.

The result of the chapter is the following.

Theorem 5
.

Let
\mathrm{L}
be one of the known superconformal algebra. Then

(a)

A superconformal algebra
L\neq\mathrm{K}(4)

does not admits any cuspidal projective module.

(b)

If
\hat{L}
is a nonsplit central extension of
\mathrm{K}(4)

but
\hat{\mathrm{L}}\neq\widehat{\mathrm{K}(4)}
, the central charge of any
cuspidal
\hat{\mathrm{L}}
-module is zero.

(c)

The superconformal algebra
\widehat{\mathrm{K}(4)}

admits cuspidal modules with an arbitrary central charge.

The Theorem is an immediate consequence of Corollaries
7.3
and
7.6
proved in this chapter.

Among all nontrivial second cohomology classes of all superconformal algebras,
the class
[\psi]
is the only one which can be represented by a
2
-cocycle of finite rank.
This property plays a role in the proof.

A criterion for the existence of projective cuspidal modules

Let
\mathrm{L}
be a
\mathbb{Z}
-graded superconformal algebra and let

0\to\mathbb{C}c\to\hat{\mathrm{L}}\to\mathrm{L}\to 0

be a nonsplit central extension.

Given a subalgebra
\mathrm{L}^{\bf(1)}\subset\mathrm{L}
we write

\hat{\mathrm{L}}^{\bf(1)}
for the inverse image of
\mathrm{L}^{\bf(1)}
in

\hat{\mathrm{L}}
.
In the next lemma we do not assume that the subalgebra

\mathrm{L}^{\bf(1)}
is a
\mathbb{Z}
-graded subalgebra.

Lemma 7.1
.

Assume that
\mathrm{L}
admits a grading element

\ell_{0}
and that
\mathrm{L}
contains a subalgebra

\mathrm{L}^{\bf(1)}
of finite codimension such that

(a)

\mathrm{L}_{\overline{0}}=\mathrm{L}^{\bf(1)}_{\overline{0}}\oplus\mathbb{C}\ell_{0}

(b)

The central extension

\mathbb{C}c\to\hat{\mathrm{L}}^{\bf(1)}\to\mathrm{L}^{\bf(1)}

splits.

Then
\hat{\mathrm{L}}
admits
cuspidal modules of arbitrary central charge.

Proof.

Let
x
be an arbitrary scalar.
By hypothesis the algebra

\hat{\mathrm{L}}^{\bf(1)}
is isomorphic to

\mathrm{L}^{\bf(1)}\oplus\mathbb{C}c
. Let
S
the one-dimensional

\hat{\mathrm{L}}^{\bf(1)}
-module on which
\mathrm{L}^{\bf(1)}
acts
trivially and
c
acts by
1
.

Then the
\hat{\mathrm{L}}
-module
\mathcal{F}(S,0)
, which is defined
in Section
4.2
, has growth one and central charge

c
. Any simple subquotient is a cuspidal
\mathrm{L}
-module
of central charge
x
.
∎

The Lie superalgebra
\widehat{\mathrm{K}(4)}

We now describe a specific central extension

\widehat{\mathrm{K}(4)}
of
\mathrm{K}(4)
.

The four odd variables are denoted as

\zeta_{1},\eta_{1},\zeta_{2},\eta_{2}
with the convention that

[\zeta_{1},\eta_{1}]=[\zeta_{2},\eta_{2}]=1
and all the other
brackets are zero.
The Grassmann algebra

{\mathrm{Grass}}(4)=\mathbb{C}[\zeta_{1},\eta_{1},\zeta_{2},\eta_{2}]
has a natural grading

{\mathrm{Grass}}(4)=\oplus_{k=0}^{4}{\mathrm{Grass}}_{k}(4)

relative
to which each odd variable
\zeta_{i},\,\eta_{i}
has degree

1
. Set
\omega:=\zeta_{1}\eta_{1}\zeta_{2}\eta_{2}
and let
the trace map
{\mathrm{Tr}}:{\mathrm{Grass}}(4)\to\mathbb{C}
defined by

{\mathrm{Tr}}(\omega)=1\hbox{ and }{\mathrm{Tr}}(a)=0

for any homogenous element
a
of degree
\neq 4
.

The
2
-cocycle of interest

\psi:\mathrm{K}(4)\times\mathrm{K}(4)\to\mathbb{C}

is the case
d=0

e=1

in the formula (4.22) of
V.G. Kac and J.W. van de Leur paper
[
27
]
.
For simplicity we will describe some extension of the cocycle
\psi
to
\mathrm{K}(4;D)
.
Nevertheless we are only interested by its values on
\mathrm{K}(4)
.

Recall that

\mathrm{K}(4;D)=\mathbb{C}[t,t^{-1}]\otimes{\mathrm{Grass}}(4).

It will be convenient to denote the
elements
f(t)\otimes a\in\mathbb{C}[t,t^{-1}]\otimes{\mathrm{Grass}}(4)
as
a(f)
. For
a=1
it is also denoted as
f(t)
.

Let
a\in{\mathrm{Grass}}_{k}(4)
,
b\in{\mathrm{Grass}}_{l}(4)

and let
n,m
be integers. Then

\psi(a(t^{n}),b(t^{m}))=\frac{2-k}{2}\,\delta_{n+m,0}{\mathrm{Tr}}(ab)

(11)

More explicitly we have

(a)

\psi(a(t^{m}),b(t^{n}))=0

if
k=l
or if the pair
\{k,l\}
is not

\{0,4\}
or
\{1,3\}
.

(b)

\psi(a(t^{m}),b(t^{n}))=1/2\,\delta_{n+m,0}\,{\mathrm{Tr}}(ab)
if
k=1
and
l=3

(c)

\psi(t^{m},\omega(t^{n}))=\delta_{n+m,0}

We write
\widehat{\mathrm{K}(4)}
for the central extension
of
\mathrm{K}(4)
defined by the cocycle
\psi
and let
c\in\widehat{\mathrm{K}(4)}
be the corresponding central element.
We are now looking for convenient formulas for
the bracket of
\widehat{\mathrm{K}(4)}
.

For
k=0,1,\cdots,4
, set

\mathrm{K}_{k}(4;D)=\mathbb{C}[t,t^{-1}]\otimes{\mathrm{Grass}}_{k}(4)
.
Recall that the bracket in
\mathrm{K}(4;D)

of two arbitrary elements

a(f)\in\mathrm{K}_{k}(4;D)\hbox{ and }b(g)\in\mathrm{K}_{l}(4;D)

is given by the formula

[a(f),b(g)]=ab\left(\frac{2-k}{2}fDg-\frac{2-l}{2}gDf\right)+[a,b](fg)

which implies that

[\mathrm{K}_{k}(4;D),\mathrm{K}_{l}(4;D)]\subset\mathrm{K}_{k+l}(4;D)\oplus\mathrm{K}_{k+l-2}(4;D).

We now define an algebra denoted as

\widehat{\mathrm{K}(4)}
that we will identify with the previous definition. First we consider the
vector space

\widehat{{\mathrm{Grass}}}(4)=\oplus_{k=0}^{4}\,\widehat{{\mathrm{Grass}}_{k}}(4)

defined by

\widehat{{\mathrm{Grass}}_{4}(4)}=\mathbb{C}c\hbox{ and }\widehat{{\mathrm{Grass}}_{k}(4)}={\mathrm{Grass}}_{k}(4)\hbox{ for }k\neq 4.

As a vector space

\widehat{\mathrm{K}(4)}=\oplus_{k=0}^{4}\,\widehat{\mathrm{K}_{k}(4)}

where
\widehat{\mathrm{K}_{k}(4)}=\mathbb{C}[t,t^{-1}]\otimes\widehat{{\mathrm{Grass}}_{k}(4)}
.

As before an arbitrary element
f(t)\otimes a\in\mathbb{C}[t,t^{-1}]\otimes\widehat{{\mathrm{Grass}}}(4)
is denoted as
a(f)
. We define the bracket
[a(f),b(g)]

of two arbitrary elements

a(f)\in\widehat{\mathrm{K}_{k}(4)}\hbox{ and }b(g)\in\widehat{\mathrm{K}_{l}(4)}

by the following formulas:

(a)

If
k,l
and
k+l
are
\neq 4

[a(f),b(g)]=ab\left(\frac{2-k}{2}fDg-\frac{2-l}{2}gDf\right)+[a,b](fg).

(12)

It is the same as the formula in
\mathrm{K}(4)
and it is well defined since

[a(f),b(g)]
lies in
\oplus_{i=0}^{3}\mathrm{K}_{i}(4)=\oplus_{i=0}^{3}\widehat{\mathrm{K}_{i}(4)}
.

(b)

The formulas

[f,c(g)]=c(fDg)

(13)

[a(f),b(g)]={\mathrm{Tr}}(\frac{ab}{2})\,c(fg)+[a,b](fg)\hbox{ if }k=1\hbox{ and }l=3

(14)

[a(f),b(g)]=[a,b](fg)\hbox{ if }k=l=2

(15)

define the bracket when
k+l=4
.

(c)

Formula
13
and the formulas

[a(f),c(g)]=[a,\omega](fDg)\hbox{ if }k=1

(16)

[a(f),c(g)]=0\hbox{ if }k\geq 2

(17)

define the bracket when
l=4
.

These formulas together with the skew-symmetry

[a(f),b(g)]=-(-1)^{kl}[b(g),a(f)]

entirely define the bracket on
\widehat{\mathrm{K}(4)}
.

At this point we have neither check that the defined bracket on

\widehat{\mathrm{K}(4)}
satisfies the Jacobi identity
nor that
\widehat{\mathrm{K}(4)}
is the desired central
extension of
\mathrm{K}(4)
.

Let
\pi:\widehat{\mathrm{K}(4)}\to\mathrm{K}(4)

and
\sigma:\mathrm{K}(4)\to\widehat{\mathrm{K}(4)}

be the linear maps

\pi(a(f))=a(f)\hbox{ if }a(f)\in\widehat{\mathrm{K}_{k}(4)}\hbox{ for }k\neq 4

\pi(c(f))=\omega(Df)

\sigma(a(f))=a(f)\hbox{ if }a(f)\in K_{k}(4)\hbox{ for }k\neq 4

\sigma(\omega(t^{n}))=1/n\,c(t^{n}).

The last formula is well defined since

\omega(t^{n})
belongs to

\mathrm{K}(4)
only if
n\neq 0
.

It is clear that
\pi
is an algebra morphism.
It is easy to check that

[\sigma(x),\sigma(y)]=\sigma([x,y])+\psi(x,y)c

which shows that
\widehat{\mathrm{K}(4)}
is
the central extension of
\mathrm{K}(4)
corresponding with the cocycle
\psi
.

Moreover we observe that

[\widehat{\mathrm{K}_{k}(4)},\widehat{\mathrm{K}_{l}(4)}]\subset\widehat{\mathrm{K}_{k+l}(4)}\oplus\widehat{\mathrm{K}_{k+l-2}(4)}.

The subalgebra
\mathrm{K}(4)^{\bf(1)}\subset\mathrm{K}(4)

The set of quadratic elements

\mathcal{Q}=\{\zeta_{1}\zeta_{2},\eta_{1}\eta_{2},\zeta_{i}\eta_{j}\mid 1\leq i,j\leq 2\}

is a basis of the Lie subalgebra
\mathfrak{so}(4)
in
\mathrm{K}(4)
. Let
\mathrm{K}(4;D)^{\bf(1)}
be the ideal of
the commutative superalgebra

\mathbb{C}[t,t^{-1},\zeta_{1},\eta_{1},\zeta_{2},\eta_{2}]

generated by
(t-1)
and
\mathfrak{so}(4)
.
By Lemma
2.1
,
\mathrm{K}(4;D)^{\bf(1)}
is a Lie subalgebra of
\mathrm{K}(4;D)
. Set

\mathrm{K}(4)^{\bf(1)}=\mathrm{K}(4;D)^{\bf(1)}\cap\mathrm{K}(4),

and let
\widehat{\mathrm{K}(4)}^{\bf(1)}
be its inverse image in

\widehat{\mathrm{K}(4)}
.

Lemma 7.2
.

We have

\mathrm{K}(4)_{\overline{0}}=\mathrm{K}(4)^{\bf(1)}_{\overline{0}}\oplus\mathbb{C}E_{0}\hbox{ and }\dim\,K(4)_{\overline{1}}/\mathrm{K}(4)^{\bf(1)}_{\overline{1}}=4.

Moreover
the central extension

0\to\mathbb{C}c\to\widehat{\mathrm{K}(4)}^{\bf(1)}\to\mathrm{K}(4)^{\bf(1)}\to 0

splits.

Proof.

Set

I:=(t-1)\mathbb{C}[t,t^{-1}]=\{f\in\mathbb{C}[t,t^{-1}]\mid f(1)=0\}.

For
0\leq k\leq 4
set
\widehat{\mathrm{K}_{k}(4)}^{\bf(1)}=\widehat{\mathrm{K}(4)}^{\bf(1)}\cap\widehat{\mathrm{K}_{k}(4)}
.
It is easy to see that

\widehat{\mathrm{K}(4)}^{\bf(1)}=\oplus_{k=0}^{4}\,\widehat{\mathrm{K}_{k}(4)}^{\bf(1)},

where
\widehat{\mathrm{K}_{0}(4)}^{\bf(1)}=I
,

\widehat{\mathrm{K}_{k}(4)}^{\bf(1)}=\widehat{\mathrm{K}_{k}(4)}
for
k\geq 2
and

\widehat{\mathrm{K}_{1}(4)}^{\bf(1)}=I\otimes\zeta_{1}\oplus I\otimes\eta_{1}\oplus I\otimes\zeta_{2}\oplus I\otimes\eta_{2}.

It follows that

\widehat{\mathrm{K}(4)}_{\overline{0}}=\widehat{\mathrm{K}(4)}^{\bf(1)}_{\overline{0}}\oplus\mathbb{C}E_{0}\hbox{, and }

\widehat{\mathrm{K}(4)}_{\overline{1}}=\widehat{\mathrm{K}(4)}^{\bf(1)}_{\overline{1}}\oplus\Bigl(\mathbb{C}\zeta_{1}\oplus\mathbb{C}\eta_{1}\oplus\mathbb{C}\zeta_{2}\oplus\mathbb{C}\eta_{2}\Bigr)

which implies the first claim.

For
x,y\in\widehat{\mathrm{K}_{k}(4)}

the degree
4
component of

[x,y]
is denoted as
[x,y]_{top}
.
The previous formulas shows that

[\widehat{\mathrm{K}_{k}(4)},\widehat{\mathrm{K}_{l}(4)}]_{top}=0

except if
k\neq l
and
\{k,l\}=\{0,4\}
or

\{k,l\}=\{1,3\}
. We deduce that
the bracket
[-,-]_{top}
is the sum of two components

\mu_{0}:\widehat{\mathrm{K}_{0}(4)}\times\widehat{\mathrm{K}_{4}(4)}\to\widehat{\mathrm{K}_{4}(4)}\,\,

\mu_{1}:\widehat{\mathrm{K}_{1}(4)}\times\widehat{\mathrm{K}_{3}(4)}\to\widehat{\mathrm{K}_{4}(4)}

and their symmetric counterparts, where

\mu_{0}(f,c(g))=c(fDg)

\mu_{1}(a(f),b(g))={\mathrm{Tr}}(\frac{ab}{2})c(fg)

for any
a\in{\mathrm{Grass}}_{1}(4),b\in{\mathrm{Grass}}_{3}(4)
and
f,g\in\mathbb{C}[t,t^{-1}]
.

We observe that
\mu_{0}
and
\mu_{1}
are

\mathbb{C}[t,t^{-1}]
-linear on the first argument.
Hence we have

\left[\widehat{\mathrm{K}(4)}^{\bf(1)},\widehat{\mathrm{K}(4)}^{\bf(1)}\right]_{top}

\subset\mu_{0}\left(\widehat{\mathrm{K}_{0}(4)}^{\bf(1)},\widehat{\mathrm{K}_{4}(4)}^{\bf(1)}\right)+\mu_{1}\left(\widehat{\mathrm{K}_{1}(4)}^{\bf(1)},\widehat{\mathrm{K}_{3}(4)}^{\bf(1)}\right)

\subset\mu_{0}\left((1-t)\widehat{\mathrm{K}_{0}(4)},\widehat{\mathrm{K}_{4}(4)}\right)+\mu_{1}\left((1-t)\widehat{\mathrm{K}_{1}(4)},\widehat{\mathrm{K}_{3}(4)}\right)

\subset(1-t)\mathbb{C}[t,t^{-1}]\otimes c,

which implies that

c\notin\left[\widehat{\mathrm{K}(4)}^{\bf(1)},\widehat{\mathrm{K}(4)}^{\bf(1)}\right]
.
Hence the central extension

0\to\mathbb{C}c\to\widehat{\mathrm{K}(4)}^{\bf(1)}\to\mathrm{K}(4)^{\bf(1)}\to 0

splits.
∎

Corollary 7.3
.

There are
cuspidal
\widehat{\mathrm{K}(4)}
-modules
M
with a
nonzero central charge.

Proof.

By Lemma
7.2
the superalgebra

\widehat{\mathrm{K}(4)}
and its subalgebra

\mathrm{K}(4)^{\bf(1)}
satisfy the hypothesis of
Lemma

7.1
which insures the existence of
cuspidal modules of nonzero central charge.
∎

A Criterion for the vanishing of the central charge

Let
H=\mathbb{C}h
be a
1
-dimensional vector space
and let

\mathfrak{g}=\mathrm{Vir}\ltimes H\otimes\mathbb{C}[t,t^{-1}]

be the Lie considered in chapter
5
.
Let

\phi:\wedge^{2}\mathfrak{g}\to\mathbb{C}
be a
E_{0}
-invariant 2-cochain. Since
\phi(\mathfrak{g}_{n},\mathfrak{g}_{m})=0
whenever

n+m\neq 0
we only need to provide the value

\phi(x,y)
where
x
,
y
are basis elements of
opposite degree.

With this convention, let

\phi_{i}
,
i=1,2
or
3
, be the

E_{0}
-invariant 2-cochains defined by

\phi_{i}(h(t^{n}),h(t^{-n}))=\delta_{i,1}n

(18)

\phi_{i}(E_{n},h(t^{-n}))=\delta_{i,2}n^{2}

(19)

\phi_{i}(E_{n},E_{-n})=\delta_{i,3}n^{3}.

(20)

An easy computation shows that these cochains are cocycles. The classes of the cocycles
\phi_{i}
are denoted as
[\phi_{i}]
.

Lemma 7.4
.

The
3
classes

[\phi_{1}]
,
[\phi_{2}]
and
[\phi_{3}]
form a basis of
H^{2}(\mathfrak{g})
.

Proof.

Let
E_{0}^{*}
and
h^{*}
be the elements of
\mathfrak{g}^{*}
defined by

E^{*}_{0}(E_{0})=1,E^{*}_{0}(h)=0,h^{*}(E_{0})=0\text{ and }h^{*}(h)=1

while
E^{*}_{0}(\mathfrak{g}_{n})=h^{*}(\mathfrak{g}_{n})=0
if
n\neq 0
.

Since
\{E^{*}_{0},h^{*}\}
is a basis of
\mathfrak{g}_{0}^{*}
,
the set
\{\mathrm{d}E^{*}_{0},\mathrm{d}h^{*}\}
is a basis
of the space of
E_{0}
-equivariant coboundaries.
Furthermore the
5
cocycles
\phi_{1}
,
\phi_{2}
,
\phi_{3}
,
\mathrm{d}L_{1}
and
\mathrm{d}L_{2}
are linearly independent.
Since any
2
-cocycle of
\mathfrak{g}
is equivalent to an
E_{0}
-invariant cocycle, the classes

[\phi_{1}]
,
[\phi_{2}]
and
[\phi_{3}]
are linearly independent.

Next we claim that any
E_{0}
-equivariant

2
-cocycle
\phi
is equivalent to a linear combination of
[\phi_{1}]
,
[\phi_{2}]
and

[\phi_{3}]
. It is well known that

H^{2}(\mathrm{Vir})=\mathbb{C}[\phi_{3}].

Hence for some
a\in\mathbb{C}
,
\phi
is equivalent to

a\phi_{3}+\phi^{\prime}

where
\phi^{\prime}(\mathrm{Vir},\mathrm{Vir})=0
.

Set
a_{n}=\phi^{\prime}(E_{n},h(t^{-n}))
. The cocycle equation

\phi^{\prime}([E_{n},E_{m}],h(t^{-n-m}))=\phi^{\prime}(E_{n},[E_{m},h(t^{-n-m})])-\phi^{\prime}(E_{m},[E_{n},h(t^{-n-m})])

means that

(n-m)a_{n+m}=(n+m)(a_{n}-a_{m}).

It follows that
a_{n}=bn+cn^{2}
for some

b,c\in\mathbb{C}
.
Set

\phi"=\phi^{\prime}-b\,\mathrm{d}h^{*}-c\phi_{2}.

It follows from the previous consideration that

\phi"(\mathrm{Vir},\mathfrak{g})=0
, hence the bilinear map

B:\mathbb{C}[t,t^{-1}]\times\mathbb{C}[t,t^{-1}],\,\,(t^{n},t^{m}))\mapsto\phi"(h(t^{n}),h(t^{m}))

is
\mathrm{Vir}
-equivariant. Such a map is necessarily proportional to
\phi_{1}
, see e.g.

[
19
]
, which completes the proof of the lemma.
∎

Let
\mathrm{L}
be a superconformal algebra, let

\hat{\mathrm{L}}
be a
1
-dimensional central extension
and let
c\in\hat{\mathrm{L}}
be a central element.
Here we will use the convention that

\mathrm{L}
is
1/d\mathbb{Z}
-graded for some integer
d>0
and that the subalbalgebra
\mathrm{Vir}
is endowed with its usual
\mathbb{Z}
-grading.

Given a subalgebra
G\subset\mathrm{L}
we write

\hat{G}
for the inverse image of
G
in
\hat{\mathrm{L}}
.
Recall that
\mathfrak{g}=\mathrm{Vir}\ltimes H\otimes\mathbb{C}[t,t^{-1}]
.

Lemma 7.5
.

Let
G\subset\mathrm{L}
be a subalgebra isomorphic to
\mathrm{Vir}
or to
\mathfrak{g}
.

Assume that the central extension

\mathbb{C}c\to\hat{G}\to G

is not split. Then
c
acts trivially on any
cuspidal
\hat{\mathrm{L}}
-module.

Proof.

First assume that
G=\mathrm{Vir}
. By Corollary
3.1

c
acts trivially on any
simple subquotient. Therefore
c
is nilpotent of index
d:=\mathrm{Max}_{n}(\dim\,M_{n})
.
Since
\mathrm{Ker}c
is a nonzero submodule, it follows
that
M=\mathrm{Ker}\,c
, that is
c
acts trivially.

Next assume that

\mathrm{G}=\mathrm{Vir}\ltimes H\otimes\mathbb{C}[t,t^{-1}]
. By Lemma
7.4

we can assume that the cocycle of the central extension

\mathbb{C}c\to\hat{G}\to G

is
\phi=a_{1}\phi_{1}+a_{2}\phi_{2}+a_{3}\phi_{3}
for some
nonzero triple
(a_{1},a_{2},a_{3})\in\mathbb{C}^{3}
.

For
\delta\in\mathbb{C}
we consider the embedding

\tau_{\delta}:\mathrm{Vir}\to\mathrm{Vir}\ltimes H\otimes\mathbb{C}[t,t^{-1}]

defined by
\tau_{\delta}(E_{n})=E_{n}-n\delta\,h(t^{n})
.
Then we have

\phi(\tau_{\delta}(E_{n}),\tau_{\delta}(E_{-n}))=(a+2b\delta-c\delta^{2})n^{3}.

Thus for
a+2b\delta-c\delta^{2}\neq 0
the central extension

\mathbb{C}c\to\widehat{\tau_{\delta}(\mathrm{Vir})}\to\tau_{\delta}(\mathrm{Vir})

is not split. Thus by the previous assertion

c
acts trivially on any cuspidal

\hat{\mathrm{L}}
-module.
∎

The central charge of cuspidal projective
\mathrm{L}
-modules

In their paper
[
27
]
V.G. Kac and J.W. van de Leur have defined
2
-cocycles for various
superconformal algebras
\mathrm{L}
.

For
\mathrm{L}=\mathrm{K}(4)
, besides the cocycle
\psi
, they define two other
2
-cocycles
\psi_{1},\psi_{2}
. Indeed

\psi_{1}
and
\psi_{2}
satisfy

\psi_{1}(t^{n},t^{m})=n^{3}\delta_{n+m,0}

(21)

\psi_{2}(t^{n},t^{m})=0

(22)

\psi_{2}(a(t^{n}),b(t^{m}))=n\delta_{n+m,0}{\mathrm{Tr}}(ab)\hbox{ for }a,b\in{\mathrm{Grass}}_{2}(4)

(23)

where
{\mathrm{Tr}}:{\mathrm{Grass}}(4)\to\mathbb{C}
is the linear map
satisfying
{\mathrm{Tr}}({\mathrm{Grass}}_{k}(4))=0
if
k\neq 4
and

{\mathrm{Tr}}(\zeta_{1}\eta_{1}\zeta_{2}\eta_{2})=1
.

A complete definition of the cocycle
\psi_{1}
is is given by the formulas (4.21) of
[
27
]
.
The cocycle
\psi_{1}
in
[
27
]
satisfies

\psi_{1}(t^{n},t^{m})=(n^{3}-n)\delta_{n+m,0}
but it provides an equivalent cohomology class.
Similarly
the cocycle
\psi_{2}
is fully defined by
the formulas (4.22) of
[
27
]
for

d=1
and
e=0
. However for our purpose we do not need a complete description of these two cocycles.

For
\mathrm{L}=\mathrm{W}(2):=\mathrm{Der\,}\,\mathbb{C}[t,t^{-1},\xi_{1},\xi_{2}]
set

h=\xi_{1}\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi_{2}}-\xi_{2}\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi_{1}}.

There is a
2
-cocycle
\psi_{3}:\mathrm{L}\times\mathrm{L}\to\mathbb{C}
which satisfies

\psi_{3}(h(t^{n}),H(t^{m}))=2n\delta_{n+m,0}

(24)

For
\mathrm{L}=\mathrm{W}(1):=\mathrm{Der\,}\,\mathbb{C}[t,t^{-1},\xi]

set
h=\xi\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi}
.
There is a
2
-cocycle
\psi_{4}:\mathrm{L}\times\mathrm{L}\to\mathbb{C}
which satisfies

\psi_{4}(t^{n}\frac{\partial}{\partial t},h(t^{m}))=n^{2}\delta_{n+m,0}.

(25)

For complete formulas for
\psi_{3}
and
\psi_{4}
we refer to formulas (4.14) and (2.10) of
[
27
]
.
The following
result is due to V. G. Kac and J.W. van der Leur, see Theorem
4.1
[
27
]
, except Assertion (e) for the superalgebra
\mathrm{CK}(6)
which is
Theorem 4.1 of S.J. Cheng and V.G. Kac paper
[
11
]
.

Cheng-Kac-van de Leur Theorem
.

Let
\mathrm{L}
be a
superconformal algebra.

(a)

If
\mathrm{L}=\mathrm{K}(4)
or
\mathrm{K}_{NS}(4)
,
the cohomology classes

[\psi_{1}],[\psi_{1}]\hbox{ and }[\psi]

form a basis of
H^{2}(\mathrm{L})
.

(b)

Assume
\mathrm{L}=\mathrm{K}(N)
or
\mathrm{K}_{NS}(N)

with
1\leq N\leq 3
. Then

H^{2}(\mathrm{L})
is the
1
-dimensional space generated
by the class of
\psi_{1}|_{L}
.

(c)

Assume
\mathrm{L}=\mathrm{K}^{(2)}(2)
or

\mathrm{K}^{(2)}(4)
. Then

H^{2}(\mathrm{L})
is the
1
-dimensional space generated
by the class of
\psi_{1}|_{\mathrm{L}}
.

(d)

We have

H^{2}(\mathrm{W}(1))=\mathbb{C}[\psi_{4}]

H^{2}(\mathrm{W}(2))=\mathbb{C}[\psi_{3}].

(e)

If
\mathrm{L}=\mathrm{S}(2;\gamma)
then

H^{2}(\mathrm{L})
is the
1
-dimensional space generated
by the class of
\psi_{3}|_{\mathrm{L}}
.

(f)

If
\mathrm{L}
is one of the known
superalgebra but not from the previous list,
then
H^{2}(\mathrm{L})=0

Corollary 7.6
.

Let
\mathrm{L}
be one of the known superconformal algebra. Then

(a)

If
\hat{\mathrm{L}}
is a nonsplit central extension of
\mathrm{K}(4)

but
\hat{L}\neq\widehat{\mathrm{K}(4)}
, the central charge of any
cuspidal
\hat{\mathrm{L}}
-module is zero.

(b)

If
\mathrm{L}\neq\mathrm{K}(4)

then
\mathrm{L}
does not admit any cuspidal projective module.

Proof.

Let
\hat{\mathrm{L}}
be a nonsplit central extension of
\mathrm{L}
. For a subalgebra
G\subset\mathrm{L}

let
\hat{G}
be its inverse image in
\hat{\mathrm{L}}
.

The general idea is to find a subalgebra
G
isomorphic
to
\mathrm{Vir}
or to

\mathrm{Vir}\ltimes\mathbb{C}[t,t^{-1}]
such that
the central extension

0\to\hat{G}\to G\to 0

is not split. Then we use
Lemma
7.5
to conclude.

Let start with the case-by-case analysis.
First assume that
\hat{\mathrm{L}}
is a nonsplit central extension of
\mathrm{K}(4)
with

\hat{\mathrm{L}}\neq\widehat{\mathrm{K}(4)}
. By Theorem

Cheng-Kac-van de Leur Theorem
(a) this extension is represented by
the

2
-cocycle
\phi=a\psi_{1}+b\psi_{2}+c\psi
for some

a,b
and
c\in\mathbb{C}
with

(a,b)\neq(0,0)
.

If
a\neq 0
set
G=\mathrm{Vir}
. By
Formulas
11
,
21
and
22
we have

\phi(t^{n},t^{m})=an^{3}\delta_{n+m,0}

and therefore the extension

0\to\hat{G}\to G\to 0

is not split.

Otherwise we have
b\neq 0
.
Choose
h\in{\mathrm{Grass}}_{2}(4)
with

{\mathrm{Tr}}(h^{2})=1
and let

G=\mathrm{Vir}\ltimes h\otimes\mathbb{C}[t,t^{-1}].

By Formula
11
and
23

we have

\phi(h(t^{n}),h(t^{m}))=bn\delta_{n+m,0}.

Thus by Lemma
7.4

the extension

0\to\hat{G}\to G\to 0

is not split.

Thus we have completed the proof for
\mathrm{L}=\mathrm{K}(4)
. For

\mathrm{L}\neq\mathrm{K}(4)
we will find a subalgebra
G
isomorphic
to
\mathrm{Vir}
or
\mathrm{Vir}\ltimes\mathbb{C}[t,t^{-1}]
such that
the restriction map
H^{2}(\mathrm{L})\to H^{2}(G)
is injective.

First assume that
\mathrm{L}=\mathrm{K}(N)
or
\mathrm{K}_{NS}(N)
for

N\leq 3
. Since
H^{2}(\mathrm{L})
is generated by the class of
\psi_{1}|_{L}
. Since Formula
21
shows that
\psi_{1}|_{\mathrm{Vir}}
is the usual
Virasoro cocycle we deduce that

H^{2}(\mathrm{L})\simeq H^{2}(\mathrm{Vir})
.

The proof for
\mathrm{L}=\mathrm{K}^{(2)}(2)
or

\mathrm{L}=\mathrm{K}^{(2)}(4)
is identical. Indeed
Lemma
Cheng-Kac-van de Leur Theorem
(c) shows that

H^{2}(\mathrm{L})\simeq H^{2}(\mathrm{Vir})
.

Assume now that
\mathrm{L}=\mathrm{W}(2)
or
\mathrm{S}(2;\gamma)
and let

h=\xi_{1}\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi_{2}}-\xi_{2}\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi_{1}}

and set

G=\mathrm{Vir}\ltimes h\otimes\mathbb{C}[t,t^{-1}].

By Theorem
Cheng-Kac-van de Leur Theorem
the class of
\psi_{3}

generates
H^{2}(\mathrm{L})
and by formula

24
we have

\psi_{3}(h(t^{n}\otimes),h(t^{m}))=2n\delta_{n+m,0}

It follows that

[\psi_{3}|_{G}]=2[\phi_{1}]\hbox{ modulo }\mathbb{C}[\phi_{2}]+\mathbb{C}[\phi_{3}].

Thus the restriction map
H^{2}(\mathrm{L})\to H^{2}(G)
is injective.

For
\mathrm{L}=\mathrm{W}(1)
set
G=\mathrm{L}_{\overline{0}}
. By
Theorem
Cheng-Kac-van de Leur Theorem
the class of
\psi_{4}

generates
H^{2}(\mathrm{L})
and by formula

25
we have

[\psi_{4}|_{G}]=[\phi_{2}]\hbox{ modulo }\mathbb{C}[\phi_{1}]+\mathbb{C}[\phi_{3}],

which proves that the restriction map
H^{2}(L)\to H^{2}(G)
is injective.
∎

The exceptional cocycle of
\widehat{\mathrm{K}(4)}

The proof of Lemma
7.1
shows that

\widehat{\mathrm{K}(4)}
acts on

\mathbb{C}[t,t^{-1},\xi_{1},\xi_{2},\xi_{3},\xi_{4}]
by differential
operators of degree one. We will not use it, by
we will write the corresponding abelian cocycle.
All other known cocycles with values in current algebras
are differential operators of order
\leq 1
, e.g the divergence operator (some nonabelian cocycles are described in
[
43
]
).

By contrast, the exceptional cocycle of
\widehat{\mathrm{K}(4)}
is a differential operator of order
3
, as shown by the following formulas.

Set
\omega=\xi_{1}\xi_{2}\xi_{3}\xi_{4}
and let consider the following linear map

\mathcal{D}:\widehat{\mathrm{K}(4)}\to\mathbb{C}[t,t^{-1},\xi_{1},\cdots,\xi_{4}]

defined by:

\mathcal{D}(f)=2\omega(D^{3}(f)-D(f))

\mathcal{D}(f\xi_{i})=(-1)^{i}\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi_{i}}\omega(D^{2}(f)-f/4)

\mathcal{D}(f\xi_{i}\xi_{j})=(-1)^{i+j}2\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi_{i}}\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi_{j}}\omega(Df)

\mathcal{D}(f\xi_{i}x_{j}x_{k})=(-1)^{i+j}2\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi_{i}}\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi_{j}}\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi_{j}}\omega(f)

\mathcal{D}(c(f))=f,\text{ for all }f\in\mathbb{C}[t,t^{-1}].

The subalgebra
\mathfrak{k}
of
\widehat{\mathrm{K}(4)}

with basis

\{1,t^{\pm 1}\}\cup\{\xi_{i}(t^{\pm 1/2})\mid 1\leq i\leq 4\}\cup\{\xi_{i}\xi_{j}\mid 1\leq i<j\leq 4\}

is isomorphic to
\mathfrak{osp}(4,2)
.

Lemma 7.7
.

The linear map

\mathcal{D}:\widehat{\mathrm{K}(4)}\to\mathbb{C}[t,t^{-1},\xi_{1},\cdots,\xi_{4}]

is a
\mathfrak{k}
-invariant cocycle.
In particular
\mathcal{D}
is conformally invariant.

Proof.

This can be checked by direct computation.
∎

For
\partial\in\widehat{\mathrm{K}(4)}
write as

\overline{\partial}
its image in

\partial\in\mathrm{K}(4)
.

Corollary 7.8
.

The map

\partial\mapsto(\overline{\partial},\mathcal{D}(\partial))

realizes an embedding

\widehat{\mathrm{K}(4)}\subset\mathrm{K}(4)\ltimes\mathbb{C}[t,t^{-1},\xi_{1},\cdots,\xi_{4}].

Therefore
\widehat{\mathrm{K}(4)}
acts faithfully on

\mathbb{C}[t,t^{-1},\xi_{1},\cdots,\xi_{4}]
.

8
Highest weight theory

In the chapter we describe the general approach to classify all cuspidal modules for untwisted superconformal
algebras
\mathrm{L}
, that is for algebras
of the following list

\mathrm{W}(n),n\geq 2;\hskip 2.84526pt\mathrm{S}(n;\gamma),n\geq 2;\hskip 2.84526pt\mathrm{CK}(6);

\mathrm{K}(N)\hbox{ and }\mathrm{K}_{NS}(N),N\geq 3;\hskip 2.84526pt\widehat{\mathrm{K}(4)}.

We recall the general definitions and notations
of Chapter
2
. Let
H\subset\mathrm{L}_{0}
be the Cartan subalgebra and let
F\in H
be the chosen element. The eigenvalues of
\mathrm{ad}(F)
are integers. The corresponding eigenspace decomposition

L=\oplus_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathrm{L}^{(n)}

provides a triangular decomposition

\mathrm{L}=\mathrm{L}^{-}\oplus C_{\mathrm{L}}(F)\oplus\mathrm{L}^{+}.

where
\mathrm{L}^{\pm}=\oplus_{n>0}\,\mathrm{L}^{(\pm n)}
.
Except for
\mathrm{L}=\mathrm{W}(n)
, we have
C_{\mathrm{L}}(F)=C_{L}(H)
.
For
\mathrm{L}=\mathrm{W}(n)
,

C_{\mathrm{L}}(F)=C_{L}(H)\oplus L^{(\epsilon_{1}+\cdots+\epsilon_{n})}

but this brings only little differences.

In Chapter
2
, we have defined a nilpotent ideal
\mathrm{Rad}\,C_{\mathrm{L}}(F)
in
C_{\mathrm{L}}(F)
. (The ideal
is not a radical in some theoretical sense but it is a case-by-case definition.) We have

C_{\mathrm{L}}(F)=\mathrm{G}\ltimes\mathrm{Rad}\,C_{\mathrm{L}}(F)

where
\mathrm{G}
is isomorphic to
the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
of Chapter
5
or to the Lie superalgebra

\mathfrak{G}_{*}
of Chapter
6
.

Set
B:=C_{\mathrm{L}}(F)\ltimes\mathrm{L}^{+}
.
For
\delta,u\in\mathbb{C}
and
\lambda\in H
,
we consider the
\mathrm{G}
-module

{\mathrm{Tens}}(\lambda,\delta,u)
as a
B
-module, with a trivial
action of
\mathrm{Rad}\,C_{\mathrm{L}}(H)
and
\mathrm{L}^{+}
. The generalized Verma module

M(\lambda,\delta,u):=\mathrm{Ind}_{B}^{L}\,{\mathrm{Tens}}(\lambda,\delta,u)

has a unique simple quotient denoted as
V(\lambda,\delta,u)
.

In chapter
2
we have defined the simple coroots
h_{1},\cdots h_{m}\in H
.
A weight
\lambda\in H^{*}
is called
dominant
if
all
\lambda(h_{i})
are nonnegative integers.

The main result of the chapter is the following theorem.

Theorem 6
.

Let
V
be a cuspidal
\mathrm{L}
-module.
Then

V\simeq V(\lambda,\delta,u)

for some triple

(\lambda,\delta,u)\in H^{*}\times\mathbb{C}\times\mathbb{C}
.

Moreover
\lambda
is dominant and
\lambda(h_{1})\geq 1
.

However the
\mathrm{L}
-module
V(\lambda,\delta,u)
is not always cuspidal because its homogenous components could be infinite dimensional. Therefore Theorem
6
reduces the classification of all cuspidal
modules to the following question:

Besides the requirement that
\lambda
is dominant and
\lambda(h_{1})\geq 1
,

which condition insures that
V(\lambda,\delta,u)

is cuspidal?

Part II answers it
by a case-by-case analysis.

Existence of a highest weight

Let
\mathrm{L}
be a superconformal algebra and
let
V
be a cuspidal
\mathrm{L}
-module. Then
V

decomposes into weight spaces

V=\oplus_{\lambda\in H^{*}}\,V^{(\lambda)}.

As usual, an element
\lambda\in H^{*}
is called
a
weight of
V
if
V^{(\lambda)}\neq 0
.

Lemma 8.1
.

Any cuspidal

\mathrm{L}
-module
V
admits only finitely many weights.

Proof.

Let
\lambda\in H^{*}
be an arbitrary nonzero weight of
V
. Choose
h\in H
with
\lambda(h)=1
and
let
d
be the upper bound of the dimensions
\dim\,V_{i}
.

Let
\mathfrak{g}=\mathrm{Vir}\ltimes H\otimes\mathbb{C}[t,t^{-1}]
. We observe that
V^{(\lambda)}
is a
\mathfrak{g}
-module.
Hence by Corollary
5.13

h(t)|_{V^{(\lambda)}}

is invertible which implies that all dimensions

\dim\,V_{i}^{(\lambda)}
are nonzero.
It follows that
V
admits at most
d
nonzero weights,
which proves the assertion.
∎

Given weights
\lambda\neq\mu
we write

\lambda>\mu

if their difference can be written as

\lambda-\mu=\sum_{\alpha\in\Delta^{+}}\,m_{\alpha}\alpha\hbox{ and }\mu-\lambda=\sum_{\beta\in\Delta^{-}}\,n_{\beta}\beta

where all
m_{\alpha}
and
n_{\beta}
are nonnegative integers. When
\Delta^{-}\neq-\Delta^{+}
these two decompositions are not equivalent.

Lemma 8.2
.

Any cuspidal

\mathrm{L}
-module
V
admits a highest weight
\lambda
.
Moreover

(a)

V^{(\lambda)}
is a simple
C_{\mathrm{L}}(F)
-module,
and

(b)

the weight
\lambda
is dominant and nonzero.

Proof.

By definition of
F
,

\alpha(F)
is an integer for any root
\alpha\in\Delta
.
Hence for two weights
\lambda,\mu
of
V
,

\lambda(F)-\mu(F)
is an integer.
Since
V
admits only finitely many weights
by Lemma
8.1
, we can
choose a weight
\lambda
of
V
such that

\lambda(F)
is maximal.

When
C_{\mathrm{L}}(F)=C_{\mathrm{L}}(H)
,

V^{(\lambda)}
is a
C_{\mathrm{L}}(F)
-module
which is obviously simple. However for
\mathrm{L}=\mathrm{W}(n)

a proof is required.
Set
\omega=\epsilon_{1}+\cdots+\epsilon_{n}
and

V^{+}=\oplus_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}V^{(\lambda+m\omega)}.

Then
V^{+}
is a
C_{\mathrm{L}}(F)
-module
which is obviously simple. Since
\mathrm{L}^{(\omega)}
acts
nilpotently on
V
, we deduce that
\mathrm{L}^{(\omega)}
acts
trivially on
V^{+}
. It implies that

V^{+}=V^{(\lambda)},

which shows the claim for
\mathrm{W}(n)
.

Let
\mu
be another weight of
V
.
By
Poincaré-Birkhoff-Witt Theorem, we have

V=U(\mathrm{L}^{-})V^{(\lambda)}.

Thus

\mu-\lambda=\sum_{\beta\in\Delta^{-}}\,n_{\beta}\beta

for some nonnegative integers
n_{\beta}
.
If we consider the graded dual
V^{(*)}
of
V
, we have

V^{(*)}=U(\mathrm{L}^{+})(V^{(*)})^{(\lambda)},

which proves that

\lambda-\mu=\sum_{\alpha\in\Delta^{+}}\,m_{\alpha}\alpha

for some nonnegative integers
m_{\alpha}
.

Hence

\mu<\lambda
, that is
\lambda
is the highest weight
of
V
, which proves Assertion (a).

By the definition of Chapter
2
, each
h_{i}
is part
of a
\mathfrak{sl}(2)
-triple
(e_{i},h_{i},f_{i})

lying in
\mathrm{L}_{0}
with
e_{i}\in\mathrm{L}^{+}
. Since each
homogenous component
V_{n}
is a
finite dimensional module over

\mathfrak{sl}(2)=\mathbb{C}e_{i}\oplus\mathbb{C}h_{i}\oplus\mathbb{C}f_{i}

and
e_{i}V^{(\lambda)}=0
we deduce
\lambda
is dominant.

It is easy to show that
\mathrm{L}
is generated by

\mathrm{L}^{+}
and the elements
f_{i}
. Since

\mathrm{L}.V^{(\lambda)}\neq 0
, it follows that

f_{i}.V^{(\lambda)}\neq 0
for some
f_{i}
. We similarly deduce that
\lambda(h_{i})\neq 0
for some
i
, which proves that
\lambda\neq 0
.
∎

The subalgebra
C_{\mathrm{L}}(F)
for the superalgebras
\mathrm{K}_{*}(N)

The proof Theorem
8.2
requires
an explicit description of the Lie subalgebra

\mathfrak{C}:=C_{\mathrm{L}}(F)
and its radical
\mathrm{Rad}\,C_{\mathrm{L}}(F)
for
contact superalgebras
\mathrm{L}=\mathrm{K}_{*}(N)
. In this case we have
C_{\mathrm{L}}(F)=C_{\mathrm{L}}(H)
.

We
indistinctly denote as

\mathrm{K}_{*}(N)
a contact superalgebra of
Ramond type or Neveu-Schwarz type.
Indeed if
\mathrm{L}:=\mathrm{L}_{\overline{0}}\oplus\mathrm{L}_{\overline{1}}
denotes the superalgebra
\mathrm{K}(N)
,
we identify the
Neveu-Schwarz superalgebra
\mathrm{K}_{NS}(N)
as

\mathrm{L}_{\overline{0}}\oplus t^{1/2}\mathrm{L}_{\overline{1}}
,
see Section
2.2
.

We now fix an integer
N\geq 3
with
N=2m
or
N=2m+1
and let
\mathrm{L}:=\mathrm{K}_{*}(N)
. As in
Section
2.5
,
for
N=2m
we denote the odd variables as

\zeta_{1},\eta_{1},\cdots\zeta_{m},\eta_{m}
and for
N=2m+1
the additional variable is denoted as
\xi
.

First we describe the even part
\mathfrak{C}_{\overline{0}}
of

\mathfrak{C}:=C_{\mathrm{L}}(H)
.
For a subset
I=\{i_{1},\cdots,i_{r}\}
of
\{1,\cdots,m\}

it will be convenient to set

\epsilon_{I}=\zeta_{i_{1}}\eta_{i_{1}}\cdots\zeta_{i_{r}}\eta_{i_{r}}.

Assume that
N\neq 4
. As a vector space

\mathfrak{C}_{\overline{0}}=\oplus_{I}\,\mathbb{C}[t,t^{-1}]\epsilon_{I},

where
I
runs over the power set
2^{\{1,\cdots,m\}}
.

The formula of Lie bracket of
\mathfrak{C}_{\overline{0}}
is

[f(t)\epsilon_{I},g(t)\epsilon_{J}]=\Bigl(1-|I|)f(t)Dg(t)-(1-|J|)g(t)Df(t)\Bigr)(\epsilon_{I}.\epsilon_{J})

for any
f(t),g(t)\in\mathbb{C}[t,t^{-1}]
,

I,J\in 2^{\{1,\cdots,m\}}
. Here

D=\frac{t\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}
, the symbol
|I|
is the cardinality of the set
I
and

\epsilon_{I}.\epsilon_{J}
is the Grassman product,
namely

\epsilon_{I}.\epsilon_{J}=\epsilon_{I\cup J}\hbox{ if }I\cap J=\emptyset\hbox{ and }\epsilon_{I}.\epsilon_{J}=0\hbox{ otherwise }.

The previous formula shows that the Lie algebra

\mathfrak{C}_{\overline{0}}
admits a grading

\mathfrak{C}_{\overline{0}}=\mathfrak{C}_{{\overline{0}},0}\oplus\mathfrak{C}_{{\overline{0}},1}\oplus\cdots\mathfrak{C}_{{\overline{0}},n}

relative to which the elements
f(t)\epsilon_{I}
has degree
|I|
.

For the special case
N=4
, there is a similar description except
that
\zeta_{1}\eta_{1}\zeta_{2}\eta_{2}
is not in

\mathrm{K}(4)
. Thus
\mathfrak{C}_{2}
consists of elements

f(t)\zeta_{1}\eta_{1}\zeta_{2}\eta_{2}
where
f(t)

is a Laurent polynomial without constant term.

When
N=2m
the algebra
\mathfrak{C}
is purely even, namely

\mathfrak{C}_{\overline{1}}=0
. We now describe
\mathfrak{C}_{\overline{1}}
for
N=2m+1
. Let
\tau=0
if

\mathrm{L}=\mathrm{K}(N)
and
\tau=1/2
otherwise.

As a vector space

\mathfrak{C}_{\overline{1}}=\oplus_{I}\,t^{\tau}\mathbb{C}[t,t^{-1}]\epsilon_{I}\xi,

where
I
runs over
2^{\{1,\cdots,n\}}
.
The formula of the additional brackets of
\mathfrak{C}
are

[f(t)\epsilon_{I},a(t)\epsilon_{I}\xi]=\bigl((1-|I|)f(t)Da(t)-(\frac{1}{2}-|J|)a(t)Df(t)\Bigr)(e_{I}.e_{J}\xi)\hbox{ and }

[a(t)\epsilon_{I}\xi,b(t)\epsilon_{I}\xi]=a(t)b(t)(e_{I}.e_{J})

for any
f(t)\in\mathbb{C}[t,t^{-1}]
,

a(t),b(t)\in t^{\tau}\mathbb{C}[t,t^{-1}]
and

I,J\in 2^{\{1,\cdots,n\}}
.

These formulas shows that the superalgebra

\mathfrak{C}
admits a grading

\mathfrak{C}=\mathfrak{C}_{0}\oplus\mathfrak{C}_{1}\oplus\cdots\mathfrak{C}_{n}

relative to which the elements
f(t)\epsilon_{I}

and
a(t)\epsilon_{I}\xi
have degree
|I|
.

We now return to the general case
N=2m
or
2m+1
.
By definition,
\mathfrak{R}:=\mathrm{Rad}\,C_{\mathrm{L}}(H)
is precisely the subalgebra

\mathfrak{R}=\mathfrak{C}_{2}\oplus\cdots\oplus\mathfrak{C}_{n}=\bigoplus_{k=2}^{m}\mathfrak{C}_{k}.

Obviously
\mathfrak{R}
is a nilpotent ideal of
\mathfrak{C}
and

\mathfrak{R}_{\overline{0}}
is a nilpotent ideal of

\mathfrak{C}_{\overline{0}}
.

Lemma 8.3
.

Assume
N\neq 4
.
As a
\mathfrak{C}_{\overline{0}}
-module the ideal

\mathfrak{R}_{\overline{0}}
is generated by the set

\Sigma:=\{\epsilon_{I}\mid I\in 2^{\{1,\cdots,n\}}\hbox{ and }|I|=2\}.

Proof.

The lemma is a consequence of the following claims

(a)

As a
\mathrm{Vir}
-module,

\mathfrak{C}_{{\overline{0}},2}
is generated by
\Sigma
.

(b)

For any
k\geq 2

[\mathfrak{C}_{{\overline{0}},1},\mathfrak{C}_{{\overline{0}},k}]=\mathfrak{C}_{{\overline{0}},k+1}.

For any
\epsilon_{I}\in\Sigma
we have

[E_{n},\epsilon_{I}]=-nt^{n}\epsilon_{I}

hence

\mathbb{C}[t,t^{-1}]\epsilon_{I}=\mathbb{C}\epsilon_{I}\oplus[\mathrm{Vir},\epsilon_{I}]

which proves Claim (a).

Let
t^{n}\epsilon_{I}
be an arbitrary
basis element of
\mathfrak{C}_{\overline{0},k}
for some
subset
I
of cardinality
k\geq 3
.
Choose
i\in I
and set
J=I\setminus\{i\}
.
Since

[\epsilon_{J},t^{n}\epsilon_{\{i\}}]=n(k-2)t^{n}\epsilon_{I}

the element
t^{n}\epsilon_{I}
belongs to

[\mathfrak{C}_{{\overline{0}},1},\mathfrak{C}_{{\overline{0}},k-1}]
if
n\neq 0
.
Moreover the formula

[t^{-1}\epsilon_{J},t\epsilon_{\{i\}}]=(k-2)\epsilon_{I}

shows that
\epsilon_{I}
also belongs to

[\mathfrak{C}_{{\overline{0}},1},\mathfrak{C}_{{\overline{0}},k-1}]

which completes the proof of Assertion (b).
∎

Some nilpotency criteria

Lemma 8.4
.

Let
R
be an associative algebra
and let
a,b\in R
. Assume

a^{n}=0\hbox{ and }[a,[a,b]]=0.

Then

[a,b]^{2n-1}=0

Proof.

For any
r\in R
, we have

\mathrm{ad}^{2n-1}(a)(r)=\sum_{k=0}^{2n1}(-1)^{k}\binom{2n-1}{k}a^{k}ra^{2n-1-k}=0.

Therefore

0=\mathrm{ad}^{2n-1}(a)(b^{n-1})=(2n-1)![a,b]^{n}

which proves the lemma.
∎

In the following two lemmas we do not assume that
the Lie algebras or their modules are finite dimensional.

Lemma 8.5
.

Let
\mathrm{G}
be a Lie algebra,
let
\mathrm{R}
be a nilpotent ideal and let

M
be a
\mathrm{G}
-module. Set

{\mathcal{N}}:=\{x\in\mathrm{R}\mid x|_{M}\hbox{ is nilpotent }\}.

(a)

The set
{\mathcal{N}}
is a vector space.

(b)

The vector space
{\mathcal{N}}
is an ideal
of
\mathrm{G}
.

Proof of Assertion (a).
For integers
d,n\geq 1

let
\mathfrak{u}
be the free nilpotent Lie algebra of index
d

over two generators
a
and
b
and set

S(d,n):=U(\mathfrak{u})/I

where
I
is the two sided-ideal generated by
a^{n}
and
b^{n}
.

Let
J\subset S\mathfrak{u}
be the graded ideal associated with
I
and let
\sqrt{J}
be its radical. Since
\mathfrak{u}
is finite dimensional,
Gabber’s involutivity Theorem

[
15
]
states that
\sqrt{J}
is stable by the Poisson bracket of
S\mathfrak{u}
. By definition,

a
and
