b
belong to
\sqrt{J}
and generate
\mathfrak{u}
. We conclude that

\sqrt{J}=\mathfrak{u}S\mathfrak{u}.

Therefore
J
and

I
have finite codimension, that is the algebra

S(d,n)
is finite dimensional.

The algebra
S(d,n)
has a grading

S(d,n)=\oplus_{k\geq 0}S(d,n)_{k}

relative to which
a
and
b
are homogenous of degree
one. It follows that

(a+b)^{N(d,n)}\equiv 0\hskip 2.84526pt\,\mathrm{mod}\,I

where
N(d,n)
is the smallest integer
N

such that
S(d,n)_{N}=0
.

Now let
d
be the nilpotency index of the Lie algebra

\mathrm{R}
and
x,y\in\mathcal{N}
be nilpotent elements of index
n
.
The associative subalgebra
S\subset\mathrm{End}(M)
generated
by
x
and
y
is a quotient of
S(n,d)
. Therefore

x+y
is nilpotent of index
N(d,n)
which completes the proof of Assertion (a). ∎

Proof of Assertion (b).
Let
t
be a formal variable. For an arbitrary vector space
E

let
E[[t]]
be the space of formal series

\sum_{k\geq 0}t^{k}e_{k}

where
e_{k}\in E
.

Let
\partial
be an arbitrary element in
\mathrm{G}
.
Then

\exp t\,\partial:=\sum_{k\geq 0}\frac{t^{k}}{k!}\partial^{k}\hbox{ and }\exp t\,\mathrm{ad}(\partial):=\sum_{k\geq 0}\frac{t^{k}}{k!}\,\mathrm{ad}(\partial)^{k}

are well defined operators on
M[[t]]
and

\mathrm{R}[[t]]
. Observe that for any
x\in\mathcal{N}

\exp(t\,\mathrm{ad}\partial)(x)

is nilpotent of same index as
x
and

\mathrm{R}[[t]]
is a nilpotent Lie algebra of same index as
\mathrm{R}
.

Thus by the previous proof

\left(\exp(t\,\mathrm{ad}\partial)(x)-x\right)^{N}=0

for some
N>0
. Since

\left(\exp(t\,\mathrm{ad}\partial\right)(x)-x)^{N}=t^{N}\,[\partial,x]^{N}+o(T^{N})

we have proved that
[\partial,x]^{N}=0
that is

[\partial,x]
belongs to
\mathcal{N}
. Therefore
\mathcal{N}
is an ideal. ∎

Lemma 8.6
.

Let
M=M_{\overline{0}}\oplus M_{\overline{1}}
be a free
\mathbb{C}[t,t^{-1}]
-module of finite
rank and let
\mathfrak{u}\subset\mathrm{End}_{\mathbb{C}[t,t^{-1}]}(M)
be a nilpotent Lie superalgebra. Assume that any
x\in\mathfrak{u}_{\overline{0}}

acts nipotently on
M
.

Then
\mathfrak{u}
acts nilpotently on
M
.

Proof.

Let

\mathfrak{U}\subset\mathrm{End}_{\mathbb{C}(t)}(M\otimes\mathbb{C}(t))
be the

\mathbb{C}(t)
-vector space generated by
\mathfrak{u}
. So

\mathfrak{U}
is a nilpotent Lie superalgebra acting
on the finite dimensional
\mathbb{C}(t)
-vector space

M\otimes\mathbb{C}(t)
. By Engel’s Theorem

\mathfrak{U}_{\overline{0}}
acts nilpotently on

M\otimes\mathbb{C}(t)
. It follows that
\mathfrak{U}
acts nilpotently
which proves the lemma.
∎

Proof that
\mathrm{Rad}\,C_{\mathrm{L}}(F)
acts trivially on the highest weight component

Let
\mathrm{L}
be a superconformal algebra and
let
V
be a cuspidal

\mathrm{L}
-module with highest weight
\lambda
.
In this section we prove that

\mathrm{Rad}\,C_{\mathrm{L}}(F)
acts trivially on
V^{(\lambda)}
.
Indeed the statement is tautological when

\mathrm{Rad}\,C_{\mathrm{L}}(F)=0
and it has been proved for

\mathrm{L}=\mathrm{W}(n)
.

Thus we consider the case
\mathrm{L}:=\mathrm{K}_{*}(N)
, with
N\geq 4
.

Lemma 8.7
.

Assume
N\geq 5
. Then

(\mathrm{Rad}\,C_{\mathrm{L}}(H))V^{(\lambda)}=0.

Proof.

We first prove that each

\epsilon_{I}\in\Sigma
acts nilpotently on
V^{(\lambda)}
. To simplify we will prove
it for
I=\{1,2\}
, the general proof being
the same up to a permutation of the indices.

If
N=5
, set

a=\zeta_{1}\eta_{1}\zeta_{2}\xi\hbox{ and }b=\eta_{2}\xi

and for
N\geq 6
set

a=\zeta_{1}\eta_{1}\zeta_{2}\eta_{3}\hbox{ and }b=\eta_{2}\zeta_{3}.

In both cases,
a
is a root vector.
By Proposition
8.1

V
admits
only finitely many weights thus the operator
a
acts nilpotently on
V
. Moreover

[a,b]=\zeta_{1}\eta_{1}\zeta_{2}\eta_{2}=\epsilon_{I}.

Futhermore
a
and
\epsilon_{I}
are divisible by

\zeta_{1}\eta_{1}
hence

0=[a,\epsilon_{I}]=[a[a,b]].

So by Lemma
8.4
the operator

\epsilon_{I}
acts nilpotently on
V^{(\lambda)}
,
which proves the claim.

Set
\mathfrak{C}:=C_{\mathrm{L}}(H)
and
\mathfrak{R}:=\mathrm{Rad}\,C_{\mathrm{L}}(H)
.
Let us prove that any element
x\in\mathfrak{R}_{\overline{0}}
acts nilpotently
on
V^{(\lambda)}
.
Observe that
\mathfrak{R}_{\overline{0}}

is a nilpotent ideal in
\mathfrak{C}_{\overline{0}}
. Thus
by Lemma
8.5
the set

{\mathcal{N}}:=\{x\in\mathrm{Rad}\,C_{\mathrm{L}}(H)_{\overline{0}}\mid x|_{V^{(\lambda)}}\hbox{ is nilpotent}\}

is also an ideal of
\mathfrak{C}_{\overline{0}}
.
By Lemma
8.3
, the set
\Sigma
generates the ideal
\mathfrak{R}_{\overline{0}}
and we have proved that

\Sigma
lies in
{\mathcal{N}}
. We conclude

\mathfrak{R}_{\overline{0}}={\mathcal{N}}.

We finally prove that
\mathfrak{R}
acts trivially.
Assume otherwise. Recall that
\mathfrak{R}
admits a grading

\mathfrak{R}=\mathfrak{R}_{2}\oplus\cdots\mathfrak{R}_{m}.

Let
k\geq 2
be the largest integer such that

\mathfrak{R}_{k}V^{(\lambda)}\neq 0
and let

\mathfrak{u}\subset\mathrm{End}(V^{(\lambda)})
be the homorphic image of the superalgebra

\oplus_{l\geq k}\mathfrak{R}_{l}.

By Lemma
8.2
(b), the weight

\lambda
is nonzero.
Thus choose
h\in H
with
\lambda(h)=1
.
By Corollary
5.13
the operator
h(t)
is invertible. Since

[h(t),\mathfrak{R}_{k}]\subset\mathfrak{R}_{k+1}

all operators in
\mathfrak{u}
commutes with
h(t)
, that is

\mathfrak{u}\subset\mathrm{End}_{\mathbb{C}[h(t),h(t)^{-1}]}(V^{(\lambda)}).

We have proved that
\mathfrak{u}_{\overline{0}}
consists of nilpotent elements. So by Lemma
8.6
the superalgebra
\mathfrak{u}
acts nilpotently.
Since
\mathrm{Ker}\,\mathfrak{u}
is a nontrivial submodule
we have
\mathrm{Ker}\,\mathfrak{u}=V^{(\lambda)}
which contradicts the hypothesis. This completes the proof.
∎

Lemma 8.8
.

Let
\mathrm{L}=\mathrm{K}(4)
. Then

(\mathrm{Rad}\,C_{\mathrm{L}}(H))V^{(\lambda)}=0.

For
N=4
the proof is different because
the element
\zeta_{1}\eta_{1}\zeta_{2}\eta_{2}
is not in the derived algebra.

Proof.

For
\mathrm{L}=K(4)
the algebra

\mathrm{Rad}\,C_{\mathrm{L}}(H)
is the commutative Lie algebra

\mathfrak{R}=\mathfrak{C}_{2}
with basis

t^{n}\zeta_{1}\eta_{1}\zeta_{2}\eta_{2}=t^{n}\epsilon_{{\{1,2}\}}\hskip 2.84526pt\hbox{ for }n\in\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{0\}.

Set

\mathfrak{k}:=\mathrm{Vir}\ltimes\mathfrak{C}_{2}
and let

\mathfrak{g}:\mathrm{Vir}\ltimes\mathbb{C}[t,t^{-1}]

be the Lie algebra considered in chapter 5.

Since
\mathfrak{C}_{2}
is isomorphic to

\Omega^{1}_{0}
as a
\mathrm{Vir}
-module,
the linear map

\psi:(\partial,f(t))\in\mathfrak{g}\mapsto(\partial,Df(t)\epsilon_{{\{1,2}\}})\in\mathfrak{k}

is a Lie algebra homorphism with kernel
\mathbb{C}
.
Hence
V^{(\lambda)}
is a
\mathfrak{g}
-module on which the element

1
acts trivially. By Corollary
5.13

\mathfrak{C}_{2}
acts nilpotently on
V^{(\lambda)}
.
Since
\mathrm{Ker}\,\mathfrak{C}_{2}
is a nontrivial
\mathfrak{C}
-submodule
we have
\mathrm{Ker}\,\mathfrak{C}_{2}=V^{(\lambda)}
, that is
we have proved

(\mathrm{Rad}\,C_{\mathrm{L}}(H))V^{(\lambda)}=0.

∎

The following statement explain why we view
the ideal

\mathrm{Rad}(C_{\mathrm{L}}(F))\subset C_{\mathrm{L}}(F)

as a radical.

Corollary 8.9
.

Let
\mathrm{L}
be a superconformal algebra
of the following list:

\mathrm{W}(n),n\geq 2;\hskip 2.84526pt\mathrm{S}(n;\gamma),n\geq 2;\hskip 2.84526pt\mathrm{CK}(6);\mathrm{K}_{*}(N),N\geq 3;\hskip 2.84526pt\widehat{\mathrm{K}(4)}.

Let
V
be a cuspidal
\mathrm{L}
-module with highest weight

\lambda
.
Then

\mathrm{Rad}(C_{\mathrm{L}}(F)).V^{(\lambda)}=0.

Proof.

For
\mathrm{L}=\mathrm{W}(n)
we have

\mathrm{Rad}(C_{\mathrm{L}}(F))=\mathrm{L}^{(\epsilon_{1}+\cdots\epsilon_{n})}
.
By Lemma
8.2
,

\mathrm{L}^{(\epsilon_{1}+\cdots\epsilon_{n})}
acts
trivially on
V^{(\lambda)}
, which prove the claim for that case.

For
\mathrm{L}=\mathrm{K}_{*}(N)
,
N\geq 4
, the claim results from
Lemmas
8.7
and
8.8
.

For other superconformal algebras
\mathrm{L}
, the
radical
\mathrm{Rad}(C_{\mathrm{L}}(F))
is trivial and the claim
is tautological.
∎

Proof of Theorem
8.2

Lemma 8.10
.

The subalgebra
\mathfrak{m}
generated by

\mathrm{L}^{+}
and
f_{1}
contains the Virasoro element

E_{0}
.

Proof.

The proof is based on a case-by-case analysis.

First assume
\mathrm{L}=\mathrm{W}(n)
. Then

f_{1}=\xi_{1}\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi_{2}},

and
\xi_{1}D
and
\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi_{2}}

belong to
\mathrm{L}^{+}
. Since

[f_{1},\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi_{2}}]=\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi_{1}}\hbox{ and }[\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi_{1}},\xi_{1}D]=D

the algebra
\mathfrak{m}
contain
D=-E_{0}
.

For
\mathrm{L}=\mathrm{S}(n;\gamma)
we have

f_{1}=\xi_{1}\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi_{2}},

and
\xi_{1}(D+\gamma\xi_{2}\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi_{2}})
and
\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi_{2}}
belong to
\mathrm{L}^{+}
.
Since

[f_{1},\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi_{2}}]=\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi_{1}}\hbox{ and }[\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi_{1}},\xi_{1}(D+\gamma\xi_{2}\frac{\partial\xi_{2}}{)}]=D+\gamma\xi_{2}\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi_{2}}

the algebra
\mathfrak{m}
contains

D+\gamma\xi_{2}\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi_{2}}=-E_{0}
.

For
\mathrm{L}=\mathrm{K}_{*}(2m+1)
, we have

f_{1}=\xi\eta_{1}

and
\zeta_{1}
belongs to
\mathrm{L}^{+}
. Since

[[f_{1}[f_{1},\zeta_{1}]=2\eta_{1}\hbox{ and }[\eta_{1},\xi_{1}]=D,

the algebra
\mathfrak{m}
contains
D=-E_{0}
.

For
\mathrm{L}=\mathrm{K}(2m)
, we have

f_{1}=\eta_{1}\eta_{2}

and
\zeta_{1}
and
\zeta_{2}
belongs to

\mathrm{L}^{+}
. Since

[f_{1},\zeta_{2}]=\eta_{1}\hbox{ and }[\eta_{1},\zeta_{1}]=D

the algebra
\mathfrak{m}
contains
D=-E_{0}
.

For
\mathrm{L}=CK(6)
, set
s_{1}=x_{1}\wedge x_{4}
and

s_{2}=x_{1}\wedge x_{3}
. For
i=1
or
2
, consider

X_{i}:=\begin{pmatrix}0&s_{1}\\
\phi(s_{i})D&0\end{pmatrix}

The matrix
S_{1}
and
S_{2}
belong to
\mathrm{L}^{+}
and
a computation in
\hat{P(3)}
shows that

[[f_{1},X_{2}],X_{1}]=D.

Thus
\mathfrak{m}
contains
E_{0}=-D
, which completes the proof.
∎

We now turn to the proof of the main result.

Proof of Theorem
8.2
.

Let
\mathrm{L}
be an untwisted superconformal algebra and let
V
be a cuspidal module. Recall that

C_{\mathrm{L}}(F)=\mathrm{G}\ltimes\mathrm{Rad}_{\mathrm{L}}(F)

where
G
is the Lie algebra or superalgebra

\mathfrak{g}=\mathrm{Vir}\ltimes H\otimes\mathbb{C}[t,t^{-1}]\hbox{ or }\mathfrak{G}_{*}=\mathrm{K}_{*}(1)\ltimes H\otimes\mathbb{C}[t,t^{-1},\xi].

By Lemma
8.2
,
V
admits
a highest weight
\lambda\neq 0
.
By Corollary
8.9
, the ideal

\mathrm{Rad}(C_{\mathrm{L}}(F))
acts trivially on
V^{(\lambda)}
.
Thus by Theorems
3
and
4
,
the simple
C_{\mathrm{L}}(F)
-module
V^{(\lambda)}
is
isomorphic to
{\mathrm{Tens}}(\lambda,\delta,u)
for some

\delta,u\in\mathbb{C}
. Therefore

V\simeq V(\lambda,\delta,u).

Let
\mathfrak{m}\subset\mathrm{L}
be the subalgebra generated by

\mathrm{L}^{+}
and
f_{1}
. Since
E_{0}.V^{(\lambda)}\neq 0
,
it follows from Lemma
8.10
that

f_{1}.V^{(\lambda)}\neq 0
.
However

e_{1}.V^{(\lambda)}=0

and
V
admits only finitely many weights. Hence by the elementary
\mathfrak{sl}(2)
-theory we conclude that
\lambda(h_{1})\geq 1
.
∎

PART II: Case-by-case classification of cuspidal modules

Let
L
be a superconformal algebra
of the following list

\mathrm{W}(n),n\geq 2;\hskip 2.84526pt\mathrm{S}(n;\gamma),n\geq 2;\hskip 2.84526pt\mathrm{CK}(6);\hskip 2.84526pt\mathrm{K}_{*}(N),N\geq 3,

where
\mathrm{K}_{*}(N)
denotes the contact superalgebra of
Ramond
\mathrm{K}(N)
or of Neveu-Schwarz type
\mathrm{K}_{NS}(N)
.
In Part I we have shown that any cuspidal

\mathrm{L}
-module is isomorphic to some module
V(\lambda,\delta,u)
, as it is defined in Chapter
8
.
However the modules
V(\lambda,\delta,u)
are not
always cuspidal because their homogenous components
could be infinite dimensional.
Therefore the classification of all cuspidal

\mathrm{L}
-modules is reduced to the following question:

Besides the requirement that
\lambda
is dominant and
\lambda(h_{1})\geq 1
,

which condition insures that
V(\lambda,\delta,u)

is cuspidal?

Part II provides an answer based on a case-by-case analysis.

The proofs will always follow the following pattern.
We use Proposition
4.7
to show that
\lambda(h_{1})\geq 2
is a sufficient condition for cuspidality
of
V(\lambda,\delta,u)
. The analysis of the
\lambda(h_{1})=1
case
is based on determining if
\lambda-2\epsilon_{1}
is a weight of
V(\lambda,\delta,u)
.

9
Distributions and multidistributions

Part II uses tedious computations to check
necessary conditions for cuspidality. In order to ease these computation, we use
the setting of formal distributions and formal multidistributions.
For Part III, we also need the notion of
locality and semi-locality.

Indeed we do not follow the usual normalization, as in
[
24
]
,
for the formal distributions. This allows a uniform treatment of Ramond and Neveu-Schwarz forms and it is better adapted to
twisted superconformal algebras.

Algebra of distributions

Let
A
be a vector space and let
z
for a formal variable.
A formal expression

a(z)=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\,a_{n}z^{n}

where all coefficients
a_{n}
lie in
A
is called a

A
-valued distribution
.
We write

A[[z,z^{-1}]]
for the space of
all
A
-valued distributions and the coefficients
a_{n}
are called the
modes
of
a(z)
. The Ramond derivation
D_{z}=z\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}z}
obviously acts on the space

A[[z,z^{-1}]]
. Indeed for
a(z)=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\,a_{n}z^{n}

D_{z}(a(z))=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\,na_{n}z^{n}.

Assume now that
A
is a Lie superalgebra and let

a(z)=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\,a_{n}z^{n}
and

b(z)=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\,b_{n}z^{n}
be distributions. The product

[a(z),b(z)]
cannot be defined. However for each integer

n
we can
define the product
[-,-]_{n}
by

[a(z),b(z)]_{n}:=[a_{n}z^{n},b(z)].

A
conformal algebra
of distributions over
A

is a
\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
-graded subspace
\mathcal{C}\subset A[[z,z^{-1}]]
which is stable
by the Ramond derivation
D_{z}
and by all the products

[-,-]_{n}
.

Two distributions
a(z),b(z)\in A[[z,z^{-1}]]
are called

mutually local
if

(z_{1}-z_{2})^{N}[a(z_{1}),b(z_{2})]=0\text{ for some }N>0.

In the definition, it is tacitly assumed that
a(z)
and
b(z)
belong to
A_{\bar{0}}\cup A_{\bar{1}}
.

In our setting, Dong Lemma
[
13
]
is sated as:

Dong Lemma
.

Let
\mathcal{C}\subset A[[z,z^{-1}]]

be a conformal algebra which is generated by a
family of mutually local distributions.

Then
\mathcal{C}
consists of mutually local distributions.

Algebra of multidistributions

Given
z_{1},z_{2},\cdots,z_{d}
be

d
formal variables and
m:=(m_{1},\cdots,m_{d})\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
we set

z^{m}=z_{1}^{m_{1}}\cdots z_{d}^{m_{d}}.

A
A
-valued mutidistribution
in the variables
z_{1},\cdots,z_{d}

is a formal expression

a(z_{1},\cdots,z_{d})=\sum_{m\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\,a_{m}z^{m}

where all coefficients
a_{m}
lie in
A
.
The Ramond derivations

D_{z_{i}}=z_{i}\frac{\partial}{\partial z_{i}}
act on the space
A[[z_{1},z_{1}^{-1},\cdots,z_{d},z_{d}^{-1}]]
of multidistributions. Moreover the symmetric group
S_{d}
acts on
A[[z_{1},z_{1}^{-1},\cdots,z_{d},z_{d}^{-1}]]
by
permuting the formal variables
z_{1},\cdots,z_{d}
.

When
A
is an algebra, we can also defined the product
of two multidistributions
a
and
b
. First
we proceed with the formal operadic definition.
Let

a(z_{1},\cdots,z_{d}):=\sum_{m\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}a_{m}z^{m}\text{ and }b(z_{1},\cdots,z_{e}):=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}b_{n}z^{n}

be two
multidistributions, where
d,e
are positive integers.
We defined

a\circ b(z_{1},\cdots,z_{d+e}):=\sum_{m\in\mathbb{Z}^{e+d}}a_{(m_{1},\cdots,m_{d})}b_{(m_{d+1},\cdots,m_{d+e})}z_{1}^{m_{1}}\cdots z_{e+d}^{m_{d+e}}.

A space
\mathcal{M}=\oplus_{d\geq 1}\mathcal{M}_{d}
where

\mathcal{M}_{d}\subset A[[z_{1},z_{1}^{-1},\cdots,z_{d},z_{d}^{-1}]]
is called an
algebra of
A
-valued multidistributions

if

(a)

\mathcal{M}_{d}
is stable by the action of the
symmetric group
S_{d}
and the Ramond derivations

D_{z_{1}},\cdots D_{z_{d}}
.

(b)

\mathcal{M}
is stable by the products, that is

\mathcal{M}_{d}\circ\mathcal{M}_{e}\subset\mathcal{M}_{d+e}
.

Informally, we consider that the product

a\circ b
is legal only if
a
and
b
are
multidistributions in disjoint sets of variables, say

a(z_{1},\cdots,z_{d}):=\sum_{m\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}a_{m}z^{m}\text{ and }b(z_{d+1},\cdots,z_{d+e}):=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}b_{n}z_{d+1}^{n_{1}}\cdots z_{d+e}^{n_{e}}.

Then we write

a(z_{1},\cdots,z_{d})b(z_{d+1},\cdots,z_{d+e}):=\sum_{(m,n)\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}\times\mathbb{Z}^{e}}a_{m}b_{n}z_{1}^{m_{1}}\cdots z_{d}^{m_{d}}z_{d+1}^{m_{d+1}}\cdots z_{d+e}^{m_{d+e}}.

Local multidistributions

For
d\geq d
, an
A
-valued multidistribution

a(z_{1},\cdots,z_{d})=\sum_{m\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\,a_{m}z^{m}
in
d
variables is called

local
if

(z_{i}-z_{j})^{M}\,a(z_{1},\cdots,z_{d})=0\,\forall i,j

for some integer
M
.

For an integer
N
, set

\Lambda_{N}=\{m=(m_{1},\cdots,m_{d})\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}\mid m_{1}+\cdots+m_{d}=N\}\text{ and }

H_{N}=\{(x_{1},\cdots,x_{d})\in\mathbb{C}^{d}\mid x_{1}+\cdots+x_{d}=N\}.

A function
P_{N}:\Lambda_{N}\to\mathbb{C}
is called a

polynomial
if it extends to polynomial function
on the hyperplane
H_{N}\simeq\mathbb{C}^{N-1}
. Since

\Lambda_{N}
is Zariski-dense in
H_{N}
the polynomial extension, if any, is unique.

It is well-known that the multidistribution

a(z_{1},\cdots,z_{d})
is local if and only if

(a)

For any
N\in\mathbb{Z}
, the function

P_{N}:\Lambda_{N}\to A,\,\,(m_{1},\cdots,m_{d})\in\Lambda_{N}\mapsto a_{m_{1}}\cdots a_{m_{d}}

is polynomial, and

(b)

the degree of
P_{N}
is uniformly bounded,

see
[
24
]
.
Let
\mathrm{Mode}_{N}(a)\subset A
be the subspace
generated by the set
\{a_{m}\mid m\in\Lambda_{N}\}
.
Since
P_{N}
is a polynomial, it follows that:

Corollary 9.1
.

Let
a(z_{1},\cdots,z_{d})=\sum_{m\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\,a_{m}z^{m}
be a local
distribution. Then

(a)

The subspaces
\mathrm{Mode}_{N}(a)
have finite dimensions
and
\dim\,\mathrm{Mode}_{N}(a)
is uniformly bounded.

(b)

Let
N\in\mathbb{Z}
and let

\Lambda^{\prime}\subset\Lambda_{N}
be a
Zariski dense subset in
H_{N}
. Then the set

\{a_{m}\mid m\in\Lambda^{\prime}\}
spans
\mathrm{Mode}_{N}(a)
.

The multidistribution algebra for algebras of Ramond type

A Ramond type superconformal algebra
\mathrm{L}
has a given
structure of
\mathbb{C}[t,t^{-1}]
-module, and

\mathrm{L}=\mathbb{C}[t,t^{-1}]\otimes\mathrm{L}_{0}.

For
a\in\mathrm{L}_{0}
set

a(z_{1},\cdots,z_{d}):=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}a(t^{n_{1}+\cdots+n_{d}})\,z_{1}^{n}\cdots z_{d}^{n_{d}}.

Let
\mathcal{M}_{d}(\mathrm{L})
be the
\mathbb{C}[D_{z_{1}},\cdots,D_{z_{d}}]
-module
generated by the multidistributions
a(z_{1},\cdots,z_{d})
when
a
runs over
\mathrm{L}_{0}
. The formulas of
chapter
2
show that

[a(z_{1}),b(z_{2})]\in\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathrm{L}),\text{ for any }a,\,b\in L_{0}.

For example the formula

[t^{n}D,t^{m}D]=(m-n)t^{n+m}D

can be written as

[D(z_{1}),D(z_{2})]=(D_{z_{2}}-D_{z_{1}})D(z_{1},z_{2}).

It follows that

\mathcal{M}(\mathrm{L}):=\oplus_{d\geq 2}\mathcal{M}_{d}(L)

is an algebra of multidistributions. It is called the

algebra of multidistributions
of
\mathrm{L}
.
It is clear that all distributions in
\mathcal{M}(\mathrm{L})

are pairwise local.

Multidistributions for algebras of Neveu-Shwarz type

Let
\mathrm{L}
be superconformal algebra of Ramond type.
Recall that its Neveu-Schwarz form
\mathrm{L}_{NS}
is defined as

(\mathrm{L}_{NS})_{\bar{0}}=\mathbb{C}[t,t^{-1}]\otimes\mathrm{L}_{\bar{0},0}\text{ and }(L_{NS})_{\bar{1}}=\mathbb{C}[t,t^{-1}]\otimes t^{\frac{1}{2}}\mathrm{L}_{\bar{1},0}.

For
a\in\mathrm{L}_{\bar{0},0}
and
b\in\mathrm{L}_{\bar{1},0}

we set

a(x_{1})=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\,a(t^{n})\,x_{1}^{n}\text{ and }b(y_{1})=\sum_{n\in\frac{1}{2}+\mathbb{Z}}\,b(t^{n})\,y_{1}^{n}

More generally, we consider two infinite sets
\{x_{i}\mid i\in\mathbb{Z}\}

and
\{y_{i}\mid i\in\mathbb{Z}\}
of formal variables. Given
d
formal variables
z_{1},\cdots,z_{d}
with

z_{1}\in\{x_{1},y_{1}\}\cdots z_{d}\in\{x_{d},y_{d}\}
,
a monomial

z_{1}^{m_{1}}\cdots z_{m}^{m_{d}}
is called
legal

if
m_{i}
is an integer if
z_{i}=x_{i}
and
m_{i}
is an half-integer otherwise. Let

a\in\mathrm{L}_{\bar{0},0}\cup\mathrm{L}_{\bar{1},0}
. Assume that
the cardinality of the set
\{i|z_{i}=y_{i}\}
has the same parity as
a
. Then we define the multidistribution

a(z_{1},\cdots,z_{d})=\sum_{m}a(t^{m_{1}+\cdots+m_{d}})\,z_{1}^{m_{1}}\cdots z_{m}^{m_{d}}

where the summation only runs over legal
monomials
z_{1}^{m_{1}}\cdots z_{m}^{m_{d}}
.
Then the multidistribution algebra of
\mathcal{M}(\mathrm{L}_{NS})
is defined
by the space generated by the multidistributions

a(z_{1},\cdots,z_{d})

and their iterated Ramond derivatives.
Except the rule about the legal monomials, the algebra

\mathcal{M}(\mathrm{L}_{NS})
is similar to the algebra

\mathcal{M}(\mathrm{L})
.

Multidistributions for algebras
\mathrm{K}^{(2)}(2m)
with
m\geq 2

Recall that
\mathrm{K}^{(2)}(2m)
is the algebra
\mathrm{L}^{\sigma}

of fixed points under the involution
\sigma
, where

\mathrm{L}=\widehat{\mathrm{K}(4)}
for
m=2
,
\mathrm{L}=\mathrm{K}(2m)
otherwise.
Recall that

\mathrm{L}^{\sigma}=\mathbb{C}[t^{2},t^{-2}]\otimes\mathrm{L}_{0}^{\sigma}\oplus\mathbb{C}[t^{2},t^{-2}]\otimes t\mathrm{L}_{0}^{-\sigma},

where
\mathrm{L}_{0}^{-\sigma}=\{x\in\mathrm{L}-0\mid\sigma(x)=-x\}
.

For
a\in\mathrm{L}_{0}^{\sigma}
and
b\in\mathrm{L}_{0}^{-\sigma}

we set

a(x_{1})=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\,a(t^{2n})\,x_{1}^{2n}\text{ and }b(y_{1})=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\,b(t^{2n+1})\,y_{1}^{2n+1}.

More generally, we consider two infinite sets
\{x_{i}\mid i\in\mathbb{Z}\}

and
\{y_{i}\mid i\in\mathbb{Z}\}
of formal variables. Given
d
formal variables
z_{1},\cdots,z_{d}
with

z_{1}\in\{x_{1},y_{1}\}\cdots z_{d}\in\{x_{d},y_{d}\}
,
a monomial

z_{1}^{m_{1}}\cdots z_{m}^{m_{d}}
is called
legal

if
m_{i}
is an even integer if
z_{i}=x_{i}
and
m_{i}
is an odd integer otherwise. Let

a\in\mathrm{L}_{\bar{0},0}\cup\mathrm{L}_{\bar{1},0}
. Assume that
the cardinality of the set
\{i|z_{i}=y_{i}\}
has the same parity as
a
. Then we define the multidistribution

a(z_{1},\cdots,z_{d})=\sum_{m}\,a(t^{m_{1}+\cdots+m_{d}})\,z_{1}^{m_{1}}\cdots z_{m}^{m_{d}}

where the summation only run over legal
monomials
z_{1}^{m_{1}}\cdots z_{m}^{m_{d}}
.
Then the multidistribution algebra
\mathcal{M}(\mathrm{L}^{\sigma})
is defined
by the space generated by the multidistributions

a(z_{1},\cdots,z_{d})

and their Ramond derivatives.

The algebra of multidistributions
\mathcal{M}(\mathrm{K}^{(2)}(2m)))

are
mutually semi-local
that is
given two multidistributions

a(z_{1},\cdots,z_{d})
and
b(z_{d+1},\cdots,z_{d+e})

and any integers
i,j\in\{1,\cdots,d+e\}
,
we have:

(z_{i}^{2}-z_{j}^{2})^{M}[a(z_{1},\cdots,z_{d}),b(z_{d+1},\cdots,z_{d+e})]=0\text{ for }M>>0.

10
Classification of cuspidal
\mathrm{W}(n)
-modules

Recall from Section
2.3
that

\mathrm{W}(n)=\mathrm{Der\,}\,\mathbb{C}[t,t^{-1},\xi_{1},\cdots,\xi^{n}].

Its Cartan subalgebra
H
has basis

(\xi_{i}\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi_{i}})_{1\leq i\leq n}
and let
(\epsilon_{i})_{1\leq i\leq n}
be the dual basis of
H^{*}
. For
1\leq i<n
, set

h_{i}=\xi_{i}\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi_{i}}-\xi_{i+1}\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi_{i+1}}.

For

(\lambda,\delta,\,u)\in H^{*}\times\mathbb{C}\times\mathbb{C},

we have defined
the
\mathrm{W}(n)
-module
V(\lambda,\delta,u)

in Chapter
8
.
Recall that
the definition of
V(\lambda,\delta,u)

depends on the chosen triangular decomposition

\mathrm{L}=\mathrm{L}^{+}\oplus\mathrm{L}^{(0)}\oplus\mathrm{L}^{-}

of the superconformal algebra
\mathrm{L}:=\mathrm{W}(n)
.
It has been shown in Chapter
8
that

V(\lambda,\delta,u)
is cuspidal only if

\lambda
is dominant and
\lambda(h_{1})\geq 1
.

Recall that
\lambda_{i}=\lambda(\xi_{i}\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi_{i}})
.

Theorem 7
.

Let
n\geq 2
. For an arbitrary triple

(\lambda,\delta,\,u)\in H^{*}\times\mathbb{C}\times\mathbb{C},

with
\lambda
dominant,
the
\mathrm{W}(n)
-module
V(\lambda,\delta,u)
is cuspidal
if and only if

(a)

either
\lambda(h_{1})\geq 2

(b)

or
\lambda(h_{1})=1
and
\lambda_{1}=1-\delta
.

Since we will repeatedly use the same scheme of proof
in our case-by-case analysis, we will provide some details. The sufficiency of Conditions (a) and (b),
namely Corollary
10.2
, uses Proposition
4.7
about coinduction functors.

Their necessity is based on explicit tedious
computations. In order to alleviate the computation, we use
the setting of multidistributions, see Section
9
.
Then the necessity of Conditions (a) and (b) is shown
by Corollary
10.5
.

Highest weights of finite dimensional
\mathfrak{gl}(1,n)
-modules

Consider the Lie superalgebra

\mathfrak{gl}(1,n)
. Then

\mathfrak{gl}(1,n)_{\overline{0}}
has basis

\{e_{0,0}\}\cup\{e_{i,j}\mid 1\leq i,j\leq n\}

and
\mathfrak{gl}(1,n)_{\overline{1}}
has basis

\{e_{0,j}\mid j\neq 0\}\cup\{e_{i,0}\mid i\neq 0\}.

The Lie bracket is

[e_{i,j},e_{k,l}]=\delta_{j,k}e_{i,l}-(-1)^{|e_{i,j}||e_{k,l}|}\delta_{i,l}e_{k,j}.

Set

H=\oplus_{i=1}^{n}\,\mathbb{C}e_{i,i}

and let
\epsilon_{i}
be the dual basis of
H^{*}
.
The action of
H
on
\mathfrak{gl}(1,n)
provides an eigenspace
decomposition

\mathrm{L}=H^{e}+\sum_{\alpha\in\Delta(\mathfrak{k})}\,\mathfrak{gl}(1,n)^{(\alpha)},

where
\Delta(\mathfrak{gl}(1,n)):=\{\alpha\in H^{*}\mid\alpha\neq 0\hbox{ and }\mathfrak{gl}(1,n)^{(\alpha)}\neq 0\}
.

Let again
F\in H
be the element defined by

\epsilon_{1}(F)=2^{n}-1\hbox{ and }\epsilon_{k}(F)=-2^{k-1}\hbox{ for }k>1,

and set

\Delta^{+}(\mathfrak{gl}(1,n))=\{\alpha\in\Delta\mid\alpha(F)>0\},\hbox{ and }

\Delta^{-}(\mathfrak{gl}(1,n))=\{\alpha\in\Delta\mid\alpha(F)<0\}.

More explicitly

\Delta^{+}(\mathfrak{gl}(1,n))=\{\epsilon_{1}\}\cup\{-\epsilon_{i}\mid 1<i\leq n\}\cup\{\epsilon_{i}-\epsilon_{j}\mid 1\leq i<j\leq n\}

and the corresponding roots vectors are

e_{1,0},e_{0,i}
and
e_{i,j}
.

For a pair
(\lambda,\delta)\in H^{*}\times\mathbb{C}
, let
L(\lambda,\delta)
be the
simple
\mathfrak{gl}(1,n)
-module with highest weight

(\lambda,\delta)
. By this we mean that

L(\lambda,\delta)
is generated by a vector
v
satisfying

h.v=\lambda(h)v\hbox{ for }h\in H

e_{0,0}.v=\delta v

\mathfrak{gl}(1,n)^{(\alpha)}.v=0\hbox{ if }\alpha\in\Delta^{+}(\mathfrak{gl}(1,n)).

If
L(\lambda,\delta)
is finite dimensional,

\lambda
is dominant, that is

\lambda(h_{i})\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}\hbox{ for }1\leq i\leq n-1,

where
h_{i}=e_{i,i}-e_{i+1,i+1}
.
However this condition does not insure that

\dim\,L(\lambda,\delta)<\infty
. Set

\lambda_{1}=\lambda(e_{1,1})
.

Lemma 10.1
.

Let
(\lambda,\delta)\in H^{*}\times\mathbb{C}

be an arbitrary pair with
\lambda
dominant.
If

(a)

either
\lambda(h_{1})\geq 1

(b)

or
\lambda(h_{1})=0
and
\lambda_{1}+\delta=0

the
\mathfrak{gl}(1,n)
-module
L(\lambda,\delta)
is
finite dimensional.

Proof.

We first define the Kac modules.
To simplify the notation, set
\mathfrak{k}=\mathfrak{gl}(1,n)
.

We observe that

\mathfrak{k}_{\overline{0}}\simeq\mathfrak{gl}(n)\times\mathfrak{gl}(1)
.
For a pair
(\mu,\delta)\in H^{*}\times\mathbb{C}
, let

S(\mu,\delta)
be the
simple
\mathfrak{k}_{\overline{0}}
-module with highest weight

(\mu,\delta)
. It is generated by a vector
s

satisfying

h.s=\mu(h)s\hbox{ for }h\in H

e_{0,0}.s=\delta s

\mathfrak{k}_{\overline{0}}^{\alpha}.s=0\hbox{ if }\alpha\in\Delta_{\overline{0}}^{+}(\mathfrak{k}).

Set
T:=\oplus\oplus_{i=1}^{n}\mathfrak{k}^{-\epsilon_{i}}
.
Since
T
is an ideal in the subalgebra

\mathfrak{p}:=\mathfrak{k}_{\overline{0}}\oplus T
we can consider

S(\mu,\delta)
as a
\mathfrak{p}
-module on which

T
acts trivially. The
\mathfrak{k}
-module

\mathrm{Kac}(\mu,\delta):=\mathrm{Ind}_{\mathfrak{p}}^{\mathfrak{k}}\,S(\mu,\delta)

is called the
Kac module
.

First assume that
\lambda(h_{1})\geq 1
. Thus the weight

\mu:=\lambda-\epsilon_{1}
is dominant.
By Poincaré-Birkhoff-Witt Theorem, we have

\mathrm{Kac}(\mu,\delta+1)\simeq\mathbb{C}[e_{1,0},\cdots,e_{n,0}]S(\mu,\delta+1),

thus
\mathrm{Kac}(\mu,\delta+1)
is finite dimensional.
For
i\geq 2
the root
\epsilon_{i}
is negative, hence
\lambda=\mu+\epsilon_{1}
is the highest weight of

\mathrm{Kac}(\mu,\delta+1)
. Also we observe that

\mathrm{Kac}(\mu,\delta+1)^{(\lambda)}=\mathbb{C}e_{1,0}.s
.

Set
v=e_{1,0}.s
. We have

e_{0,0}.v=e_{0,0}e_{1,0}s=[e_{0,0},e_{1,0}]s+e_{1,0}e_{0,0}s=-e_{1,0}.s+(\delta+1)e_{1,0}.s=\delta v.

Hence
K(\mu,\delta+1)
admits a subquotient isomorphic to
L(\lambda,\delta)
which proves that

L(\lambda,\delta)
is finite dimensional.

Next assume that
\lambda_{1}+\delta=0
.
Set

\mathfrak{k}^{\pm}:=\oplus_{\alpha\in\Delta^{+}(\mathfrak{k})}\,\mathfrak{k}^{\alpha}.

It has been shown that
\lambda+\epsilon_{1}
is the highest
weight of
\mathrm{Kac}(\lambda,\delta)
. Let
X
be the

\mathfrak{k}
-submodule generated by
v
.
By Poincaré-Birkhoff-Witt Theorem we have

X=U(\mathfrak{k}^{-}).v
, and more precisely

X=U(\mathfrak{k}_{\overline{0}}^{-})\mathbb{C}[e_{2,0},\cdots,e_{n,0}]\mathbb{C}[e_{0,1}].v.

However we have

e_{0,1}.v=e_{0,1}e_{1,0}.s=[e_{0,1},e_{1,0.}].s=(e_{0,0}+e_{1,1})s=(\lambda_{1}+\delta)s=0.

Therefore
X=U(\mathfrak{k}_{\overline{0}}^{-})\mathbb{C}[e_{0,2},\cdots,e_{0,n}]v,

which shows that
X^{(\lambda)}=0
.
Since it has been observed that

\mathrm{Kac}(\lambda,\delta)^{(\lambda+\epsilon_{1})}=\mathbb{C}v
,
it follows that

\lambda
is a maximal weight of

\mathrm{Kac}(\lambda,\delta)/X
. Thus
L(\lambda,\delta)
is a subquotient of
\mathrm{Kac}(\lambda,\delta)/X
, which shows that

L(\lambda,\delta)
is finite dimensional.
∎

Sufficiency of Conditions (a) and (b)

Let

(\lambda,\delta,u)\in
be an arbitrary triple
with
\lambda
dominant. In this section we prove:

Corollary 10.2
.

Assume that

(a)

either
\lambda(h_{1})\geq 2

(b)

or
\lambda(h_{1})=1
and
\lambda_{1}=1-\delta
.

Then the
\mathrm{W}(n)
-module
V(\lambda,\delta,u)
is cuspidal.

Proof.

Let
{\bf m}\subset\mathbb{C}[t,t^{-1},\xi_{1},\cdots,\xi^{n}]
be the the maximal ideal

{\bf m}:=(t,\xi_{1},\cdots,\xi_{n}).

Set
\mathrm{W}(n)^{\bf(1)}={\bf m}\mathrm{W}(n)
,

\mathrm{W}(n)^{\bf(2)}={\bf m}^{2}\mathrm{W}(n)
, and

T=\mathrm{W}(n)/\mathrm{W}(n)^{\bf(1)}
.
(Intuitively
{\bf m}
is a point of the supermanifold

(\mathbb{C}^{*})^{1,n}
, the subalgebra

\mathrm{W}(n)^{\bf(1)}

is the isotropy at the point
{\bf m}
,

\mathrm{W}(n)^{\bf(2)}
consists of derivations vanishing twice
at
{\bf m}
and
T
is the tangent space at this point.)

Clearly
\mathrm{W}(n)^{\bf(2)}
is an ideal of
\mathrm{W}(n)^{\bf(1)}

and

\mathrm{W}(n)^{\bf(1)}\simeq\mathfrak{gl}(1,n)\ltimes\mathrm{W}(n)^{\bf(2)},

where
\mathfrak{gl}(1,n)
is the subalgebra with basis

e_{0,0}=(t-1)D
,
e_{i,0}=\xi_{i}D
,

e_{0,i}=(t-1)\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi_{i}}
, and

e_{i,j}=\xi_{i}\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi_{j}}

for
1\leq i,j\leq n
.

Set
\mu=\lambda-\epsilon_{1}
. We consider
the
\mathfrak{gl}(1,n)
-module
L(\mu,\delta)
as a

\mathrm{W}(n)^{\bf(1)}
-module with a trivial action of

\mathrm{W}(n)^{\bf(2)}
.
By Lemma
10.1

\dim\,L(\mu,\delta)<\infty.

We check the hypotheses of Proposition
4.7
.
Here the grading element
\ell_{0}
is the Ramond derivation
D
and

\mathrm{W}(n)_{\overline{0}}=\mathbb{C}D\oplus\mathrm{W}(n)^{\bf(1)}_{\overline{0}}.

The tangent space
T
has basis

\{D\}\cup\{\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi_{i}}\mid 1\leq i\leq n\}\hbox{ modulo }\mathrm{W}^{\bf(1)}.

Thus

T^{-}\simeq\mathbb{C}\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi_{1}}.

Since
[t-1)D,\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi_{1}}]=0
we have

\delta^{-}=0
.

By
Proposition
4.7
the highest weight of

\mathcal{F}(L(\mu,\delta),u)
is
\lambda=\mu+\epsilon_{1}
and

\mathcal{F}(L(\mu,\delta),u)^{(\lambda)}\simeq{\mathrm{Tens}}(\lambda,\delta,u).

Since
V(\lambda,\delta,u)
is a subquotient of

\mathcal{F}(L(\mu,\delta),u)
, we conclude that

V(\lambda,\delta,u)
is cuspidal.
∎

Explicit computations

Let

(\lambda,\delta,\,u)\in H^{*}\times\mathbb{C}\times\mathbb{C},

and set
V=V(\lambda,\delta,\,u)
.
We are looking
for a condition which insures that

V^{\lambda-2\epsilon_{1}}\neq 0
.

We freely use the definition of section
9.4
.
Here our formal variables are
v,w,x,y
and
z
.
Recall that for any element
a\in\mathrm{L}_{0}
, we define the formal series

a(v)=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\,(t^{n}a)v^{n}\in\mathrm{L}[[v,v^{-1}]].

We will also consider
V
-valued multidistribution,
so there all considered multidistribution are valued in the Lie superalgebra
\mathrm{L}\ltimes V
.

We have

[\xi_{1}D(v),\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi_{1}}(w)]=D_{w}\xi_{1}\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi_{1}}(v,w)+D(v,w),

[\xi_{1}\xi_{k}\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi_{k}}(v),\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi_{1}}(w)]=\xi_{k}\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi_{k}}(v,w)\hbox{ for }k\neq 1.

We deduce

\left[\xi_{1}D(v),[\xi_{1}D(w),\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi_{1}}(x)]\right]=(D_{w}-D_{v})\,\xi_{1}D(v,w,x),

\left[\xi_{1}\xi_{k}\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi_{k}}(v),[\xi_{1}D(w),\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi_{1}}(x)]\right]=-(D_{v}+D_{x})\xi_{1}\xi_{k}\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi_{k}}(u,w,x),

\left[\xi_{1}\xi_{k}\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi_{k}}(v),[\xi_{1}\xi_{l}\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi_{l}}(w),\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi_{1}}(x)]\right]=0.

In what follows we will always implicitly exclude the case
\lambda=0
.
Thus
V^{(\lambda)}={\mathrm{Tens}}(\lambda,\delta,u)
is
a
\mathbb{C}[t,t^{-1}]
-module. Thus for

\mathrm{v}\in V^{(\lambda)}_{0}
the element
\mathrm{v}(t^{n}):=t^{n}\otimes\mathrm{v}
is well
defined. As before, we can define the formal distribution

\mathrm{v}(z):=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\,\mathrm{v}(t^{n})z^{n}.

We have

D(v)\mathrm{v}(z)=(\delta D_{v}+\Delta_{z})\mathrm{v}(u,z),

where
\Delta_{z}=D_{z}+u
.

Lemma 10.3
.

For
\mathrm{v}\in V^{(\lambda)}_{0}
,
set
\tilde{\mathrm{v}}=\mathrm{v}(v,w,x,y,z)
. Let

2\leq k,l\leq n
.
We have

(a)
\xi_{1}D(v)\,\xi_{1}D(w)\,\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi_{1}}(x)\,\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi_{1}}(y)\,\mathrm{v}(z)=\delta(1-\delta-\lambda_{1})(D_{v}-D_{w})(D_{x}-D_{y})\tilde{\mathrm{v}}

(b)
\xi_{1}\xi_{k}\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi_{k}}(v)\,\xi_{1}D(w)\,\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi_{1}}(x)\,\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi_{1}}(y)\,\mathrm{v}(z)=\lambda_{k}(\lambda_{1}+\delta-1)(D_{x}-D_{y})\tilde{\mathrm{v}}

(c)
\xi_{1}\xi_{k}\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi_{k}}(v)\,\xi_{1}\xi_{l}\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi_{l}}(w)\,\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi_{1}}(x)\,\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi_{1}}(y)\,\mathrm{v}(z)=0

Proof.

Set
\mathrm{L}=\mathrm{W}(n)
. Let
A_{1},A_{2}
be formal distributions in
\mathrm{L}^{(\epsilon_{1})}
and

B_{1},B_{2}
be formal distributions in

\mathrm{L}^{(-\epsilon_{1})}
. Since the distribution

[A_{1},[A_{2},B_{2}]]\mathrm{v}
has weight

\lambda+\epsilon_{1}
, we have
[A_{1},[A_{2},B_{2}]]\mathrm{v}=0
.

We deduce the formula

A_{1}A_{2}B_{1}B_{2}\mathrm{v}=A+B-C

where

A:=[A_{1},[A_{2},B_{1}]B_{2}\,\mathrm{v},

B:=[A_{2},B_{1}][A_{1},B_{2}]\,\mathrm{v},\hbox{ and }

C:=[A_{1},B_{1}][A_{2},B_{2}])\,\mathrm{v}

which will be used in the next computations.
Also we have

C=[A_{1},[A_{2},B_{1}]B_{2}.\mathrm{v}=[[A_{1},[A_{2},B_{1}]B_{2}]\,\mathrm{v}.

Proof of (a)
In the Formula (a), we have

A_{1}=\xi_{1}D(v)
,
A_{2}=\xi_{1}D(w)
,
B_{1}=\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi_{1}}(x)
and

B_{2}=\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi_{1}}(y)
. Thus we decompose

\xi_{1}D(v)\,\xi_{1}D(w)\,\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi_{1}}(x)\,\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi_{1}}(y)\mathrm{v}(z)
as
A+B-C
.
We compute now each term
A
,
B
and
C
.

A=\left[\xi_{1}D(v),[\xi_{1}D(w),\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi_{1}}(x)]\right]\,\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi_{1}}(y)\,\mathrm{v}(z)

=\left((D_{w}-D_{v})\xi_{1}D(v,w,x)\right)\,\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi_{1}}(y)\mathrm{v}(z)

=\left[(D_{w}-D_{v})\xi_{1}D(v,w,x),\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi_{1}}(y)\right]\mathrm{v}(z)

=(D_{w}-D_{v})\left(D_{y}\xi_{1}\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi_{1}}(v,w,x,y)+D(v,w,x,y)\right)\,\mathrm{v}(z)

=(D_{w}-D_{v})\left(\lambda_{1}D_{y}+(\delta(D_{v}+D_{w}+D_{x}+D_{y})+\Delta_{z}\right)\,\tilde{\mathrm{v}}

=(D_{w}-D_{v})\left((\delta(D_{v}+D_{w})+\delta D_{x}+(\delta+\lambda_{1})D_{y}+\Delta_{z}\right)\,\tilde{\mathrm{v}}

B=[\xi_{1}D(w),\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi_{1}}(x)]\,[\xi_{1}D(v),\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi_{1}}(y)]\,\mathrm{v}(z)

=\left(D_{x}\xi_{1}\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi_{1}}(w,x)+D(w,x)\right)\,\left(D_{y}\xi_{1}\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi_{1}}(v,y)+D(v,y)\right)\,\mathrm{v}(z)

=\left(\lambda_{1}D_{x}+\delta(D_{x}+D_{w})+D_{v}+D_{y}+\Delta_{z}\right)\left(\lambda_{1}D_{y}+\delta(D_{v}+D_{y})+\Delta_{z}\right)\tilde{\mathrm{v}}

=\left(D_{v}+\delta D_{w}+(\delta+\lambda_{1})D_{x}+D_{y}+\Delta_{z}\right)\,\left(\delta D_{v}+(\lambda_{1}+\delta)D_{y}+\Delta_{z}\right)\,\tilde{\mathrm{v}}

Since

C=[\xi_{1}D(v),\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi_{1}}(x)]\,[\xi_{1}D(w),\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi_{1}}(y)]\,\mathrm{v}(z)

the formula for
C
is the same as for
B

by exchanging the roles
v
and
w
. Therefore

C=(\delta D_{v}+D_{w}+(\delta+\lambda_{1})D_{x}+D_{y}+\Delta_{z})(\delta D_{w}+(\lambda_{1}+\delta)D_{y}+\Delta_{z})\tilde{\mathrm{v}}

Since
[\xi_{1}D(v),\xi_{1}D(w)]=0
and
[\,\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi_{1}}(x),\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi_{1}}(y)]=0
Formula (a)
is skew symmetric in the variables
(v,w)
and in the variables
(x,y)
. Hence we have

A+B-C=f(\delta,\lambda_{1},\Delta_{z})(D_{v}-D_{w})(D_{y}-D_{x})\tilde{\mathrm{v}}

for some function
f
. Thus it is enough to evaluate
the coefficient of the monomial
D_{v}D_{y}
. This coefficient is

-(\delta+\lambda_{1})
in expression
A
,

\lambda_{1}+2\delta
in expression
B
and

\delta(\lambda_{1}+\delta)
in expression
C
.
Therefore
A+B-C
contains the monomial

\delta(1-\delta-\lambda_{1})
, which proves that

A+B-C=\delta(1-\delta-\lambda_{1})(D_{v}-D_{w})(D_{x}-D_{y})\,\tilde{\mathrm{v}},

which proves Formula (a).

Proof of (b)

Assume
k\neq 1
. We similarily decompose Formula (b) as a sum
A+B-C
. We have

A=-(D_{v}+D_{x})\xi_{1}\xi_{k}\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi_{k}}(v,w,x)\,\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi_{1}}(y)\,\mathrm{v}(z)

=-(D_{v}+D_{x})\left[\xi_{1}\xi_{k}\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi_{k}}(v,w,x),\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi_{1}}(y)\right]\,\mathrm{v}(z)

=-(D_{v}+D_{x})\xi_{k}\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi_{k}}(v,w,x,y)\,\mathrm{v}(z)

=-\lambda_{k}(D_{v}+D_{x})\tilde{\mathrm{v}}

B=[\xi_{1}D(w),\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi_{1}}(x)]\,[\xi_{1}\xi_{k}\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi_{k}}(v),\,\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi_{1}}(y)]\,\mathrm{v}(z)

=\left(D_{x}\xi_{1}\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi_{1}}(w,x)+D(w,x)\right)\,\xi_{k}\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi_{k}}(v,y)\,\mathrm{v}(z)

=\lambda_{k}(\lambda_{1}D_{x}+D_{v}+D_{y}+\Delta_{z}+\delta D_{w}+\delta D_{x})\tilde{\mathrm{v}}

=\lambda_{k}(D_{v}+\delta D_{w}+(\lambda_{1}+\delta)D_{x}+D_{y}+\Delta_{z})\,\tilde{\mathrm{v}}

C=[\xi_{1}\xi_{k}\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi_{k}}(v),\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi_{1}}(x)]\,[\xi_{1}D(w),\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi_{1}}(y)]\mathrm{v}(z)

=\xi_{k}\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi_{k}}(v,x)\left(D_{y}\xi_{1}\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi_{1}}(w,y)+D(w,y)\right)\,\mathrm{v}(z)

=\lambda_{k}(\lambda_{1}D_{y}+\delta D_{w}+\delta D_{y}+\Delta_{z})\,\tilde{\mathrm{v}}

Thus

A+B-C=\lambda_{k}(\lambda_{1}+\delta-1)(D_{x}-D_{y})\tilde{\mathrm{v}},

which proves Formula (b).

Formula (c) is obvious.
∎

Necessity of Conditions (a) and (b)

Let

(\lambda,\delta,\,u)\in H^{*}\times\mathbb{C}\times\mathbb{C},

and set
V:=V(\lambda,\delta,\,u)
.

Lemma 10.4
.

Assume that
\lambda(h_{1})=1
. If

V^{(\lambda-2\epsilon_{1})}=0
, then

\lambda_{1}=1-\delta.

Proof.

Set
\mathrm{L}=\mathrm{W}(n)
. By hypothesis
we have

\mathrm{L}^{(\epsilon_{1})}\mathrm{L}^{(\epsilon_{1})}\mathrm{L}^{(-\epsilon_{1})}\mathrm{L}^{(-\epsilon_{1})}V^{(\lambda)}=0.

Therefore by Lemma
10.3
, we have

\delta(1-\delta-\lambda_{1})=0
and

\lambda_{2}(\lambda_{1}+\delta-1)=0
.
Thus

(\lambda_{2}+\delta)(\lambda_{1}+\delta-1)=0.

Since
\lambda(h_{1})=\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2}
, we have

\lambda_{2}+\delta=\lambda_{1}+\delta-1
. We deduce

(\lambda_{1}+\delta-1)^{2}=0,

which proves the Lemma.
∎

Corollary 10.5
.

Assume that the
\mathrm{W}(n)
-module

V(\lambda,\delta,u)
is cuspidal.
Then

(a)

either
\lambda(h_{1})\geq 2
,

(b)

or
\lambda(h_{1})=1
and
\lambda_{1}=1-\delta
.

Proof.

By Proposition
6
,

\lambda(h_{1})
is an integer
\geq 1
.
Set
V=V(\lambda,\delta,u)
.

If
V^{(\lambda-2\epsilon_{1})}=0
, then Condition (a) or
(b) is satisfied by Lemma
10.4
.

Assume otherwise. Any
negative root
\alpha\in\Delta^{-}
satisfies

\alpha=-\epsilon_{1}\hbox{ modulo }\Lambda
, or

\alpha=-\epsilon_{1}+\epsilon_{2}\hbox{ modulo }\Lambda
, or

\alpha=\epsilon_{2}\hbox{ modulo }\Lambda
, or

\alpha
belongs to
\Lambda
,

where
\Lambda:=\oplus_{k\geq 3}\mathbb{Z}\epsilon_{k}
.
Hence

-\epsilon_{1}-\epsilon_{2}
is not
a positive combination of negative roots. Thus

V^{(\lambda-\epsilon_{1}-\epsilon_{2})}=0
.

It follows that
e_{1}V^{(\lambda-2\epsilon_{1})}=0
. Since

V
contains only finitely many weights, we have

(\lambda-2\epsilon_{1})(h_{1})\geq 0
, which proves that

\lambda(h_{1})\geq 2
.
∎

11
Classification of cuspidal
\mathrm{S}(n;\gamma)
-modules

To insure that
\mathrm{S}(n;\gamma)
is simple, we assume
n\geq 2
. By definition,
\mathrm{S}(n;\gamma)
is a
\mathbb{Z}
-graded subalgebra of
\mathrm{W}(n)
. Its Cartan subalgebra is

H^{0}:=\oplus_{i=1}^{n-1}\,\mathbb{C}h_{i}.

In Section
2.4
an embedding

\mathrm{Vir}\subset S(n,\gamma)
has been chosen.
A weight
\lambda\in(H^{0})^{*}
is dominant if and only if
all
\lambda(h_{i})
are nonnegative integers.

For any triple

(\lambda,\delta,\,u)\in(H^{0})^{*}\times\mathbb{C}\times\mathbb{C},
we have defined the
\mathrm{S}(n;\gamma)
-module

V(\lambda,\delta,u)
in Chapter
8
.
The result of the chapter is:

Theorem 8
.

Let

(\lambda,\delta,\,u)\in(H^{0})^{*}\times\mathbb{C}\times\mathbb{C},

be an arbitrary triple with
\lambda
dominant. Then
the
\mathrm{S}(n;\gamma)
-module
V(\lambda,\delta,u)
is cuspidal if and only if

(a)

either
\lambda(h_{1})\geq 2

(b)

or
\lambda(h_{1})=1
and

\delta=1
.

The proof is an immediate consequence of
Lemma
11.1
and Corollary
11.4

proved below.

Sufficiency of Conditions (a) and (b)

Let
(\lambda,\delta,u)\in(H^{0})^{*}\times\mathbb{C}\times\mathbb{C}

be an arbitrary triple with
\lambda
dominant.

Lemma 11.1
.

Assume that

(a)

either
\lambda(h_{1})\geq 2

(b)

or
\lambda(h_{1})=1
and
\lambda_{1}=1-\delta
.

Then the
\mathrm{S}(n;\gamma)
-module
V(\lambda,\delta,u)
is cuspidal.

Proof.

By definition
\mathrm{S}(n;\gamma)
is a subalgebra of
\mathrm{W}(n)
and
H^{0}
is a
1
-codimensional subspace
of the Cartan subalgebra
H
of
\mathrm{W}(n)
. The weight
\lambda
admits a unique
extension
\tilde{\lambda}\in H^{*}
with

\tilde{\lambda}_{1}=0
.

The subalgebras
\mathrm{Vir}
of
\mathrm{W}(n)
and
\mathrm{S}(n;\gamma)

are different. However
on the

C_{\mathrm{W}(n)}(F)
-module
{\mathrm{Tens}}(\tilde{\lambda},\delta,u)
the Virasoro element

E_{n}=-\left(t^{n}D+(n+\gamma)t^{n}\xi_{1}\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi_{i}}\right)\in\mathrm{S}(n;\gamma)

acts as the Virasoro
element
-t^{n}D\in\mathrm{W}(n)
. Therefore as a

C_{\mathrm{S}(n;\gamma)}(F)
-modules

{\mathrm{Tens}}(\tilde{\lambda},\delta,u)
and

{\mathrm{Tens}}(\lambda,\delta,u)
are isomorphic.

It follows that the
\mathrm{S}(n;\gamma)
-module

V(\lambda,\delta,u)
is a subquotient of the

\mathrm{W}(n)
-module
V(\tilde{\lambda},\delta,u)
,
which is cuspidal by Corollary
10.2
. So

V(\lambda,\delta,u)
is also cuspidal.
∎

Necessity of Conditions (a) and (b)

Let
(\lambda,\delta,u)\in(H^{0})^{*}\times\mathbb{C}\times\mathbb{C}

be an arbitrary triple. Set

V=V(\lambda,\delta,u)
.

For
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, define the elements

A_{n}\in S(n:\gamma)
as

A_{n}=t^{n}\xi_{1}D+(n+\gamma)t^{n}\xi_{1}\xi_{2}\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi_{2}}.

Let consider the formal distribution

A(v)=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}A_{n}v^{n}
. With the same notations as
in Subsection
10.3
:

Lemma 11.2
.

Let
\mathrm{v}\in\tilde{V}^{(\lambda)}_{0}
.
We have

A(v)\,A(w)\,\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi_{1}}(x)\,\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi_{1}}(y)\,\mathrm{v}(z)=(\delta-\lambda(h_{1}))(1-\delta)(D_{v}-D_{w})(D_{x}-D_{y})\tilde{\mathrm{v}},

where
\tilde{\mathrm{v}}=\mathrm{v}(v,w,x,y,z)
.

Proof.

As before we extend

\lambda
to a linear form

\tilde{\lambda}\in H^{*}
satisfying
\tilde{\lambda}_{1}=0
.
Let
\tilde{V}
be the
\mathrm{W}(n)
-module

V(\tilde{\lambda},\delta,u)
. Since
V(\lambda,\delta,u)

is a quotient of the
\mathrm{S}(n;\gamma)
-module generated by

\tilde{V}^{(\lambda)}
it is enough to show the same formula for
\mathrm{v}\in V(\tilde{\lambda}_{0}
.

Set
\Delta_{v}=D_{v}+\gamma
and

\Delta_{w}=D_{w}+\gamma
. We have

A(v)=\xi_{1}D(v)+\Delta_{v}\xi_{1}\xi_{2}\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi_{2}}(v),

thus
A(v)A(w)=A+B+C+D
where

A:=\xi_{1}D(v)\xi_{1}D(w),

B:=\Delta_{v}\xi_{1}\xi_{2}\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi_{2}}(v)\xi_{1}D(w),

C=:\xi_{1}D(v)\Delta_{w}\xi_{1}\xi_{2}\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi_{2}}(w)\hbox{ and }

D:=\Delta_{v}\xi_{1}\xi_{2}\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi_{2}}(v)\Delta_{w}\xi_{1}\xi_{2}\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi_{2}}(w).

Hence
A(v)\,A(w)\,\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi_{1}}(x)\,\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi_{1}}(y)\,\mathrm{v}(z)
is the sum of four terms that we compute succesively. By
Lemma
10.3
:

A\,\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi_{1}}(x)\,\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi_{1}}(y)\,\mathrm{v}(z)=\delta(1-\delta)(D_{v}-D_{w})(D_{x}-D_{y})\tilde{\mathrm{v}},

B\,\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi_{1}}(x)\,\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi_{1}}(y)\,\mathrm{v}(z)=\Delta_{v}\tilde{\lambda_{2}}(\delta-1)(D_{x}-D_{y})\tilde{\mathrm{v}}.

We observe that

C=-\Delta_{w}\xi_{1}\xi_{2}\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi_{2}}(w)\,\xi_{1}D(v)
, thus

C\,\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi_{1}}(x)\,\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi_{1}}(y)\,\mathrm{v}(z)=-\Delta_{w}\tilde{\lambda_{2}}(\delta-1)(D_{x}-D_{y})\tilde{\mathrm{v}}.

By Lemma
10.3
we have

D\,\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi_{1}}(x)\,\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi_{1}}(y)\,\mathrm{v}(z)=0.

We observe that
\tilde{\lambda_{2}}=-\lambda(h_{1})
and

\Delta_{v}-\Delta_{w})=\Delta_{v}-\Delta_{w}
, thus

A(v)\,A(w)\,\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi_{1}}(x)\,\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi_{1}}(y)\,\mathrm{v}(z)=(\delta-\lambda(h_{1}))(1-\delta)(D_{v}-D_{w})(D_{x}-D_{y})\tilde{\mathrm{v}}.

∎

Lemma 11.3
.

Assume that
\lambda(h_{1})=1

and
\delta\neq 1
.

Then
V(\lambda,\delta,u)
is not cuspidal.

Proof.

Set
V:=V(\lambda,\delta,u)
.
For
\mathrm{W}(n)
we have proved that

e_{1}.V^{(\lambda-2\epsilon_{1})}=0
. For

\mathrm{S}(n;\gamma)
we only prove that

V^{(\lambda-2\epsilon_{1})}
contains a nonzero vector
w
such that
e_{1}.w=0
.

By Lemma
11.2

we have

w:=(t^{n}\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi_{1}})(t^{m}\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi_{1}}).\mathrm{v}\neq 0

for some
\mathrm{v}\in V^{(\lambda)}
and
n,m\in\mathbb{Z}
. Recall
that
e_{1}=\zeta_{1}\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi_{2}}

and

e_{1}.v=0\hbox{ and }t^{n+m}\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi_{2}}.v=0.

We have

\hskip-162.1807pte_{1}.w=(\zeta_{1}\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi_{2}})(t^{n}\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi_{1}})(t^{m}\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi_{1}}).\mathrm{v}

=[\zeta_{1}\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi_{2}},t^{n}\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi_{1}})](t^{m}\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi_{1}}).\mathrm{v}+(t^{n}\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi_{1}})[\zeta_{1}\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi_{2}},t^{m}\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi_{1}})].\mathrm{v}

=(t^{n}\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi_{2}})(t^{m}\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi_{1}}).\mathrm{v}+(t^{n}\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi_{1}})(t^{m}\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi_{2}}).\mathrm{v}

=(t^{m}\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi_{1}})(t^{n}\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi_{2}}).\mathrm{v}+(t^{n}\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi_{1}})(t^{m}\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi_{2}}).\mathrm{v}

\hskip-182.09763pt=0

Since
w
has weight
\lambda-2\epsilon_{1}
, we have

h_{1}.w=-w
. Thus by elementary
\mathfrak{sl}(2)
-theory, we have

(f_{1})^{l}.w\neq 0
for any
l\geq 0
, which implies that

V
has infinitely many weights. Thus by Lemma

8.1
, the module
V
is not cuspidal.

∎

Corollary 11.4
.

Assume that the
\mathrm{S}(n;\gamma)
-module

V(\lambda,\delta,u)
is cuspidal.
Then

(a)

either
\lambda(h_{1})\geq 2
,

(b)

or
\lambda(h_{1})=1
and
\delta=1
.

Proof.

By Proposition
6
,

\lambda(h_{1})
is an integer
\geq 1
.
If
\lambda(h_{1})=1
, then Condition (b) is
satisfied by Lemma
11.3
. Otherwise
Condition (a) holds.
∎

12
The embedding
K(3)\subset\widehat{\mathrm{K}(4)}\subset\mathrm{CK}(6)

We freely use the notation of Section
2.6
about
\mathrm{CK}(6)
. We recall that the
Dynkin diagram of its subalgebra
\mathfrak{so}(6)\simeq\mathfrak{sl}(4)
is labelled as follows

2
3
1

Consider the element

c=h_{1}+h_{2}+2h_{3}\in\mathrm{CK}(6)
. We observe that
the centralizer subalgebra
C_{\mathrm{CK}(6)}(c)
contains the copy of
\mathrm{Vir}
in
\mathrm{CK}(6)
.

In Section
7.2

we have also defined a specific central extension

\widehat{\mathrm{K}(4)}
of
\mathrm{K}(4)
. Since the restriction of this central extension to
\mathrm{Vir}
splits,

\widehat{\mathrm{K}(4)}
contains a well-defined
copy of
\mathrm{Vir}
.

In this chapter, we prove

Theorem 9
.

There is an isomorphism

\widehat{\mathrm{K}(4)}\simeq C_{\mathrm{CK}(6)}(c).

Consequently there are embeddings

\mathrm{K}(3)\subset\widehat{\mathrm{K}(4)}\subset\mathrm{CK}(6)\text{ and}

\mathrm{K}(3)_{NS}\subset\widehat{\mathrm{K}(4)}_{NS}\subset\mathrm{CK}(6)_{NS}.

All these algebra homomorphisms preserve the subalgebras
\mathrm{Vir}
.

The superconformal algebras
\widehat{\mathrm{K}(4)}
and

C_{\mathrm{CK}(6)}(c)
have conformal dimensions
16
,
so computing their multiplication table would have been very long.
However we will used Jordan theory to reduce the number of computations.
Indeed, it is enough to compare two Jordan superalgebras of conformal dimension
4
.

The proof in the Neveu-Schwarz case is strictly identical to the Ramond case. Thus we will only explain the latter case.

TKK
-construction

This section uses the theory of
Jordan algebras, see
[
20
]
[
44
]
and
[
47
]

for references.
A
TKK
-superalgebra
is a Lie superalgebra
\mathrm{L}

endowed with a
\mathfrak{sl}(2)
-triple

({\bf e},{\bf h},{\bf f})
in
\mathrm{L}_{\overline{0}}
such that
\mathrm{ad}({\bf h})
is
diagonalizable and its eigenvalues are

\pm 2
and
0
. Let

\mathrm{L}=\mathrm{L}^{(-2)}\oplus\mathrm{L}^{(0)}\oplus\mathrm{L}^{(2)}

be the corresponding eigenspace decomposisition.
The space

\mathrm{Jor}(\mathrm{L}):=\mathrm{L}^{(2)}

is a unital Jordan superalgebra for the product

a\circ b:=\frac{1}{2}[a,[{\bf f},b]].

Its unit is the element
{\bf e}\in\mathrm{L}^{(2)}
.

Conversely let
J
be a a unital Jordan superalgebra.
For homogenous elements
a,b
in
J
the operator

\partial_{a,b}:c\in J\mapsto a(cb)-(-1)^{|a||b|}(ac)b

is a derivation. The linear span

\mathrm{Inn}(J)
of these derivations is called the Lie algebra of inner derivations. Then
the space

\mathrm{TKK}(J):=\mathrm{Inn}(J)\oplus\mathfrak{sl}(2)\otimes J

has a structure of Lie superalgebra.
It is called the
TKK-construction
, in reference of the works of J. Tits
[
50
]
, I.M. Kantor

[
28
]
and M. Koecher
[
32
]
.

However the
TKK
-construction

J\mapsto\mathrm{TKK}(J)
is not functorial. For a unital Jordan superalgebra, we write as

\widehat{\mathfrak{sl}_{2}}(J)
the universal central extension of the perfect Lie superalgebra
\mathrm{TKK}(J)
. It is shown in
[
2
]
that

J\mapsto\widehat{\mathfrak{sl}_{2}}(J)

is functorial.

Recall that two perfect Lie suparalgebra

\mathfrak{g}
and
\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}
are called
isogenous
if their
universal central extension are isomorphic.
The following result is well-known:

Lemma 12.1
.

(a)

Let
\mathrm{L}
be a
TKK
-superalgebra. If
L

is generated by
\mathrm{L}^{(\pm 2)}
, the
\mathrm{L}
is a central extension of
\mathrm{TKK}(\mathrm{L}^{(2)})

(b)

Given a Jordan subalgebra
J^{\prime}\subset J
, the subalgebra
\mathrm{TKK}(J^{\prime})
is isogenous to a subalgebra of

\mathrm{TKK}(J)
.

Indeed
\mathrm{K}(4)
and

\mathrm{CK}(6)
are
TKK
-algebras.
We will show that
\mathrm{Jor}(K(4))
is
a subalgebra of
\mathrm{Jor}(\mathrm{CK}(6))
, though

\mathrm{K}(4)
is not a subalgebra of
\mathrm{CK}(6)
. This illustrates Assertion (b) of the previous lemma.

The Lie superalgebra
C_{\mathrm{CK}(6)}(c)\subset\mathrm{CK}(6)

Set
\mathrm{L}=C_{\mathrm{CK}(6)}(c)
. We consider the following
\mathfrak{sl}(2)
-triple
in
\mathrm{CK}(6)

{\bf e}=e_{1}+e_{2},\,{\bf h}=h_{1}+h_{2},\,{\bf f}=f_{1}+f_{2}.

We easily observe that the eigenvalues of the operator

\mathrm{ad}({\bf h})_{\mathrm{CK}(6)}
are
0
and
\pm 2
. Moreover
this
\mathfrak{sl}(2)
-triple belongs to

\mathrm{L}
.

Recall that the space
V
has basis

x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},x_{4}
and that

\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{1}},\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{2}},\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{3}},\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{4}}
is the dual basis.
Then
c
and
{\bf h}
are the diagonal matrices

c=\mathrm{diag}(1,1,-1,-1)
and

{\bf h}=\mathrm{diag}(1,-1,1,-1)
.
Set
V_{+}=\mathbb{C}x_{1}\oplus\mathbb{C}x_{2}
and

V_{-}=\mathbb{C}x_{3}\oplus\mathbb{C}x_{4}

As a vector space
\mathrm{CK}(6)_{0}
is identified with
P(3)\oplus\mathbb{C}D
and more precisely

\mathrm{CK}(6)_{0}\simeq\mathbb{C}D\oplus\wedge^{2}V^{*}\oplus\mathfrak{sl}(V)\oplus S^{2}V.

It follows that

\mathrm{L}_{0}\simeq\mathbb{C}D\oplus V_{+}^{*}\otimes V_{-}^{*}\oplus(\mathfrak{sl}(2)\oplus\mathfrak{sl}(2))\oplus V_{+}\otimes V_{-}.

Indeed the Lie superalgebra
\mathrm{L}_{0}
is a
1
-dimensional central extension of
\mathfrak{sl}(2,2)
.
Set

{\bf e^{\prime}}=e_{2}-e_{1},S=\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{2}}\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{4}},\text{ and }H=x_{1}x_{3}.

Clearly
\mathrm{Jor}(L_{0})=\mathrm{L}_{0}^{(2)}
has basis

{\bf e},{\bf e^{\prime}},S,H
. As a
\mathrm{Vir}
-module, we have

\mathrm{Jor}(\mathrm{L})_{\bar{0}}=\Bigl(\mathbb{C}{\bf e}\oplus\bf{e^{\prime}}\Bigr)\otimes\Omega^{0}\text{and }

\mathrm{Jor}(\mathrm{L})_{\bar{1}}=S\otimes\Omega^{-\frac{1}{2}}\oplus H\otimes\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}}.

It is easy to establish the multiplication table for the superalgebra
\mathrm{Jor}(\mathrm{L})
:

Product

g

\bf{e^{\prime}}(g)

S(g)

H(g)

f

fg

\bf{e^{\prime}}(fg)

S(fg)

H(fg)

\bf{e^{\prime}}(f)

\bf{e^{\prime}}(fg)

fg

H(fD(g))

0

S(f)

\zeta_{2}(fg)

\frac{1}{2}H(fD(g))

\frac{1}{4}(fD(g)-D(f)g))

-\frac{1}{2}{\bf e^{\prime}}(fg)

H(f)

H(fg)

0

\frac{1}{2}{\bf e^{\prime}}(fg)

0

The Jordan algebra of
\mathrm{K}(4)

It is well known how to realize
\mathrm{L}=\mathrm{K}(4)
as a
TKK
-superalgebra, see
[
37
]
.

The
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
-triple is

{\bf e}=\zeta_{2}\xi,{\bf h}=2\,\zeta_{2}\eta_{2},{\bf f}=\,\xi\eta_{2},

where
\xi=\zeta_{1}+\eta_{1}
. Set
\xi^{\prime}=\zeta_{1}-\eta_{1}
and
\zeta_{2}^{*}=\zeta_{2}\zeta_{1}\eta_{1}
.
It is clear that

\mathrm{L}^{(2)}_{\bar{0}}=\Bigl(\mathbb{C}\zeta_{2}\xi\oplus\mathbb{C}\zeta_{2}\xi^{\prime}\Bigr)\otimes\Omega^{0}

\mathrm{L}^{(2)}_{\bar{1}}=\zeta_{2}\otimes\Omega^{-\frac{1}{2}}\oplus\zeta_{2}^{*}\otimes\Omega^{\frac{1}{2}}.

The multiplication table for the superalgebra
\mathrm{Jor}(\mathrm{K}(4))
is:

Product

g

\zeta_{2}\xi^{\prime}(g)

\zeta_{2}(g)

\zeta_{2}^{*}(g)

f

fg

\zeta_{2}\xi^{\prime}(fg)

\zeta_{2}(fg)

\zeta_{2}^{*}(fg)

\zeta_{2}\xi^{\prime}(f)

\zeta_{2}\xi^{\prime}(fg)

fg

\frac{1}{2}\zeta_{2}^{*}(fD(g))

0

\zeta_{2}(f)

\zeta_{2}(fg)

\frac{1}{2}\zeta^{*}_{2}(fD(g))

\frac{1}{4}(fD(g)-D(f)g)

-\frac{1}{2}\zeta_{2}\xi^{\prime}(fg)

\zeta^{*}_{2}(f)

\zeta^{*}(fg)

0

\frac{1}{2}\zeta_{2}\xi^{\prime}(fg)

0

By comparaison of the two multiplication tables, we deduce:

Corollary 12.2
.

The Jordan superalgebras

\mathrm{Jor}(\mathrm{L})
and
\mathrm{Jor}(\mathrm{K}(4))
are isomorphic.

Proof of Theorem
9
.

Lemma 12.3
.

The Lie superalgebras

\widehat{\mathrm{K}(4)}
and
C_{\mathrm{CK}(6)}(c)

are isomorphic.

Proof.

Set
\mathrm{L}=C_{\mathrm{CK}(6)}(c)
.

It is clear that
c
generates
the center of
C_{\mathrm{CK}(6)}(c)
and that

c
lies in
[\mathrm{L}_{0},\mathrm{L}_{0}]
. Hence

\mathrm{L}
is a nontrivial central extension of

C_{\mathrm{CK}(6)}(c)/\mathbb{C}c
with a
1
-dimensional
center.

It is also easy to see that

\mathrm{L}
is generated by

\mathrm{L}^{(\pm 2)}
. Thus by Lemma
12.1
and Corollary
12.2
,

C_{\mathrm{CK}(6)}(c)/\mathbb{C}c
is isomorphic

\mathrm{K}(4)
.

The eigenvalues of
\mathrm{ad}(c)|_{\mathrm{CK}(6)}
are
0
and
\pm 2
. It implies that
\mathrm{CK}(6)
, viewed
as a
C_{\mathrm{CK}(6)}(c)
-module, admits a simple subquotient
with nontrivial central charge. Hence by
Theorem
7
,
C_{\mathrm{CK}(6)}(c)
is isomorphic to the specific central extension

\widehat{\mathrm{K}(4)}
of
\mathrm{K}(4)
.
∎

Lemma 12.4
.

There is an embedding

\mathrm{K}(3)\subset\widehat{\mathrm{K}(4)}

which lifts the natural embedding

\mathrm{K}(3)\subset\mathrm{K}(4)
.

Proof.

By Theorem
7
, all projective cuspidal

\mathrm{K}(3)
-modules have zero central charge. Hence the restriction of the central extension

0\to\mathbb{C}c\to\widehat{\mathrm{K}(4)}\to\mathrm{K}(4)\to 0

to
\mathrm{K}(3)
splits. Therefore

\mathrm{K}(3)
embedds in
\widehat{\mathrm{K}(4)}
.
∎

We now conclude with the proof of
Theorem
9
.

Proof.

The Lemmas
12.4
and
12.3
show the existence of the required embeddings
\mathrm{K}(3)\subset\widehat{\mathrm{K}(4)}\subset\mathrm{CK}(6)
.

It remains to prove that these superconformal
algebras share the same subalgebra
\mathrm{Vir}
.
This statement is clear for

K(3)
and
\widehat{\mathrm{K}(4)}
. We also
observe that the copy of
\mathrm{Vir}
in

\widehat{\mathrm{K}(4)}
and
\mathrm{CK}(6)
are respectively the linear span of the sets

\{[\zeta_{2}(t^{n}),\zeta_{2}(t^{m})]\mid n,m\in\mathbb{Z}\}

\{[S(t^{n}),S(t^{m})]\mid n,m\in\mathbb{Z}\}.

Thus
\widehat{\mathrm{K}(4)}
and
\mathrm{CK}(6)
share the same
copy of
\mathrm{Vir}
.

∎

13
Exceptional
1
-parameter families of cuspidal modules over
\mathrm{K}(3)
,
\widehat{\mathrm{K}(4)}
and
\mathrm{CK}(6)
.

We label the Dynkin diagram of the
simple Lie algebra
\mathfrak{so}(6)\simeq\mathfrak{sl}(4)\subset\mathrm{CK}(6)
as
