The Dynkin diagram of

\mathfrak{so}(4)\subset\widehat{\mathrm{K}(4)}
consists of the vertices
1
and
2
and the Dynkin diagram
of
\mathfrak{so}(3)\subset\mathrm{K}(3)
is the first vertex.

In this chapter, we investigate some one-parameter families of modules over the superconformal algebras
\mathrm{K}(3)
,
\widehat{\mathrm{K}(4)}
and
\mathrm{CK}(6)
,
namely

(a)

the
\mathrm{K}(3)
-modules

S(u):=V(\omega_{1},\frac{1}{4},u)
,

(b)

the
\widehat{\mathrm{K}(4)}
-modules

S^{+}(u):=V((\omega_{1},1),\frac{1}{4},u),\,S^{-}(u):=V((\omega_{1},-1),\frac{3}{4},u)\text{ and}

(c)

the
\mathrm{CK}(6)
-modules
T(u):=V(\omega_{1},\frac{1}{4},u)
.

where in each case
\omega_{1}
denotes the first fundamental weight. For
\widehat{\mathrm{K}(4)}
, we write

(\omega_{1},\pm 1)
for the weights

(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\lambda_{c})
with

\lambda_{1}=\lambda_{2}=\frac{1}{2}
and
\lambda_{c}=\pm 1
.

Recall that
\mathrm{K}(3)\subset\widehat{\mathrm{K}(4)}\subset\mathrm{CK}(6)
, see Theorem
9
.

Theorem 10
.

Let
u\in\mathbb{C}
.

(a)

The
\mathrm{CK}(6)
-module
T(u)
has
conformal dimension
8
.

(b)

The
\mathrm{K}(4)
-modules
S^{\pm}(u)
and the

\mathrm{K}(3)
-module
S(u)
have conformal dimension
4
.

(c)

As a
\widehat{\mathrm{K}(4)}
-module we have

T(u)=S^{+}(u)\oplus S^{-}(u),\text{ and}

(d)

as
\mathrm{K}(3)
-modules,

S^{\pm}(u)\simeq S(u).

If we take into account the parity,
Assertion (b) is
T(u)=S^{+}(u)\oplus\Pi\,S^{-}(u)

and Assertion (c) is
S^{-}(u)\simeq\Pi S(u)
,
where
\Pi
is the parity change functor.
The result holds for the Neveu-Schwarz forms.
Since the statement and its proof is the same,
we will skip it.

The modules
T(u)
and
S(u)
where considered in

[
37
]
. For
\mathrm{CK}(6)
, the parameter

\delta=\frac{1}{4}
is different, because of
a different embedding of

\mathrm{Vir}
.

Proof of Theorem
10

Recall that
V
denotes a vector space with basis

x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},x_{4}
.
Let
{\bf V}
be the superspace

{\bf V}_{\bar{0}}=V\text{ and }{\bf V}_{\bar{0}}=V^{*}.

In Section
2.6
, the superconformal
algebra
CK(6)
is defined as a subalgebra
of
\mathrm{End}_{\mathbb{C}}({\bf W})
, where

\bf{W}:={\bf V}\otimes\mathbb{C}[t,t^{-1}]
. The superalgebra
\mathrm{CK}(6)
acts on
{\bf W}
by
differential operators of order
\leq 1
. Thus

t^{u}{\bf W}
is a well-defined
\mathrm{CK}(6)
-module
for any
u\in\mathbb{C}
.

Lemma 13.1
.

As a
\mathrm{CK}(6)
-module, we have

t^{u}{\bf W}\simeq V(\omega_{1},\frac{1}{4},u).

Proof.

The highest weight of

V
and
V^{*}
are respectively

\omega_{1}=\frac{\epsilon_{1}+\epsilon_{2}+\epsilon_{3}}{2}\text{ and }\omega_{3}=\frac{-\epsilon_{1}+\epsilon_{2}+\epsilon_{3}}{2}.

Hence the highest weight of
\bf{W}
is
\omega_{1}
.
By definition, we have

(t^{u}{\bf W})_{\bar{0}}\simeq V\otimes\Omega_{u}^{\frac{1}{4}}.

It is easy to show that the
\mathrm{CK}(6)
-module

t^{u}{\bf W}
is simple, thus

t^{u}{\bf W}\simeq V(\omega_{1},\frac{1}{4},u).

∎

Lemma 13.2
.

As a
\widehat{\mathrm{K}(4)}
-module, we have

t^{u}{\bf W}\simeq V((\omega_{1},1),\frac{1}{4},u)\oplus V((\omega_{1},-1),\frac{1}{4},u).

Proof.

Set
V_{+}=\mathbb{C}x_{1}\oplus\mathbb{C}x_{2}
,

V_{-}=\mathbb{C}x_{3}\oplus\mathbb{C}x_{4}
,

\bf{V_{+}}=V_{+}\oplus V_{-}^{*}\text{ and }\bf{V_{-}}=V_{-}\oplus V_{+}^{*}.

As a
\widehat{\mathrm{K}(4)}
-module,
{\bf W}
decomposes
as

{\bf W}={\bf W_{+}}\oplus{\bf W_{-}}

where
{\bf W_{+}}={\bf V_{+}}\otimes\mathbb{C}[t,t^{-1}]

and
{\bf W_{-}}={\bf V_{-}}\otimes\mathbb{C}[t,t^{-1}]
.
As
\mathfrak{so}(4)
-modules,

\bf{V_{+}}\simeq\bf{V_{+}}^{*}

and its highest weight is
\omega_{1}=\epsilon_{1}+\epsilon_{2}
. Similarly
\bf{V_{-}}\simeq\bf{V_{-}}^{*}

and its highest weight is the second fundamental weight
\omega_{2}=-\epsilon_{1}+\epsilon_{2}
. Thus the highest weight
of
{\bf W}_{\pm}
is
\omega_{1}

Since

h_{1}+h_{2}+2h_{3}=\mathrm{diag}(1,1,-1,-1),

the central element
c
acts as
1
on

\bf{V_{+}}
and
-1
on
\bf{V_{+}}^{*}
.

As a
\mathrm{Vir}
-module we have

t^{u}{\bf W}_{\bar{0}}\simeq V\otimes\Omega^{\frac{1}{4}}_{u}\text{ and }t^{u}{\bf W}_{\bar{1}}\simeq V^{*}\otimes\Omega^{\frac{3}{4}}_{u}.

By the previous considerations, it is easy to identify

t^{u}{\bf W}_{+}\simeq V((\omega_{1},1),\frac{1}{4},u)\text{ and }t^{u}{\bf W}_{-}\simeq V((\omega_{1},-1),\frac{3}{4},u),

which proves the lemma.
∎

Lemma 13.3
.

As a
\mathrm{K}(3)
-module,

S^{\pm}(u)
are isomorphic to
V(\omega_{1},\frac{1}{4},u)
.

Proof.

The highest weight of the
\mathfrak{so}(3)
-modules,
V_{\pm}
and
V_{\pm}^{*}
is
\omega_{1}
.
As a
\mathrm{Vir}
-module, there is an isomorphisms

S^{\pm}(u)^{\omega_{1}}\simeq\Omega_{u}^{\frac{1}{4}}\oplus\Omega_{u}^{\frac{3}{4}}.

Thus it follows from Section
4.4
that

S^{\pm}(u)^{\omega_{1}}\simeq{\mathrm{Tens}}(\omega_{1},\frac{1}{4},u)

as
\mathrm{K}(1)\ltimes\mathbb{C}[t,t^{-1},\xi]
-modules, which proves the lemma.
∎

It is now easy to deduce Theorem
10

Proof.

Assertion (c) and (d) follow from
Lemmas
13.1
,
13.2
and
13.3
.
Moreover the formula for the conformal dimensions of the explicit description of the modules.
∎

14
Cuspidal
\widehat{\mathrm{K}(4)}
-modules

Recall that we denote as
\widehat{\mathrm{K}(4)}
the
specific central extension

0\to\mathbb{C}c\to\widehat{\mathrm{K}(4)}\to\mathrm{K}(4)\to 0

defined in Chapter
7
.

The set of quadratic elements

\mathcal{Q}=\{\zeta_{1}\zeta_{2},\eta_{1}\eta_{2}\}\cup\{\zeta_{i}\eta_{j}\mid 1\leq i,j\leq 2\}

form a basis of the Lie subalgebra

\mathfrak{so}(4)\subset\mathrm{K}(4)
. Its inverse image
in
\widehat{\mathrm{K}(4)}
is the split central extension

\widehat{\mathfrak{so}(4)}=\mathfrak{so}(4)\oplus\mathbb{C}c.

The Cartan subalgebra of
\widehat{\mathfrak{so}(4)}
is

H=\mathbb{C}\zeta_{1}\eta_{1}\oplus\mathbb{C}\zeta_{2}\eta_{2}\oplus\mathbb{C}c,

Thus a weight
\lambda\in H^{*}
is written as a triple

(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\lambda_{c})
where

\lambda_{1}=\lambda(\zeta_{1}\eta_{1}),\lambda_{2}=\lambda(\zeta_{2}\eta_{2})\hbox{ and }\lambda_{c}=\lambda(c).

Recall that a weight
\lambda
is
dominant

if
\lambda_{1}
and
\lambda_{2}
are simultaneously
integers or half-integers and

\lambda_{2}+\lambda_{1}\geq 0\hbox{ and }\lambda_{2}-\lambda_{1}\geq 0.

In the chapter we prove the following result:

Theorem 11
.

Let

(\lambda,\delta,u)\in H^{*}\times\mathbb{C}\times\mathbb{C},

be an arbitrary triple with
\lambda
dominant.
The
\widehat{\mathrm{K}(4)}
-module

V(\lambda,\delta,u)

is cuspidal if and only if

(a)

either
\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2}\geq 2

that is
\lambda(h_{1})\geq 2
,

(b)

or

\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2}=1
,
\delta=\frac{1-\lambda_{2}}{2}
and

\lambda_{c}=2\lambda_{2}
,

(c)

or

\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2}=1
,
\delta=\frac{1+\lambda_{2}}{2}
and

\lambda_{c}=-2\lambda_{2}
.

The family of
\widehat{\mathrm{K}(4)}
-modules satisfying Condition (b) or (c) are in duality.
More precisely the graded dual of

V((\lambda_{2},1-\lambda_{2},2\lambda_{2}),\frac{1-\lambda_{2}}{2},u)

is the
\widehat{\mathrm{K}(4)}
-module

V((\lambda_{2},1-\lambda_{2},-2\lambda_{2}),\frac{1+\lambda_{2}}{2},-u).

Set
V:=V(\lambda,\delta,u)
.
The pattern of the proof of Theorem
11
is more complicated than
the proof of analogous results for the other superconformal algebras.

As usual, we use Proposition
4.7
to show that
Condition (a) is sufficient. Then we use explicit computations to
determine for which triples
(\lambda,\delta,u)

V^{(\lambda-2\epsilon_{1})}=0.

We deduce the necessity of (a), (b) or (c) for the cuspidality of
V
. Using the involution
\tau

of the Dynkin diagramm of
\mathfrak{so}(4)
we
also deduce that
Condition (b) or Condition (c) is sufficient when
\lambda_{2}\geq 1
. The remaining two families, namely

\lambda_{1}=\lambda_{2}=1/2,\delta=1/4,\lambda_{c}=2\hbox{ and }

\lambda_{1}=\lambda_{2}=1/2,\delta=3/4,\lambda_{c}=-2

are cuspidal by results of Chapter
10
.

Sufficiency of Condition (a)

Set
\mathrm{L}=\mathrm{K}(4)
and
\hat{\mathrm{L}}=\widehat{\mathrm{K}(4)}
.
We write
\hat{\mathrm{L}}^{\bf(1)}
for the subalgebra

\widehat{\mathrm{K}(4)^{\bf(1)}}
defined in Section

7.3
.
The space

T:=\mathrm{L}/\mathrm{L}^{\bf(1)}.

is called the
tangent space
. As a

\mathrm{L}^{\bf(1)}
-module
T
is a nonsplit extension

0\to T_{\overline{1}}\to T\to T_{\overline{0}}\to 0.

Thus we define the ideal

\mathrm{L}^{\bf(2)}\subset\mathrm{L}^{\bf(1)}
by

\mathrm{L}^{\bf(2)}=\{\partial\in\mathrm{L}^{\bf(1)}\mid\partial T_{\overline{0}}\subset T_{\overline{1}}\hbox{ and }\partial T_{\overline{1}}=0\}.

It is easy to verify that

\mathrm{L}^{\bf(1)}=\Bigl(\mathfrak{so}(4)\oplus(t-1)D\Bigr)\ltimes\mathrm{L}^{\bf(2)}.

Let
\hat{\mathrm{L}}^{\bf(1)}
be the inverse image of

\mathrm{L}^{\bf(1)}
in
\hat{\mathrm{L}}
. The central
extension

0\to\mathbb{C}c\to\hat{\mathrm{L}}^{\bf(1)}\to\mathrm{L}^{\bf(1)}\to 0

splits, thus
\mathrm{L}^{(2)}
can be viewed as a subalgebra
of
\hat{\mathrm{L}}^{\bf(1)}
. We have

\hat{\mathrm{L}}^{\bf(1)}=\Bigl(\mathfrak{so}(4)\oplus\mathbb{C}c\oplus\mathbb{C}(t-1)D\Bigr)\ltimes\mathrm{L}^{\bf(2)}.

For an arbitrary pair
\lambda,\delta
,
let
S(\lambda,\delta)
be the

\hat{\mathrm{L}}^{\bf(1)}
-module defined as follows

(a)

The action of
\mathrm{L}^{\bf(2)}
is trivial,
so
S(\lambda,\delta)
is a

\Bigl(\mathfrak{so}(4)\oplus\mathbb{C}c\oplus\mathbb{C}(t-1)D\Bigr)
-module.

(b)

As a
\mathfrak{so}(4)
-module
S(\lambda,\delta)

is the simple module with highest weight

(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})
,

(c)

the central elements
c
and
(t-1)D
acts
respectively as
\lambda_{c}
and
\delta
.

As a consequence of Proposition
4.7
we obtain

Corollary 14.1
.

Let

(\lambda,\delta,u)\in H^{*}\times\mathbb{C}\times\mathbb{C},

be an arbitrary triple with
\lambda
dominant. If

\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2}\geq 2\hbox{ that is }\lambda(h_{1})\geq 2,

the
\widehat{\mathrm{K}(4)}
-module

V(\lambda,\delta,u)

is cuspidal.

Proof.

We check the hypothesis of Proposition
4.7

and we compute the datum
(\omega,m,\delta^{-})
.
Here the grading element
\ell_{0}
is
D
and the
tangent space
T
has basis

\{D,\zeta_{1},\eta_{1},\zeta_{2},\eta_{2}\}\hbox{ modulo }\mathrm{L}^{\bf(1)}.

Thus

T^{-}=\mathbb{C}\eta_{1}\oplus\mathbb{C}\eta_{2},\omega=\epsilon_{1}+\epsilon_{2}\hbox{ and }m=2.

Since
[(t-1)D,\eta_{i}]=-\frac{1}{2}\eta_{i}

we have
\delta^{-}=-\frac{1}{2}
.

We have
\omega(h_{1})=2
and
\omega(h_{2})=0
.
Since the weight
\mu:=\lambda-\omega
is dominant,
the simple
\hat{L}^{\bf(1)}
-module

S(\mu,\delta+1)
is finite dimensional. By proposition

4.7
, the highest weight of the
\hat{L}
-module

M:=\mathcal{F}(S(\mu,\delta+1),u)
is
\lambda=\mu+\omega
and

M^{(\lambda)}\simeq{\mathrm{Tens}}(\lambda,\delta,u).

Hence
V(\lambda,\delta,u)
is a subquotient of

M
, which proves that
V(\lambda,\delta,u)
is cuspidal.
∎

Explicit computations

As before, we will use multidistributions
to write some series of identities as a single
identity.

Let
v
be a formal variable. For an element

a\in\widehat{{\mathrm{Grass}}}(4)
, set

a(v):=\sum_{n}a(t^{n})\,v^{n}\in\widehat{{\mathrm{Grass}}}(4)[[v,v^{-1}]].

To avoid ambiguities, the unit element will be
denoted as
D
and we set

D(v)=\sum_{n}t^{n}D\,v^{n}.

We now describe some identities of
formal distributions of
\widehat{\mathrm{K}(4)}
.
Set
\zeta_{1}^{*}=\zeta_{1}\zeta_{2}\eta_{2}

and
\eta_{1}^{*}=\eta_{1}\zeta_{2}\eta_{2}
.
In the following formulas,
v,w,x,y
and
z
are formal variables and
D_{v},D_{w}\cdots
denotes the Ramond derivations
\frac{u\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}u},\frac{v\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}v}\cdots
.

[D(v),\eta_{1}(w)]=(D_{w}-\frac{1}{2}D_{v})\eta_{1}(v,w)

[\zeta_{1}(v),\eta_{1}(w)]=D(v,w)+1/2(D_{w}-D_{v})\zeta_{1}\eta_{1}(v,w)

[\zeta_{2}\eta_{2}(v),\eta_{1}(w)]=-1/2D_{v}\,\eta_{1}^{*}(v,w)

[\zeta_{1}\eta_{1}(v),\eta_{1}(w)]=-\eta_{1}(v,w)

[\zeta_{1}^{*}(v),\eta_{1}(w)]=-(1/2)c(v,w)+\zeta_{2}\eta_{2}(v,w)

[c(v),\eta_{1}(w)]=-D_{v}\eta_{1}^{*}(v,w)

We easily deduce the following additional formulas:

[\zeta_{1}(v),[\zeta_{1}(w),\eta_{1}(x)]]=(D_{w}-D_{v})\zeta_{1}(v,w,x)

[\zeta_{1}(v),[\zeta_{1}^{*}(w),\eta_{1}(x)]]=(D_{w}+D_{x})\zeta_{1}^{*}(v,w,x)

[\zeta_{1}^{*}(v),[\zeta_{1}^{*}(w),\eta_{1}(x)]]=0

Let
(\lambda,\delta,u)\in H^{*}\times\mathbb{C}\times\mathbb{C},

be an arbitrary triple. Set
V:=V(\lambda,\delta,u)
. We always assume that
\lambda\neq 0
. Therefore

V^{(\lambda)}
is a
\mathbb{C}[t,t^{-1}]
-module.
Thus for
\mathrm{v}\in V^{(\lambda)}_{0}
we set

\mathrm{v}(z)=\sum_{n}\,\mathrm{v}(t^{n})z^{n}.

Set
\Delta_{z}=D_{z}+u
. Since
V^{(\lambda)}={\mathrm{Tens}}(\lambda,\delta,u)
, we have

D(v)\,\mathrm{v}(z)=(\delta D_{v}+\Delta_{z})\,\mathrm{v}(v,z)

\zeta_{1}\eta_{1}(v)\,\mathrm{v}(z)=\lambda_{1}\,\mathrm{v}(v,z)

\zeta_{2}\eta_{2}(v)\,\mathrm{v}(z)=\lambda_{2}\,\mathrm{v}(v,z)

c(v)\,\mathrm{v}(z)=\lambda_{c}\,\mathrm{v}(v,z)

Set
{\tilde{\mathrm{v}}}=\mathrm{v}(v,w,x,y,z)
.

Lemma 14.2
.

We have

(a)\hskip 2.84526pt\zeta_{1}(v)\,\zeta_{1}(w)\,\eta_{1}(x)\,\eta_{1}(y)\,\mathrm{v}(z)=(\delta-1+\frac{\lambda_{1}}{2})(\delta-\frac{\lambda_{1}}{2})(D_{v}-D_{w})(D_{x}-D_{y})\,{\tilde{\mathrm{v}}}

(b)\hskip 2.84526pt\zeta_{1}(v)\,\zeta_{1}^{*}(w)\,\eta_{1}(x)\,\eta_{1}(y)\,\mathrm{v}(z)=(\lambda_{2}-\frac{\lambda_{c}}{2})(\delta-1+\frac{\lambda_{1}}{2})(D_{y}-D_{x})\,{\tilde{\mathrm{v}}}

(c)\hskip 2.84526pt\zeta_{1}^{*}(v)\,\zeta_{1}^{*}(w)\,\eta_{1}(x)\,\eta_{1}(y)\,\mathrm{v}(z)=0

(d)\hskip 2.84526pt\zeta_{1}(v)\,\zeta_{1}(w)\,\eta_{1}(x)\,\eta_{1}^{*}(y)\,\mathrm{v}(z)=(\delta-\frac{\lambda_{1}}{2})(\lambda_{2}+\frac{\lambda_{c}}{2})(D_{w}-D_{v})\,{\tilde{\mathrm{v}}}

(e)\hskip 2.84526pt\zeta_{1}(v)\,\zeta_{1}^{*}(w)\,\eta_{1}(x)\,\eta_{1}^{*}(y)\,\mathrm{v}(z)=(\lambda_{2}+\frac{\lambda_{c}}{2})(\lambda_{2}-\frac{\lambda_{c}}{2})\,{\tilde{\mathrm{v}}}.

(f)\hskip 2.84526pt\zeta_{1}^{*}(v)\,\zeta_{1}^{*}(w)\,\eta_{1}(x)\,\eta_{1}^{*}(y)\,\mathrm{v}(z)=0

(g)\hskip 2.84526pt\zeta_{1}(v)\,\zeta_{1}(w)\,\eta_{1}^{*}(x)\,\eta_{1}^{*}(y)\,\mathrm{v}(z)=0

(h)\hskip 2.84526pt\zeta_{1}(v)\,\zeta_{1}^{*}(w)\,\eta_{1}^{*}(x)\,\eta_{1}^{*}(y)\,\mathrm{v}(z)=0

(k)\hskip 2.84526pt\zeta_{1}^{*}(v)\,\zeta_{1}^{*}(w)\,\eta_{1}^{*}(x)\,\eta_{1}^{*}(y)\,\mathrm{v}(z)=0

Proof.
Set
\hat{\mathrm{L}}=\widehat{\mathrm{K}(4)}
.
Let
A_{1},A_{2}
be distributions in
\mathrm{L}^{(\epsilon_{1})}
and

B_{1},B_{2}
be distributions in
\mathrm{L}^{(-\epsilon_{1})}
. Since the distribution

[A_{1},[A_{2},B_{2}]]\mathrm{v}
has weight

\lambda+\epsilon_{1}
, we have
[A_{1},[A_{2},B_{2}]]\mathrm{v}=0
.
We deduce the formula

A_{1}A_{2}B_{1}B_{2}\mathrm{v}=A+B-C

where

A:=[A_{1},[A_{2},B_{1}]B_{2}\,\mathrm{v},

B:=[A_{2},B_{1}][A_{1},B_{2}]\,\mathrm{v},\hbox{ and }

C:=[A_{1},B_{1}][A_{2},B_{2}])\,\mathrm{v}

which will be used in the next computations.

When
A_{1}(v),A_{2}(w),B_{1}(x)
and
B_{2}(y)
are the formal distributions of Formulas (1-9), it is clear
that

A_{1}(v)A_{2}(w)B_{1}(x)B_{2}(y).\mathrm{v}(x)=P(D_{v},D_{w},D_{x},D_{y},\Delta_{z};\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\lambda_{c},\delta).\tilde{\mathrm{v}}

where
P
is a degree
4
polynomial which of degree
\leq 2
over the first variables
(D_{u},D_{v},D_{w},D_{x},\Delta_{z})

and over the last variables
(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\lambda_{c})
.

Proof of Formula (a).

\zeta_{1}(v),\zeta_{1}(w)\,\eta_{1}(x)\,\eta_{1}(y)\,\mathrm{v}(z)=A+B-C

where

A=[\zeta_{1}(v),[\zeta_{1}(w),\eta_{1}(x)]]\,\eta_{1}(y)\,\mathrm{v}(z),

B=[\zeta_{1}(w),\eta_{1}(x)]\,[\zeta_{1}(v),\eta_{1}(y)]\,\mathrm{v}(z),

and

C=[\zeta_{1}(v),\eta_{1}(x)]\,[\zeta_{1}(w),\eta_{1}(y)]\,\mathrm{v}(z).

We successively compute the terms
A
,
B
and
C
.
We have

[\zeta_{1}(v),[\zeta_{1}(w),\eta_{1}(x)]]=(D_{w}-D_{v})\zeta_{1}(v,w,x).

Since

[\zeta_{1}(v,w,z),\eta_{1}(y)]=D(v,w,x,y)+1/2(D_{y}-D_{v}-D_{w}-D_{x})\zeta_{1}\eta_{1}(v,w,x,y)

we deduce

A=\left(D_{w}-D_{v}\right)\left((\delta+\frac{\lambda_{1}}{2})D_{y}+(\delta-\frac{\lambda_{1}}{2})(D_{v}+D_{w}+D_{x})+\Delta_{z}\right)\,{\tilde{\mathrm{v}}}

We have

B=(D(w,x)+\frac{1}{2}(D_{x}-D_{w})\zeta_{1}\eta_{1}(w,x))(D(v,y)+\frac{1}{2}(D_{y}-D_{v})\zeta_{1}\eta_{1}(v,y))\,\tilde{\mathrm{v}}

=\Bigl((\delta-\frac{\lambda_{1}}{2})D_{w}+(\delta+\frac{\lambda_{1}}{2})D_{x}+D_{v}+D_{y}+\Delta_{z}\Bigr)\Bigl((\delta-\frac{\lambda_{1}}{2})D_{v}+(\delta+\frac{\lambda_{1}}{2})D_{y}+\Delta_{z}\Bigr)\,\tilde{\mathrm{v}}.

We obtain
C
from the previous formula by exchanging
the roles of
v
and
w
, thus
C
equals

\Bigl((\delta-\frac{\lambda_{1}}{2})D_{v}+(\delta+\frac{\lambda_{1}}{2})D_{x}+D_{v}+D_{y}+\Delta_{z}\Bigr)\Bigl((\delta-\frac{\lambda_{1}}{2})D_{w}+(\delta+\frac{\lambda_{1}}{2})D_{y}+\Delta_{z}\Bigr)\,\tilde{\mathrm{v}}.

Since

[\zeta_{1}(v),\zeta_{1}(w)=[\eta_{1}(x),\eta_{1}(y)]=0

the left side is skew symmetric in
v,w
and in

x,y
. Therefore the right side is divisible by

(D_{v}-D_{w})(D_{x}-D_{y})
so it will be of the form

f(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\lambda_{c},\delta)(D_{v}-D_{w})(D_{x}-D_{y})\,\tilde{\mathrm{v}}.

So it is enough to consider the contribution
of the monomial
D_{v}D_{x}
in
A

B
and
C
.
These contributions are respectively

-(\delta-\lambda_{1}/2)D_{v}D_{x}\,\tilde{\mathrm{v}},\,\,(\delta+\frac{\lambda_{1}}{2})(\delta-\frac{\lambda_{1}}{2})D_{v}D_{x}\,\tilde{\mathrm{v}}\hbox{ and }0.

Thus the contribution
of the monomial
D_{v}D_{x}
in
A+B-C
is

(\delta+\lambda_{1}/2-1)(\delta-\lambda_{1}/2)D_{v}D_{x}\,\tilde{\mathrm{v}},

which proves Formula (a).∎

Proof of Formula (b).

Similarly we have

\zeta_{1}(v)|,\zeta_{1}^{*}(w),\eta_{1}(x)|,\eta_{1}(y)|,\mathrm{v}(z)=A+B-C

where

A=[\zeta_{1}(v),[\zeta_{1}^{*}(w),\eta_{1}(x)]]\,\eta_{1}(y)\,\mathrm{v}(z),

B=[\zeta_{1}^{*}(w),\eta_{1}(x)]\,[\zeta_{1}(v),\eta_{1}(y)]\,\mathrm{v}(z),

and

C=[\zeta_{1}(v),\eta_{1}(x)]\,[\zeta_{1}^{*}(w),\eta_{1}(y)]\,\mathrm{v}(z)
.

We now compute
A
,
B
and
C
. We have

A=(D_{w}+D_{x})\zeta_{1}^{*}(v,w,x)\,\eta_{1}(y)\,\mathrm{v}(z).

Since

[\zeta_{1}^{*}(v,w,x),\eta_{1}(y)]=-1/2c(v,w,x,y)+\zeta_{2}\eta_{2}(v,w,x,y)

we deduce

A=\left(\lambda_{2}-\frac{\lambda_{c}}{2}\right)(D_{w}+D_{x}){\tilde{\mathrm{v}}}.

We have

B=\Bigl(-\frac{1}{2}c(w,x)+\zeta_{2}\eta_{2}(w,x)\Bigr)\Bigl(D(v,y)+\frac{1}{2}(D_{y}-D_{v})\zeta_{1}\eta_{1}(v,y)\Bigr)\,\mathrm{v}(z)

\hskip-59.75078pt=\Bigl(\lambda_{2}-\frac{\lambda_{c}}{2}\Bigr)\Bigl(\delta(D_{v}+D_{y})+\Delta_{z}+\frac{\lambda_{1}}{2}(D_{y}-D_{v})\Big)\,{\tilde{\mathrm{v}}}.

Thus

B=\Bigl(\lambda_{2}-\frac{\lambda_{c}}{2}\Bigr)\Bigl((\delta-\frac{\lambda_{1}}{2})D_{v}+(\delta+\frac{\lambda_{1}}{2})D_{y}+\Delta_{z}\Bigr)\,{\tilde{\mathrm{v}}}

Since

[\zeta_{1}^{*}(w),\eta_{1}(y)]\,\mathrm{v}(z)=(\lambda_{2}-\frac{\lambda_{c}}{2})\,\mathrm{v}(w,y,z)

we deduce

C=(\lambda_{2}-\frac{\lambda_{c}}{2})[\zeta_{1}(v),\eta_{1}(x)]\,\mathrm{v}(w,y,z)

=(\lambda_{2}-\frac{\lambda_{c}}{2})\Bigl(D(v,x)+\frac{1}{2}(D_{v}-D_{x})\,\zeta_{1}\eta_{1}(v,x)\Bigr)\,\mathrm{v}(w,y,z).

=(\lambda_{2}-(1/2)\lambda_{c})\Bigl(\delta(D_{v}+D_{x})+(D_{w}+D_{y}+\Delta_{z})+\frac{\lambda_{1}}{2}(D_{u}-D_{w})\Bigr)\,{\tilde{\mathrm{v}}}.

Thus

C=\left(\lambda_{2}-\frac{\lambda_{c}}{2}\right)\Bigl((\delta-\lambda_{1}/2)D_{v}+(\delta+\lambda_{1}/2)D_{x}+D_{w}+D_{y}+\Delta_{z}\Bigr)\,{\tilde{\mathrm{v}}}

We deduce

A+B-C=(\lambda_{2}-\frac{\lambda_{c}}{2})(\delta-1+\lambda_{1}/2)(D_{y}-D_{v})\,{\tilde{\mathrm{v}}},

which prove Formula (b).
∎

Proof of Formula (c).

We decompose again the formula into three pieces
A
,
B
and
C
.

Since

[\zeta_{1}^{*}(v),[\zeta_{1}^{*}(w),\eta_{1}(x)]]=0

we have
A=0
.

Moreover

\displaystyle B

\displaystyle=[\zeta_{1}^{*}(w),\eta_{1}(x)][\zeta_{1}^{*}(v),\eta_{1}(y)]\,\mathrm{v}(z)

\displaystyle=(1/2c(w,x)+\zeta_{2}\eta_{2}(w,x))(1/2c(v,y)+\zeta_{2}\eta_{2}(v,y))\,\mathrm{v}(z)

\displaystyle=(\frac{1}{2}\lambda_{c}+\lambda_{2})^{2}\,\tilde{\mathrm{v}}

Since
C
can be deduced from
B
by symmetry,
we deduce that
B=C
, thus
A+B-C=0

which proves Formula (c).

Proof of Formula (d).
As before,
we decompose the left side of Formula (d) as
A+B-C
.
We have

\displaystyle A

\displaystyle=[\zeta_{1}(v),[\zeta_{1}(w),\eta_{1}(x)]]\eta_{1}^{*}(y)\,\mathrm{v}(z)

\displaystyle=(D_{w}-D_{v})\zeta_{1}(v,w,x)\eta_{1}^{*}(y)\,\mathrm{v}(z)

\displaystyle=(D_{w}-D_{v})(\zeta_{2}(v,w,x,y)+1/2c(v,w,x,y))\,\mathrm{v}(z)

Thus

A=(\lambda_{2}+\frac{\lambda_{c}}{2})(D_{w}-D_{v})\,\tilde{\mathrm{v}}.

\displaystyle B

\displaystyle=[\zeta_{1}(w),\eta_{1}(x)][\zeta_{1}(v),\eta_{1}^{*}(y)]\,\mathrm{v}(z)

\displaystyle=(D(w,x)+1/2(D_{x}-D_{v})\zeta_{1}\eta_{1}(w,x))(\zeta_{2}\eta_{2}(v,y)+1/2c(v,y))\mathrm{v}(z)

\displaystyle=(\lambda_{2}+\frac{\lambda_{c}}{2})\Bigl(D_{v}+(\delta-\frac{\lambda_{1}}{2})D_{w}+(\delta+\frac{\lambda_{1}}{2})D_{x}+D_{y}+\Delta_{z}\Bigr).\tilde{\mathrm{v}}.

Exchanging
v
and
w
, we obtain

C=(\lambda_{2}+\frac{\lambda_{c}}{2})\Bigl((\delta-\frac{\lambda_{1}}{2})D_{v}+D_{w}+(\delta+\frac{\lambda_{1}}{2})D_{x}+D_{y}+\Delta_{z}\Bigr).\tilde{\mathrm{v}}.

Thus

A+B-C=(\lambda_{2}+\lambda_{c}/2)(\delta-\lambda_{1}/2)(D_{w}-D_{v})\,\tilde{\mathrm{v}},

which proves Formula (d). ∎

Proof of Formula (e)

We have
[\zeta_{1}^{*}(w)\eta_{1}^{*}(x)]=0
. It follows that

\zeta_{1}(v)\zeta_{1}^{*}(w)\eta_{1}^{*}(x)\eta_{1}(y)\,\mathrm{v}(z)=-[\zeta_{1}(v)\eta_{1}^{*}(w)][\zeta_{1}^{*}(x)\eta_{1}(y)]\,\mathrm{v}(z).

Thus

\zeta_{1}(v)\zeta_{1}^{*}(w)\eta_{1}^{*}(x)\eta_{1}(y)\,v(z)=-(\lambda_{2}+\lambda_{c}/2)(\lambda_{2}-\lambda_{c}/2)\,{\tilde{v}}.

∎

Moreover, Formulas
(f),(g),(h)
and
(k)
are easy. Thus the proof is complete. ∎

Remark
Since
\mathrm{L}_{NS}:=\widehat{\mathrm{K}_{NS}(4)}
is isomorphic
to the Ramond type contact superalgebra
\mathrm{L}:=\widehat{\mathrm{K}(4)}
, all results
concerning
\widehat{\mathrm{K}(4)}
can be reformulated for

\widehat{\mathrm{K}_{NS}(4)}
. Instead of describing an explicit isomorphism and the way to reformulate results, it is simpler to consider that
\mathrm{L}_{NS}
is defined as in Section
2.2
as

(\mathrm{L}_{NS})_{\bar{0}}=\mathrm{L}_{\bar{0}}\hbox{ and }(\mathrm{L}_{NS})_{\bar{1}}=t^{\frac{1}{2}}\mathrm{L}_{\bar{1}}.

Then identities concerning
\mathrm{L}_{NS}
are just
polynomial extensions of identities in
\mathrm{L}
,
where the distributions
a(v)
are defined
as in Section
9.5
.

We now state as a corollary the main consequence of these tedious computations.
As before let
(\lambda,\delta,u)\in H^{*}\times\mathbb{C}\times\mathbb{C},

be an arbitrary triple and set
V:=V(\lambda,\delta,u)
. For simplicity we assume that
\lambda\neq 0
. We obtain:

Corollary 14.3
.

We have

V^{(\lambda-2\epsilon_{1})}=0

if and only if

(a)

either
\lambda_{c}=2\lambda_{2}
and

\delta=1-\lambda_{1}/2
,

(b)

or
\lambda_{c}=-2\lambda_{2}
and

\delta=\lambda_{1}/2
.

Proof.

Set
\mathrm{G}=\widehat{\mathrm{K}(4)}
.
Recall that

\Delta=\{\pm\epsilon_{1},\pm\epsilon_{2},\pm\epsilon_{1}+\pm\epsilon_{2}\}

and consider a
new triangular decomposition

G=G^{+}\oplus G^{0}\oplus G^{-},

where

G^{+}=\oplus_{k=-1}^{1}\,\mathrm{G}^{(\epsilon_{2}+k\epsilon_{1})},

G^{0}=\oplus_{k=-1}^{1}\,\mathrm{G}^{(k\epsilon_{1})}\hbox{ and }

G^{-}=\oplus_{k=-1}^{1}\,\mathrm{G}^{(-\epsilon_{2}+k\epsilon_{1})}.

By highest weight theory, the

G^{0}
-module

M=\oplus_{k\geq 0}\,V^{(\lambda-k\epsilon_{1})}

is irreducible. It follows that

V^{(\lambda-2\epsilon_{1})}=0

if and only if

\Bigl(G^{(\epsilon_{1})}\Bigr)^{2}\Bigl(G^{(-\epsilon_{1})}\Bigr)^{2}\,V^{(\lambda)}=0.

We observe that

(a)

G^{(-\epsilon_{1})}
is the span
the modes of
\eta_{1}(x)
and
\eta^{*}_{1}(x)

(b)

G^{(\epsilon_{1})}
is the span of
the modes of
\zeta_{1}(v)
and
\zeta^{*}_{1}(v)

(c)

for any nonzero

\mathrm{v}\in V^{(\lambda)}_{0}
, the modes of
\mathrm{v}(z)
generates

V^{(\lambda)}
.

It follows that

V^{(\lambda-2\epsilon_{1})}=0

if and only if all Formulas (a-k) of Lemmas
14.2
vanish. It is equivalent to
the equations:

(\delta-1+\frac{\lambda_{1}}{2})(\delta-\frac{\lambda_{1}}{2})=0

(\lambda_{2}-\frac{\lambda_{c}}{2})(\delta-1+\frac{\lambda_{1}}{2})=0

(\delta-\frac{\lambda_{1}}{2})(\lambda_{2}+\frac{\lambda_{c}}{2})=0

(\lambda_{2}+\frac{\lambda_{c}}{2})(\lambda_{2}-\frac{\lambda_{c}}{2})=0,

which are equivalent to Condition (a) or (b).
∎

Sufficiency of Condition (b) and (c)

By definition, we have

\Delta^{+}=\{\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2},\pm\epsilon_{1}+\epsilon_{2}\}

and
\Delta^{+}=-\Delta^{+}
.

The Dynkin diagram
of
\mathfrak{so}(4)

1

2

admits an involution
\tau
exchanging the two vertices.
This involution can be lifted to

\tilde{{\mathrm{Grass}}(4)}
as

\tau(\zeta_{1})=\eta_{1},\tau(\eta_{1})=\zeta_{1},\tau(\zeta_{2})=\zeta_{2},\tau(\eta_{2})=\zeta_{2}

thus it extends to
\widehat{\mathrm{K}(4)}
.

The involution
\tau
stabilizes
H
, and its action
on
H^{*}
is

\tau(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\lambda_{c})=(-\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\lambda_{c}).

We have

\tau(\Delta^{+})=\{-\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2},\pm\epsilon_{1}+\epsilon_{2}\}.

Lemma 14.4
.

Let
(\lambda,\delta,u)\in H^{*}\times\mathbb{C}\times\mathbb{C},

be an arbitrary triple. Assume that
\lambda
is dominant,
\lambda(h_{1})=1
and
\lambda_{2}\geq 1
.
If

(b)

\delta=\frac{1-\lambda_{2}}{2}
and

\lambda_{c}=2\lambda_{2}
,

(c)

or

\delta=\frac{1+\lambda_{2}}{2}
and

\lambda_{c}=-2\lambda_{2}
,

the
\widehat{\mathrm{K}(4)}
-module
V(\lambda,\delta,u)
is cuspidal.

Proof.

Assume Condition (b).

Set
\tilde{\lambda}=(\lambda_{2},\lambda_{2},\lambda_{c})

and set
V:=V(\tilde{\lambda},1-\frac{\lambda_{2}}{2},u)
.
By hypothesis

\tilde{\lambda}(h_{1})\geq 2,

thus
V
is cuspidal by Lemma
14.1
.
By Lemma
14.3
, we have

V^{\tilde{\lambda}-2\epsilon_{1}}=0.

Set

\mu=(\lambda_{2}-1,\lambda_{2},\lambda_{c})=\tilde{\lambda}-\epsilon_{1}).

It follows easily from Formula (a) of Lemma

14.2
that
V^{\mu}\neq 0
. Moreover it is clear that

V^{(\mu+\alpha)}=0\hbox{ for all }\alpha\in\tau(\Delta^{+}).

Hence the highest weight of
V^{\prime}:=\tau(V)
is

\tau(\mu)=(1-\lambda_{2},\lambda_{2},\lambda_{c})=\lambda

We have

(V^{\prime})^{(\lambda)}={\mathrm{Tens}}(\lambda,\delta^{\prime},u^{\prime}),

for some
\delta^{\prime},u^{\prime}\in\mathbb{C}
. Since the eigenvalues of

-E_{0}
are in
u\,\mathrm{mod}\,\mathbb{Z}
, we have
u=u^{\prime}
. Since

(V^{\prime})^{(\lambda-2\epsilon_{1})}=0
and
\lambda_{c}=2\lambda_{2}
, we deduce from Corollary
14.3
that
\delta^{\prime}=\delta
. Hence under Condition (a), the module
V(\lambda,\delta,u)
is cuspidal.

The proof of cuspidality under Condition (c) is identical.
∎

Corollary 14.5
.

Let
(\lambda,\delta,u)\in H^{*}\times\mathbb{C}\times\mathbb{C},

be an arbitrary triple. Assume that
\lambda
is dominant and
\lambda(h_{1})=1
. If

(b)

either
\delta=\frac{1-\lambda_{2}}{2}
and

\lambda_{c}=2\lambda_{2}
,

(c)

or

\delta=\frac{1+\lambda_{2}}{2}
and

\lambda_{c}=-2\lambda_{2}
,

the
\widehat{\mathrm{K}(4)}
-module
V(\lambda,\delta,u)
is cuspidal.

Proof.

If
\lambda_{2}\geq 1
, the corollary follows Lemma
14.4
.

Otherwise
we have
\lambda_{1}=\lambda_{2}=\frac{1}{2}
and

(\delta,\lambda_{c})=(\frac{1}{4},1)
or

(\frac{3}{4},-1)
. In both case the module

V(\lambda,\delta,u)
is cuspidal by Theorem

10
.
∎

Necessity of Conditions (a),(b) or (c) for cuspidality

Corollary 14.6
.

Let
(\lambda,\delta,u)\in H^{*}\times\mathbb{C}\times\mathbb{C},

be an arbitrary triple with
\lambda
dominant
If
V(\lambda,\delta,u)
is cuspidal, then

(a)

either
\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2}\geq 2

that is
\lambda(h_{1})\geq 2
,

(b)

or

\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2}=1
,
\delta=\frac{1-\lambda_{2}}{2}
and

\lambda_{c}=2\lambda_{2}
,

(c)

or

\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2}=1
,
\delta=\frac{1+\lambda_{2}}{2}
and

\lambda_{c}=-2\lambda_{2}
.

Proof.

If
\lambda(h1)\geq 2
,
Condition (a) is satified. By Theorem
6
, we have

\lambda(h_{1})\geq 1,

thus we can assume that

\lambda(h_{1})=1
.
Set
\mu=\lambda-2\epsilon_{1}
. It is clear that

\lambda+\epsilon_{2}-\epsilon_{1}
is not

<\lambda
. Since

\alpha_{1}=\epsilon_{1}+\epsilon_{2}
we deduce

e_{1}.V^{(\mu)}\subset V^{(\lambda+\epsilon_{2}-\epsilon_{1})}=0.

We observe that
\mu(h_{1})=-1
. Since
V
contains only finitely many weights, the elementary
\mathfrak{sl}(2)
-theory implies that
V^{(\lambda-2\epsilon_{1})}=V^{(\mu)}
is zero.
Therefore by Corrolary
14.3
Condition (b) or
Condition (c) is satisfied.
∎

15
Classification of cuspidal
\mathrm{K}(2m)
-modules for
2m\geq 4

Let
\mathrm{K}(2m)
be a contact superalgebra for some integer
m\geq 2
.
The superalgebra
\mathrm{K}(2m)
is the contact algebra on

\mathbb{C}[t,t^{-1},\zeta_{1},\eta_{1},\cdots,\zeta_{m},\eta_{m}].

The set of quadratic elements

\{\xi_{i}\xi_{j},\xi_{i}\eta_{j},\eta_{i}\eta_{j}\mid 1\leq i,j\leq m\}

form a basis of the Lie subalgebra

\mathfrak{so}(2m)\subset\mathrm{K}(2m)
.
The Cartan subalgebra
H
of the Lie algebra

\mathfrak{so}(2m)
has basis

\{\xi_{i}\eta_{i}\mid 1\leq i\leq m\}.

Consequently a weight
\lambda\in H^{*}
is written as the

m
-uple
(\lambda_{1},\cdots,\lambda_{m})
where

\lambda_{i}=\lambda(\xi_{i}\eta_{i})
.

With the conventions of chapter 2, the simple roots
of
\mathfrak{so}(2m)
are

\alpha_{1}=\epsilon_{1}+\epsilon_{2},\,\alpha_{2}=\epsilon_{2}-\epsilon_{1},\cdots,\,\alpha_{m}=\epsilon_{m}-\epsilon_{m-1},

and corresponding Dynkin diagrams
is

m
m-1
4
3
2
1

It is an elementary fact
that a weight
\lambda
is dominant if and only if

(a)

all
\lambda_{i}
are integers or all
\lambda_{i}
are half-integers,

(b)

\lambda_{1}\leq\lambda_{2}\leq\cdots\leq\lambda_{m}

(c)

\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2}\geq 0
.

In this section we will show the following result.

Theorem 12
.

Assume that
2m\geq 4
.
Let

(\lambda,\delta,u)\in H^{*}\times\mathbb{C}\times\mathbb{C},

be an arbitrary triple with
\lambda
dominant.
Then the
\mathrm{K}(2m)
-module
V(\lambda,\delta,u)

is cuspidal if and only if

\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2}\geq 2,\hbox{ that is }\lambda(h_{1})\geq 2.

The proof is an obvious consequence of
Corollaries
15.1
and

15.2
proved in this chapter.

Sufficiency of the condition
\lambda(h_{1})\geq 2

Set
\mathrm{L}=\mathrm{K}(2m)
.
As before, we define a subalgebra

\mathrm{L}^{\bf(1)}
, called the
isotropy subalgebra
and we identify its highest weight modules.

Let
\mathrm{L}^{\bf(1)}
be the ideal of the algebra

\mathbb{C}[t,t^{-1},\zeta_{1},\eta_{1}\cdots,\eta_{m}]

generated by
(t-1)
and
\mathfrak{so}(2m)
. By Lemma
2.1
,
\mathrm{L}^{\bf(1)}
is a
Lie subalgebra and we set

T:=\mathrm{L}/\mathrm{L}^{\bf(1)}.

As a

\mathrm{L}^{\bf(1)}
-module
T
is a nonsplit extension

0\to T_{\overline{1}}\to T\to T_{\overline{0}}\to 0.

Thus we define the ideal

\mathrm{L}^{\bf(2)}\subset\mathrm{L}^{\bf(1)}
by

\mathrm{L}^{\bf(2)}=\{\partial\in\mathrm{L}^{\bf(1)}\mid\partial T_{\overline{0}}\subset T_{\overline{1}}\hbox{ and }\partial T_{\overline{0}}=0\}.

It is easy to verify that

\mathrm{L}^{\bf(1)}=(\mathfrak{so}(2m)\oplus(t-1)D)\ltimes\mathrm{L}^{\bf(2)}.

For an arbitrary pair
\lambda,\delta
,
let
S(\lambda,\delta)
be the

\hat{\mathrm{L}}^{\bf(1)}
-module defined as follows

(a)

The action of
\mathrm{L}^{\bf(2)}
is trivial,
so
S(\lambda,\delta)
is a

\Bigl(\mathfrak{so}(2m)\oplus\mathbb{C}(t-1)D\Bigr)
-module.

(b)

S(\lambda,\delta)

is the simple
\mathfrak{so}(2m)
-module with highest weight

\lambda
,

(c)

the element
(t-1)D
acts
as
\delta
.

As a consequence of Proposition
4.7
we obtain

Corollary 15.1
.

Let

(\lambda,\delta,u)\in H^{*}\times\mathbb{C}\times\mathbb{C},

be an arbitrary triple with
\lambda
dominant. If

\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2}\geq 2\hbox{ that is }\lambda(h_{1})\geq 2,

the
\mathrm{K}(2m)
-module

V(\lambda,\delta,u)
is cuspidal.

Proof.

We check the hypothesis of Proposition
4.7

and we compute the datum
(\omega,m,\delta^{-})
.
Here the grading element
\ell_{0}
is
D
and the
tangent space
T
has basis

\{D\}\cup\{\zeta_{i},\eta_{i}\mid 1\leq i\leq m\}\hbox{ modulo }\mathrm{L}^{\bf(1)}.

Thus

T^{-}=\oplus_{i=1}^{m}\,\mathbb{C}\eta_{i},\,\,\omega=\sum_{i=1}^{m}\epsilon_{i}.

Since
[(t-1)D,\eta_{i}]=-\frac{1}{2}\eta_{i}

we have
\delta^{-}=-\frac{1}{2}
.

We have

\omega(h_{1})=2\hbox{ and }\omega(h_{i})=0\hbox{ for }i\geq 2.

Since the weight
\mu:=\lambda-\omega
is dominant,
the simple
\mathrm{L}^{\bf(1)}
-module

S(\mu,\delta+m/2)

is finite dimensional. By proposition

4.7
, the highest weight of the
\mathrm{K}(2m)
-module

M:=\mathcal{F}(S(\mu,\delta+1),u)
is
\lambda=\mu+\omega
and

M^{(\lambda)}\simeq{\mathrm{Tens}}(\lambda,\delta,u).

Hence
V(\lambda,\delta,u)
is a subquotient of

M
, which proves that
V(\lambda,\delta,u)
is cuspidal.
∎

Necessity of Condition
\lambda(h_{1})\geq 2

Recall that
2m\geq 4
.

Corollary 15.2
.

Assume that the

\mathrm{K}(2m)
-module
V(\lambda,\delta,u)
is cuspidal.
Then

\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2}\geq 2.

Proof.

Assume that
V:=V(\lambda,\delta,u)

is cuspidal. We claim that

V^{(\lambda-2\epsilon_{1})}\neq 0.

We identify
\mathrm{K}(4)
with the bracket subalgebra

\mathbb{C}[t,t^{-1},\zeta_{1},\eta_{1},\zeta_{2},\eta_{2}]
.
Let
V^{\prime}
be the
K(4)
-submodule generated
by
V^{(\lambda)}
and set

Since by Theorem
6
,
\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2}\geq 1
, we deduce that

\lambda_{2}\geq 1.

Next, let
V\subset V(\lambda,\delta,u)
be the

\mathrm{K}(4)
-submodule generated by
V^{(\lambda)}
.
By Corollary
14.3

\left(\mathrm{L}^{(-\epsilon_{1})}\right)^{2}V^{(\lambda)}\neq 0

.

Therefore
\mu:\lambda-2\epsilon_{1}
is a weight of

V
. We observe that

\mu+\alpha_{1}=\mu+\epsilon_{1}+\epsilon_{2}\not\leq\lambda.

Since
e_{1}V^{(\mu)}=0
we have
\mu(h_{1}\geq 0
which
implies

\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2}\geq 2.

∎

16
Classification of cuspidal modules

over
\mathrm{K}_{*}(2m+1)
for
2m+1\geq 5

In Section
2.5
we have defined the
superalgebras

\mathrm{K}_{*}(2m+1)
as the contact bracket algebra over

\mathbb{C}[t,t^{-1},t^{\tau}\zeta_{1},t^{\tau}\eta_{1},\cdots,t^{\tau}\zeta_{m},t^{\tau}\eta_{m},t^{\tau}\xi]

where
\zeta_{1},\cdots,\eta_{m},\xi
are odd variables. The superconformal
algebra is a Ramond contact superalgebra if
\tau=0

and it is a Neveu-Schwarz
contact superalgebra if
\tau=1/2
.

The set of quadratic elements

\mathcal{Q}:=\{\xi_{i}\xi_{j},\,\eta_{i}\eta_{j}\mid 1\leq i<j\leq m\}\cup\{\,\xi_{i}\xi,\,\eta_{j}\xi\mid 1\leq i\leq m\}

form a basis of the Lie subalgebra
\mathfrak{so}(2m+1)\subset\mathrm{K}(2m+1)
. The
Cartan subalgebra
H
of the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{so}(2m+1)

has basis

\{\xi_{i}\eta_{i}\mid 1\leq i\leq m\}.

Consequently a weight
\lambda\in H^{*}
is written as a

m
-uple
(\lambda_{1},\cdots,\lambda_{m})
where

\lambda_{i}=\lambda(\xi_{i}\eta_{i})
.

With respect with the triangular decomposition
defined in Chapter 2, the simple roots
of
\mathfrak{so}(2m+1)
are

\alpha_{1}=\epsilon_{1},\,\alpha_{2}=\epsilon_{2}-\epsilon_{1},\cdots,\,\alpha_{m}=\epsilon_{m}-\epsilon_{m-1},

and the corresponding simple coroots are

h_{1}=2\zeta_{1}\eta_{1},h_{2}=\zeta_{2}\eta_{2}-\zeta_{1}\eta_{1},\cdots\,h_{m}=\zeta_{m}\eta_{m}-\zeta_{m-1}\eta_{m-1}.

The corresponding Dynkin diagram is

\lxSVG@closescope {{
