It is an elementary fact
that a weight
\lambda
is dominant if and only if

(a)

all
\lambda_{i}
are integers or all
\lambda_{i}
are half-integers,

(b)

0\leq\lambda_{1}\leq\lambda_{2}\leq\cdots\leq\lambda_{m}
.

For

(\lambda,\delta,\,u)\in H^{*}\times\mathbb{C}\times\mathbb{C},

we have defined
the
\mathrm{W}(n)
-module
V(\lambda,\delta,u)

in Chapter
8
.
Observe that
\lambda(h_{1})=2\lambda_{1}
.
In this section we show the following result.

Theorem 13
.

Assume that
2m+1\geq 5
.
Let

(\lambda,\delta,u)\in H^{*}\times\mathbb{C}\times\mathbb{C},

be an arbitrary triple with
\lambda
dominant.
Then the
\mathrm{K}_{*}(2m+1)
-module
V(\lambda,\delta,u)

is cuspidal if and only if

\lambda(h_{1})\geq 2\hbox{ that is }\lambda_{1}\geq 1.

The proof results from Corollaries

16.1
and
16.2
.

Sufficiency of the condition
\lambda(h_{1})\geq 1

In this Section, we assume
2m+1\geq 3
.
Set
\mathrm{L}=\mathrm{K}_{*}(2m+1)
. As before, we define a subalgebra
\mathrm{L}^{\bf(1)}\subset\mathrm{L}
and
we identify its highest weight modules.

For
simplicity, we first treat the case

\mathrm{L}:=\mathrm{K}(2m+1)
. Let
\mathrm{L}^{\bf(1)}
be the ideal of the commutative superalgebra

\mathbb{C}[t,t^{-1},\xi_{1},\cdots,\eta_{n},\xi]

generated by
t-1
and the set
\mathcal{Q}
of quadratic elements.
By Lemma
2.1
,
\mathrm{L}^{\bf(1)}
is a
Lie subalgebra and we set

T:=\mathrm{L}/\mathrm{L}^{\bf(1)}.

As a

\mathrm{L}^{\bf(1)}
-module
T
is a nonsplit extension

0\to T_{\overline{1}}\to T\to T_{\overline{0}}\to 0.

Thus we define the ideal

\mathrm{L}^{\bf(2)}\subset\mathrm{L}^{\bf(1)}
by

\mathrm{L}^{\bf(2)}=\{\partial\in\mathrm{L}^{\bf(1)}\mid\partial T_{\overline{0}}\subset T_{\overline{1}}\hbox{ and }\partial T_{\overline{0}}=0\}.

It is easy to verify that

\mathrm{L}^{\bf(1)}=(\mathfrak{so}(2m+1)\oplus(t-1)D)\ltimes\mathrm{L}^{\bf(2)}.

For
\lambda\in H^{*}
we denote by

S(\lambda,\delta)
the simple

\mathrm{L}^{\bf(1)}/\mathrm{L}^{\bf(2)}
-module defined as follows
For an arbitrary pair
\lambda,\delta
,
let
S(\lambda,\delta)
be the

\hat{\mathrm{L}}^{\bf(1)}
-module defined as follows

(a)

The action of
\mathrm{L}^{\bf(2)}
is trivial,
so
S(\lambda,\delta)
is a

\Bigl(\mathfrak{so}(2m+1)\oplus\mathbb{C}(t-1)D\Bigr)
-module.

(b)

S(\lambda,\delta)

is the simple
\mathfrak{so}(2m+1)
-module with highest weight

\lambda
,

(c)

the element
(t-1)D
acts
as
\delta
.

For the superconformal algebra

\mathrm{L}_{NS}=\mathrm{K}_{NS}(2m+1)
, the definition is similar, namely

\mathrm{L}_{NS}^{\bf(1)}=\mathrm{L}^{\bf(1)}_{\overline{0}}\oplus t^{1/2}\mathrm{L}^{\bf(1)}_{\overline{1}},\hbox{ and }

\mathrm{L}_{NS}^{\bf(2)}=\mathrm{L}^{\bf(2)}_{\overline{0}}\oplus t^{1/2}\mathrm{L}^{\bf(1)}_{\overline{2}}.

Also we consider
S(\lambda,\delta)
as an even
vector space. Therefore it can be viewed as a

\mathrm{L}_{NS}^{\bf(1)}
-module as well.

Corollary 16.1
.

Assume that
2m+1\geq 3
.
Let

(\lambda,\delta,u)\in H^{*}\times\mathbb{C}\times\mathbb{C},

be an arbitrary triple with
\lambda
dominant. If

\lambda(h_{1})\geq 2

the
\mathrm{K}_{*}(2m+1)
-module
V(\lambda,\delta,u)

is cuspidal.

Proof.

We check the hypothesis of Proposition
4.7

and we compute the datum
(\omega,m,\delta^{-})
.
Here the grading element
\ell_{0}
is
D
and the
tangent space
T
has basis

\{D,\xi\}\cup\{\zeta_{i},\eta_{i}\mid 1\leq i\leq m\}\hbox{ modulo }\mathrm{L}^{\bf(1)}\hbox{ if }\mathrm{L}=\mathrm{L}_{R}.

\{D,t^{\frac{1}{2}}\xi\}\cup\{t^{\frac{1}{2}}\zeta_{i},t^{\frac{1}{2}}\eta_{i}\mid 1\leq i\leq m\}\hbox{ modulo }\mathrm{L}^{\bf(1)}\hbox{ if }\mathrm{L}=\mathrm{L}_{NS}.

Thus

T^{-}=\oplus_{i=1}^{m}\,\mathbb{C}t^{\tau}\eta_{i},\,\,\omega=\sum_{i=1}^{m}\epsilon_{i}.

Since

[(t-1)D,t^{\tau}\eta_{i}]=-\frac{1}{2}\eta_{i}+\tau(t-1)t^{\tau}\eta_{i}\equiv-\frac{1}{2}\eta_{i}\hbox{ modulo }\mathrm{L}^{\bf(1)}

we have
\delta^{-}=-\frac{1}{2}
.

We observe that

\omega(h_{1})=2\hbox{ and }\omega(h_{i})=0\hbox{ for }i\geq 2.

Since the weight
\mu:=\lambda-\omega
is dominant,
the simple
\hat{\mathrm{L}}^{\bf(1)}
-module

S(\mu,\delta+m/2)

is finite dimensional. By proposition

4.7
, the highest weight of the
\mathrm{K}(2m+1)
-module

M:=\mathcal{F}(S(\mu,\delta+1),u)
is
\lambda=\mu+\omega
and

M^{(\lambda)}\simeq{\mathrm{Tens}}(\lambda,\delta,u).

Hence
V(\lambda,\delta,u)
is a subquotient of

M
, which proves that
V(\lambda,\delta,u)
is cuspidal.
∎

Necessity of the condition
\lambda(h_{1})\geq 2

In this Section, we assume
2m+1\geq 5
.
Using the natural embedding

\mathbb{C}[\zeta_{1},\zeta_{2},\eta_{1},\eta_{2}]\subset\mathbb{C}[\zeta_{1},\cdots,\zeta_{m},\eta_{1},\cdots,\eta_{m},\xi]

in the Ramond case or the embedding

\mathbb{C}[t^{\frac{1}{2}}\zeta_{1},t^{\frac{1}{2}}\zeta_{2},t^{\frac{1}{2}}\eta_{1},t^{\frac{1}{2}}\eta_{2}]\subset\mathbb{C}[t^{\frac{1}{2}}\zeta_{1},\cdots,t^{\frac{1}{2}}\zeta_{m},t^{\frac{1}{2}}\eta_{1},\cdots,t^{\frac{1}{2}}\eta_{m},t^{\frac{1}{2}}\xi]

in the Neveu-Schwarz case,
we consider
\mathrm{K}(4)
as a subalgebra of
\mathrm{K}(2m+1)
.

Corollary 16.2
.

Assume that
2m+1\geq 5
.
Let

(\lambda,\delta,u)\in H^{*}\times\mathbb{C}\times\mathbb{C},

be an arbitrary triple with
\lambda
dominant.
If the
\mathrm{K}(2m+1)
-module
V(\lambda,\delta,u)

is cuspidal, then

\lambda(h_{1})\geq 2.

Proof.

Set
V=V(\lambda,\delta,u)
. We claim
that

V^{(\lambda-2\epsilon_{1})}\neq 0.

By Theorem
6
we have
\lambda(h_{1})\geq 1
, that is
\lambda_{1}\geq\frac{1}{2}
. Since
\lambda

is dominant, we deduce that

\lambda_{2}\neq 0.

The
\mathrm{K}(4)
submodule generated by
V^{(\lambda)}

has a quotient
V^{\prime}
isomorphic to

V((\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}),\delta,u)
. We consider

V^{\prime}
as a
\widehat{\mathrm{K}(4)}
-module with trivial central charge. Since
\lambda_{2}>0
, we have

0=\lambda_{c}\neq\pm 2\lambda_{2}
. Thus by Corollary
14.3

we have

V^{\prime(\lambda-2\epsilon_{1})}\neq 0

which proves that

V^{(\lambda-2\epsilon_{1})}\neq 0.

Since
V
has only finitely many weights, there are invariant by the Coxeter involution
s_{1}
. Thus

\lambda+(2-\lambda(h_{1}))\epsilon_{1}=s_{1}(\lambda-2\epsilon_{1})\leq\lambda,

which proves that
\lambda(h_{1})\geq 2
.
∎

17
Cuspidal
\mathrm{K}_{*}(3)
-modules

In Section
2.5
we have defined

\mathrm{K}_{*}(3)
as the contact bracket algebras over

\mathbb{C}[t,t^{-1},t^{\tau}\zeta_{1},t^{\tau}\eta_{1},t^{\tau}\xi]

where
\zeta_{1},\eta_{1}
and
\xi
are odd variables. The superconformal
algebra
\mathrm{K}_{*}(3)
is the Ramond contact superalgebra

\mathrm{K}(3)
if
\tau=0

and it is the Neveu-Schwarz
contact superalgebra

\mathrm{K}_{NS}(3)
otherwise.

The set of quadratic elements

\mathcal{Q}:=\{\xi_{1}\eta_{1},\xi_{1}\xi,\eta_{1}\xi\}

form a basis of the Lie subalgebra
\mathfrak{so}(3)\simeq\mathfrak{sl}(2)\subset\mathrm{K}_{*}(3)_{\bar{0}}
.

Its Cartan subalgebra is the

1
-dimensional Lie algebra
H:=\mathbb{C}\zeta_{1}\eta_{1}
.
Consequently a weight
\lambda\in H^{*}
can be described as the scalar
\lambda_{1}=\lambda(\xi_{1}\eta_{1})
.
The only roots of
\mathrm{K}_{*}(3)
are
\pm\epsilon_{1}
.

The simple coroot of
\mathfrak{so}(3)
is

h_{1}=2\zeta_{1}\eta_{1}.

Hence a weight
\lambda
is dominant if and only if

\lambda_{1}
is a nonnegative
integer or half-integer. For

(\lambda,\delta,\,u)\in H^{*}\times\mathbb{C}\times\mathbb{C},

we have defined
the
\mathrm{K}(3)
-module
V(\lambda,\delta,u)

in Chapter
8
.
Observe that
\lambda(h_{1})=2\lambda_{1}
.
In this section we show the following result.

Theorem 14
.

Let

(\lambda,\delta,u)\in H^{*}\times\mathbb{C}\times\mathbb{C},

be an arbitrary triple with
\lambda
dominant.
Then the
\mathrm{K}_{*}(3)
-module
V(\lambda,\delta,u)

is cuspidal if and only if

(a)

\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2}\geq 2
, that is

\lambda(h_{1})\geq 2
, or

(b)

\lambda(h_{1})=1=\frac{1}{2}
and
\delta=\frac{1}{4}
.

The proof for
\mathrm{K}(3)
and
\mathrm{K}_{NS}(3)
are identical, so we mostly consider the Ramond form

\mathrm{K}(3)
. When needed, we explain how to adapt the proof in the Neveu-Schwarz case.
The proof of the previous theorem results from Corollaries
17.1
and

17.3

proved below.

Sufficiency of Conditions (a) and (b)

Corollary 17.1
.

Let

(\lambda,\delta,u)\in H^{*}\times\mathbb{C}\times\mathbb{C},

be an arbitrary triple with
\lambda
dominant. If

(a)

\lambda_{1}\geq 1
, that is

\lambda(h_{1})\geq 2
, or

(b)

\lambda_{1}=\frac{1}{2}
and
\delta=\frac{1}{4}
,

then the
\mathrm{K}(3)
-module
V(\lambda,\delta,u)

is cuspidal.

Proof.

The sufficiency of Condition (a) follows from Corollary
16.1
and the sufficiency
of Condition (b) follows from Theorem
10
.
∎

Necessity of Conditions (a) and (b)

The proof of the necessity of Conditions (a) and (b)
requires some computations which are easy consequences
of Lemma
14.2
.

First assume that
\mathrm{L}:=\mathrm{K}(3)
is the Ramond contact superalgebra. Let
v
be a formal variable.
As before, for any
a\in{\mathrm{Grass}}(3)=\mathrm{L}_{0}
we
we consider the distribution

a(v)=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\,a(t^{n})v^{n}\in\mathrm{L}[v,v^{-1}].

To avoid ambiguities, the unit element is denoted
D
and we set

D(v)=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\,(t^{n}D)v^{n}.

Let

(\lambda,\delta,u)\in H^{*}\times\mathbb{C}\times\mathbb{C},

be an arbitrary triple with
\lambda\neq 0

and set

V:=V(\lambda,\delta,u).

Let
\mathrm{v}
be a non zero element in
V^{(\lambda)}_{\bar{0},0}
and let
z
be another formal variables.
Since
V^{(\lambda)}
is a
\mathbb{C}[t,t^{-1},\xi]
-module
we set

\mathrm{v}(z)=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\,\mathrm{v}(t^{n})v^{n}\hbox{ and }(\xi\mathrm{v})(z)=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\,(\xi\mathrm{v})(t^{n})v^{n}.

For
\mathrm{L}_{NS}=\mathrm{K}_{NS}(3)
we follow the conventions of
Section
9.5
, thus for an odd
element
a\in\mathrm{L}_{0}
we write

a(v):=\sum_{n\in\frac{1}{2}+\mathbb{Z}}\,a(t^{n})v^{n}\text{ and }

(\xi\mathrm{v})(v):=\sum_{n\in\frac{1}{2}+\mathbb{Z}}\,(\xi\mathrm{v})(t^{n})v^{n}.

As before, set

{\tilde{\mathrm{v}}}=\mathrm{v}(v,w,x,y,z)\hbox{ and }{\widetilde{\xi\mathrm{v}}}={\xi\mathrm{v}}(v,w,x,y,z).

Lemma 17.2
.

We have

(a)\hskip 2.84526pt\zeta_{1}(v)\,\zeta_{1}(w)\,\eta_{1}(x)\,\eta_{1}(y)\,\mathrm{v}(z)=(\delta-1+\frac{\lambda_{1}}{2})(\delta-\frac{\lambda_{1}}{2})(D_{v}-D_{w})(D_{x}-D_{y})\,{\tilde{\mathrm{v}}}

(b)\hskip 2.84526pt\zeta_{1}(v)\,\zeta_{1}(w)\,\eta_{1}(x)\,\eta_{1}(y)\,\xi\mathrm{v}(z)=(\delta+\frac{\lambda_{1}-1}{2})(\delta-\frac{\lambda_{1}-1}{2})(D_{v}-D_{w})(D_{x}-D_{y})\,{\widetilde{\xi\mathrm{v}}}

Proof.

Set
D_{v}=\frac{v\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}v}
and

\Delta_{z}=u+\frac{z\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}z}
. We have

D(v)\,\mathrm{v}(z)=(\delta D_{v}+\Delta_{z})\,\mathrm{v}(v,z)

\zeta_{1}\eta_{1}(v)\,\mathrm{v}(z)=\lambda_{1}\,\mathrm{v}(v,z).

Thus the first formula follows from Lemma
14.2
(a).

We have

D(v)\,(\xi\mathrm{v})(z)=((\delta+\frac{1}{2})D_{v}+\Delta_{z})\,(\xi\mathrm{v})(v,z)\hbox{ and }

\zeta_{1}\eta_{1}(v)\,(\xi\mathrm{v})(z)=\lambda_{1}\,(\xi\mathrm{v})(v,z),

which similarly proves Formula (b).
∎

Corollary 17.3
.

Let

(\lambda,\delta,u)\in H^{*}\times\mathbb{C}\times\mathbb{C},

be an arbitrary triple with
\lambda
dominant.
If the
\mathrm{K}(3)
-module
V(\lambda,\delta,u)

is cuspidal then

(a)

\lambda_{1}\geq 1
, or

(b)

\lambda_{1}=\frac{1}{2}
and
\delta=\frac{1}{4}
.

Proof.

By Theorem
6
we have

\lambda_{1}\geq 1
, that is
\lambda_{1}\geq\frac{1}{2}
.

Set
V:=V(\lambda,\delta,u)
. First assume that

V^{(\lambda-2\epsilon_{1})}\neq 0.

The weights of
V
are stable by the Coxeter involution

s_{1}
, we deduce that

\lambda\geq s_{1}(\lambda-2\epsilon_{1})=\lambda+(2-2\lambda_{1}\epsilon_{1}),

which implies that
\lambda_{1}\geq 1
.

Consequently when
\lambda_{1}=\frac{1}{2}
we have

V^{(\lambda-2\epsilon_{1})}=0.

Lemma
17.2
implies that

(\delta-1+\frac{\lambda_{1}}{2})(\delta-\frac{\lambda_{1}}{2})=0\hbox{ and }

(\delta+\frac{\lambda_{1}-1}{2})(\delta-\frac{\lambda_{1}-1}{2}),

that is

(\delta-\frac{3}{4})(\delta-\frac{1}{4})=0\hbox{ and }(\delta-\frac{1}{4})(\delta+\frac{1}{4}),

which implies that
\delta=\frac{1}{4}
.
∎

18
Cuspidal modules over the Chen-Kac superalgebra
\mathrm{CK}(6)

The Chen-Kac superconformal algebra contains
the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{so}(6)\supset H
.
Relative to the chosen triangular decomposition,
the Dynkin diagram is labelled as:

2
3
1

Weights
\lambda
for
\mathrm{CK}(6)
can be described
by their values
\lambda(h_{i})
or as
triples
(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3})
.
Indeed

\lambda(h_{1})=\lambda_{2}-\lambda_{1},\,\lambda(h_{2})=\lambda_{2}+\lambda_{1},\,\text{ and }\lambda(h_{3})=\lambda_{3}-\lambda_{2}.

In this Section we prove:

Theorem 15
.

Let

(\lambda,\delta,u)\in H^{*}\times\mathbb{C}\times\mathbb{C},

be an arbitrary triple with
\lambda
dominant.
The
\mathrm{CK}(6)
-module
V(\lambda,\delta,u)

is cuspidal if and only if

(a)

\lambda(h_{1})\geq 2
, or

(b)

\lambda(h_{1})=1
,
\lambda(h_{3})=0
and
\delta=\frac{\lambda_{1}}{2}
.

Proof of Theorem
15

Lemma 18.1
.

Let

(\lambda,\delta,u)\in H^{*}\times\mathbb{C}\times\mathbb{C},

be an arbitrary triple with
\lambda
dominant.
If

(a)

\lambda(h_{1})\geq 2
, or

(b)

\lambda(h_{1})=1
,
\lambda(h_{3})=0
and
\delta=\frac{\lambda_{1}}{2}
.

the
\mathrm{CK}(6)
-module
V(\lambda,\delta,u)

is cuspidal.

Proof.

The paper
[
37
]
investigates
the cuspidal
\mathrm{Conf}(\mathrm{CK}(6))
-module. The embedding of
\mathrm{Vir}
in
[
37
]
and here are not the same.
However it follows from
[
37
]
that the

\mathrm{CK}(6)
-module

V(\lambda,\delta,u)
is cuspidal.
∎

Theorem 16
.

Let

(\lambda,\delta,u)\in H^{*}\times\mathbb{C}\times\mathbb{C},

be an arbitrary triple with
\lambda
dominant. If the
\mathrm{CK}(6)
-module
V(\lambda,\delta,u)

is cuspidal then

(a)

\lambda(h_{1})\geq 2
, or

(b)

\lambda(h_{1})=1
,
\lambda(h_{3})=0
and
\delta=\frac{\lambda_{1}}{2}
.

Proof.

Asume that
V(\lambda,\delta,u)
is cuspidal
and write
\lambda=\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3}
.
By Theorem
9
,
the superconformal algebra
\widehat{\mathrm{K}(4)}
embedds in
\mathrm{CK}(6)
and its center
c
of

\widehat{\mathrm{K}(4)}
is
c=h_{1}+h_{2}+2h_{3}
.

Set
\lambda_{c}=\lambda(h_{1})+\lambda(h_{2})+2\lambda(h_{3})
.
Obviously the
\widehat{\mathrm{K}(4)}
-module

V((\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\lambda_{c}),\delta,u)

is a subquotient of
\mathrm{CK}(6)
-module
V(\lambda,\delta,u)
, thus
this
\widehat{\mathrm{K}(4)}
-module
is cuspidal.
Hence by Theorem
11
, one of the following three assertions hold:

(1)

either
\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2}\geq 2

that is
\lambda(h_{1})\geq 2
,

(2)

or

\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2}=1
,
\delta=\frac{1-\lambda_{2}}{2}
and

\lambda_{c}=2\lambda_{2}
,

(3)

or

\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2}=1
,
\delta=\frac{1+\lambda_{2}}{2}
and

\lambda_{c}=-2\lambda_{2}
.

In the first case, Assertion (a) holds.

We now consider the second case.
We observe that
\lambda(h_{1})=\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2}=1

and that
\delta=\frac{1-\lambda_{2}}{2}=\frac{\lambda_{1}}{2}
. Moreover

\lambda(c)=\lambda(h_{1})+\lambda(h_{2})+2\lambda(h_{3})=2\lambda_{3}.

Therefore
\lambda_{2}=\lambda_{3}
, which is equivalent to
\lambda(h_{3})=0
. Thus Assertion (b) holds.

The third case is impossible. Indeed

\lambda(c)=\lambda(h_{1})+\lambda(h_{2})+2\lambda(h_{3})

is necessarily positive, though
-2\lambda_{2}
is negative.
∎

PART III: Classification of cuspidal modules

for twisted superconformal algebras

The superconformal algebra
\mathrm{K}(2n)
of Ramond type
admit an involution
\sigma
which extends the Dynkin
diagram involution of their subalgebra
\mathfrak{so}(2n)
.
Set
\mathrm{K}^{\bf(2)}(2n):=\mathrm{K}(2n)^{\sigma}
.
We will now deduce from the classification
of cuspidal
K(2n)
-modules the classification
of cuspidal
\mathrm{K}^{\bf(2)}(2n)
-modules.

19
Locality for modules of growth one

As in Chapter
5
we consider the

\mathbb{Z}
-graded Lie algebra

\mathfrak{g}:=\mathrm{Vir}\ltimes h\otimes\mathbb{C}[t,t^{-1}].

Let
\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{C}
and let
A,B
be
\mathfrak{g}
-modules,
such that

A=A^{(\alpha)}\text{ and }B=B^{(\beta)},

that is the operators
h|_{A}
and
h|_{B}
act by multiplication by, respectively,
\alpha
and
\beta
.
Assume moreover that
A
and
B
are
\mathbb{Z}
-graded
\mathfrak{g}
-modules of growth one and the scalars
\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{C}
are nonzero. By Corollary
5.13
, the operator

h(t)|_{A}
and
h(t)|_{B}

are invertible.
Thus for
a\in A_{0}
,
b\in B_{0}
we define the distributions:

a(z)=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\,\Bigl(\frac{h(t)}{\alpha}\Bigr)^{n}\cdot a\,z^{n}\text{ and }b(z)=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\,\Bigl(\frac{h(t)}{\beta}\Bigr)^{n}\cdot b\,z^{n}.

In this chapter, we prove the following result:

Theorem 17
.

Let
C
be another

\mathbb{Z}
-graded
\mathfrak{g}
-module of growth one, and let

\pi:A\otimes B\to C

be an homomorphism of
\mathbb{Z}
-graded
\mathfrak{g}
modules.

The distributions
a(z)
and
b(z)
are mutually local, that is

(z_{1}-z_{2})^{N}\pi(a(z_{1}),b(z_{2}))=0\text{ for some }N>>0.

Nilpotency in the commutative algebras

Let
R
be a commutative unital algebra endowed with a derivation
\partial
. By Lemma
5.1
, its radical

\mathrm{Rad}(R)
is stable by
\partial
.

Lemma 19.1
.

Let
r,s\in R
such that

\partial^{d}(r)=\alpha
,
\partial^{d}(s)=\beta
for
some nonnegative integer
d
and some

\alpha,\,\beta\in\mathbb{C}\setminus 0
. Suppose further that

f(r,s)=0
for some nonzero homogenous polynomial
f(x,y)
.

Then
r/\alpha-s/\beta
is nilpotent.

Proof.

Without loss of generality, we can assume that

\mathrm{Rad}(R)=0
and that the polynomial
f
is square free.
Thus

f=L_{1}\cdots L_{n},

where
L_{1},\dots,L_{n}
are linear forms which are pairwise nonproportional.

By hypothesis
\partial^{d}(L_{i}(r,s))=L_{i}(\alpha,\beta)
and
therefore
\partial^{d+1}(L(r,s))=0
. It follows that

0=\partial^{nd}(f(r,s))=c\prod_{i=1}^{i=n}\,\partial^{d}(L_{i}(r,s))=c\prod_{i=1}^{i=n}\,L_{i}(\alpha,\beta)

for some
c\neq 0
. Permuting some factors, we can assume that
L_{1}(\alpha,\beta)=0
.

We claim that
L_{1}(r,s)=0
, which proves the lemma. Suppose otherwise. It has been proved that
\partial^{d}(L_{1}(r,s))=0
, thus there is an integer
q\geq 0
such that
\partial^{q}(L_{1}(r,s))\neq 0

and
\partial^{q+1}(L_{1}(r,s))=0
. We have

0=\partial^{q+(n-1)d}(f(r,s))

=c^{\prime}\partial^{q}(L_{1}(r,s))\prod_{i=2}^{i=n}\,\partial^{d}(L_{i}(r,s))

=c^{\prime}\partial^{q}(L_{1}(r,s))\prod_{i=2}^{i=n}\,L_{i}(\alpha,\beta)

for some
c^{\prime}\neq 0
. Since for
i\geq 2
the linear forms
L_{i}
are not
proportional to
L_{1}
,
\prod_{i=2}^{i=n}\,L_{i}(\alpha,\beta)

is not zero. Hence
\partial^{q}(L_{1}(r,s))=0
which contradicts the choice of
q
. This completes the proof of the lemma.
∎

Structure of
\mathfrak{g}
-modules

Consider the
\mathfrak{g}
module
S=A\otimes B
.
The image of
U(h\otimes\mathbb{C}[t,t^{-1}])
in

\mathrm{End}(S)
, denoted by
\mathcal{A}
, is the algebra generated by the operators
h(t^{i})\otimes 1+1\otimes h(t^{i})
for
i\in\mathbb{Z}
.

Set
x_{\pm}=h(t^{\pm 1})\otimes 1
,
y_{\pm}=1\otimes h(t^{\pm 1})

and let
\mathcal{B}\subset\mathrm{End}(S)
be the algebra
generated by
\mathcal{A}
and the operators

x_{\pm}
and
y_{\pm}
.

Lemma 19.2
.

Assume that
\alpha+\beta\neq 0
. Then
S
is a finitely generated

\mathcal{A}
-module.

Proof.

The space
S
admits a
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
-grading

S=\oplus_{i,j\in\mathbb{Z}}S_{i,j}
, where

S_{i,j}=A_{i}\otimes B_{j}
. By Corollary
5.13
the
operators
x_{\pm}
and
y_{\pm}
are invertible. Since these four operators are bi-homogenous of degree

(\pm 1,0)
and
(0,\pm 1)
, we have

S=\mathcal{B}.S_{0,0}
,

which shows that
S
is finitely generated as a

\mathcal{B}
-module.
Therefore, it is enough to prove that that
\mathcal{B}

is finitely generated as an
\mathcal{A}
-module. Equivalently, we will prove that
x_{\pm}
and
y_{\pm}
are integral over
\mathcal{A}
.

By definition, the operators

u:=x_{+}+y_{+}=h(t)\otimes 1+1\otimes h(t)\text{ and }v:=h(t^{2})\otimes 1+1\otimes h(t^{2})

belong to

\mathcal{A}
. By Corollary
5.13
, the operators

\frac{x_{+}^{2}}{\alpha}-(h(t^{2})\otimes 1)\text{ and }\frac{y_{+}^{2}}{\beta}-(1\otimes h(t^{2}))

are nilpotent.
Thus
\frac{x_{+}^{2}}{\alpha}+\frac{y_{+}^{2}}{\beta}-v
is nilpotent, say

(\frac{x_{+}^{2}}{\alpha}+\frac{y_{+}^{2}}{\beta}-v)^{m}=0.

The substitution
y_{+}^{2}=x_{+}^{2}-2ux_{+}+u^{2}
in the previous equation
provides the equation

\Bigl(\frac{(\alpha+\beta)}{\alpha\beta}x_{+}^{2}-\frac{2u}{\beta}x_{+}+(u^{2}-v)\Bigr)^{m}=0.

which shows that
x_{+}
is integral over
\mathcal{A}
.
Since
y_{+}=u-x_{+}
, the element
y_{+}
is also integral over
\mathcal{A}
.

Similarly,
x_{-}
and
y_{-}
are integral over

\mathcal{A}
,
which completes the proof of the lemma.
∎

Let
\mathcal{A}_{A}
and
\mathcal{A}_{B}
be the image of
of
U(h\otimes\mathbb{C}[t,t^{-1}])
in, respectively,
\mathrm{End}(A)

and
\mathrm{End}(B)
. By definition we have

\mathcal{A}\subset\mathcal{B}\subset\mathcal{A}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{A}_{B}.

Let
I:=\{a\in\mathcal{A}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{A}_{B}\mid aS\subset\mathrm{Ker}\,\pi\}
.

Lemma 19.3
.

Assume
\alpha+\beta\neq 0
. There exists a nonzero homogenous polynomial

f(x,y)\in\mathbb{C}[x,y]
such that
f(x_{+},y_{+})
belongs to
I
.

Proof.

For integers
k\leq l
set

S_{[k,l]}=\oplus_{k\leq i,j\leq l}S_{i,j}
and

C_{[k,l]}=\oplus_{k\leq i\leq l}C_{i}
.
By Lemma
19.2
, there are integers

k\leq l
such that
S_{[k,l]}
generates the

\mathcal{A}
-module
S
.

For any homogenous polynomial
f(x,y)
of degree
N
, we observe that
\pi(f(x_{+},y_{+})(S_{[k,l]}))
lies in

C_{[N+2k,N+2l]}
. Since
C
has growth one, there is an integer
D
such that

\dim\,{\mathrm{Hom}}(S_{[k,l]},C_{[N+2k,N+2l]})\leq D\,\,\,\forall N\in\mathbb{Z}.

Thus there is a nonzero degree
D

homogenous polynomial
f
in two variables such that

\pi(f(x_{+},y_{+})(S_{[k,l]}))=0
.

Since
S=U(h\otimes\mathbb{C}[t,t^{-1}]).S_{[k,l]}
, it follows
that

\displaystyle\pi(f(x_{+},y_{+}).S)

\displaystyle=\pi\Bigl(f(x_{+},y_{+})U(h\otimes\mathbb{C}[t,t^{-1}]).S_{[k,l]}\Bigr)

\displaystyle=U(h\otimes\mathbb{C}[t,t^{-1}]).\pi\Bigl(f(x_{+},y_{+}).S\Bigr)

\displaystyle=0,

that is
f(x_{+},y_{+})
belongs to the ideal
I
.
∎

Lemma 19.4
.

Assume
\alpha+\beta\neq 0
. There exists
m\geq 2
such that

\pi\Bigl((\frac{h(t)}{\alpha}\otimes 1-1\otimes\frac{h(t)}{\beta})^{m}A\otimes B\Bigr)=0
.

Proof.

Consider te commutative algebra

R=\mathcal{A}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{A}_{B}/I.

The ideal
I
is invariant with respect to
\mathrm{Vir}
, henceforth the Lie algebra
\mathrm{Vir}
acts on algebra
R
.

Set
\partial=\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}
. We have
\partial x_{+}=\alpha
,
\partial x_{-}=\beta

and
f(x_{+},y_{+})
vanishes in
R
, where
f
is the homogenous polynomial defined in Lemma
19.3
. Hence Lemma
19.1

implies that
\frac{x_{+}}{\alpha}-\frac{x_{-}}{\beta}
is nilpotent modulo

I
, which is equivalent to the assertion of the lemma.
∎

The lemma also holds for
\alpha+\beta=0
, but the proof is different.

Lemma 19.5
.

For
\alpha+\beta=0
, there exists
m\geq 2
such that

\pi\Bigl((\frac{h(t)}{\alpha}\otimes 1-1\otimes\frac{h(t)}{\beta})^{m}A\otimes B\Bigr)=0
.

Proof.

Without loss of generality, we can assume that
C=C^{(0)}
.
Thus by Corollary
5.12

h(t)^{m}C=0
for some
m\geq 1
. We deduce that

\pi\Bigl((h(t)\otimes 1+1\otimes h(t))^{m}S\Bigr)=0,

which is equivalent to the lemma.
∎

Proof of Theorem
17

Proof.

By lemmas
19.4
and
19.5
, we have

\pi\Bigl((\frac{h(t)}{\alpha}\otimes 1-1\otimes\frac{h(t)}{\beta})^{m}A\otimes B\Bigr)=0.

However we have

\displaystyle(z_{1}^{-1}-z_{2}^{-1})^{m}a(z_{1})\otimes b(z_{2})

\displaystyle=\sum_{k+l=m}\,(-1)^{k}\binom{m}{k}\sum_{n_{1},n_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}}\,\bigl(\frac{h(t)}{\alpha}\bigr)^{n_{1}}a\,z^{n_{1}-k}\otimes\bigl(\frac{h(t)}{\beta}\bigr)^{n_{2}}bz_{2}^{n_{2}-l}\,

\displaystyle=\Bigl(\sum_{k+l=m}\,(-1)^{k}\binom{m}{k}\frac{h(t)}{\alpha}^{k}\otimes\frac{h(t)}{\beta}^{l}\Bigr)a(z_{1})\otimes b(z_{2})

\displaystyle=\Bigl(\frac{h(t)}{\alpha}\otimes 1-1\otimes\frac{h(t)}{\beta}\Bigr)^{m}a(z_{1})\otimes b(z_{2})

Thus
\pi((z_{1}^{-1}-z_{2}^{-1})^{m}a(z_{1})\otimes b(z_{2}))=0

which is equivalent to

(z_{1}-z_{2})^{m}\pi(a(z_{1})\otimes b(z_{2}))=0,

which proves the theorem. ∎

20
Two kinds of cuspidal
\mathrm{K}(2m)
-modules

In order to state the classification of
cuspidal
\mathrm{K}^{(2}(2m)
-modules in the next chapter, we need a preliminary result about the types of

\mathrm{K}(2m)
-modules.

Recall that

\mathrm{K}^{(2)}(2m)=\mathrm{K}(2m)^{\sigma},

for some involution
\sigma
.
A cuspidal
\mathrm{K}(2m)
-module
M
is called of
first kind
if

\sigma_{*}V\not\simeq V,

otherwise it is called of
second kind
.
We similarly define the
\widehat{\mathrm{K}(4)}
-modules of
first or second kind.

Theorem 18
.

Let
(\lambda,\delta,u)\in H^{*}\times\mathbb{C}\times\mathbb{C}
be an arbitrary triple.

(a)

If
m\geq 3
, the
\mathrm{K}(2m)
-module
V(\lambda,\delta,u)
is of second type if and only if

\lambda_{1}=1.

(b)

The
\widehat{\mathrm{K}(4)}

-module
V(\lambda,\delta,u)
is
of second type if and only if

\lambda_{1}=1\text{ and }(\lambda_{c},\delta)\neq(\pm 2\lambda_{2},\frac{1}{2}).

Proof of Theorem
18

Let
m\geq 2
.
Set
\mathrm{L}:=\mathrm{K}(2m)\text{ if }m\neq 2
or

\mathrm{L}:=\widehat{\mathrm{K}(4)}
if
m=2
. The involution
\sigma
stabilizes the subalgebras
\mathfrak{so}(2m)\subset\mathrm{L}_{\bar{0},0}
and its Cartan subalgebra
H
. Thus
\sigma
acts on
H^{*}
and

\sigma(\epsilon_{1})=-\epsilon_{1}\text{ and }\sigma(\epsilon_{k})=\epsilon_{k}\text{ for }k\neq 1.

Indeed
\sigma|_{H}
is the Dynkin diagram involution.

Let
V
be a cuspidal
\mathrm{L}
-module with highest weight

\lambda
. All weights
\mu
of
V
are

\leq\lambda
, that is

\lambda-\mu=\sum_{\alpha\in\Delta^{+}}\,m_{\alpha}\alpha,

where all
m_{\alpha}
are nonnegative integers.
(Obvserve that
\Delta=-\Delta
.)

The module
V
also admits a unique weight
\lambda^{\prime}

such that all weights
\mu
of
V
satisfy

\sigma(\mu)\leq\sigma(\lambda^{\prime})
or equivalently

\lambda^{\prime}-\mu=\sum_{\beta\in\sigma(\Delta^{+})}m_{\beta}\,\beta,

where all
m_{\beta}
are nonnegative integers.
The weight
\lambda^{\prime}
is called the

\sigma
-highest weight
of
V
.

Let
(\lambda,\delta,u)\in H^{*}\times\mathbb{C}\times\mathbb{C}
be an arbitrary triple such that the

\mathrm{L}
-module
V:=V(\lambda,\delta,u))
is cuspidal.
Set
\mathfrak{g}(H):=\mathrm{Vir}\ltimes H\otimes\mathbb{C}[t,t^{-1}]
.
We always assume that the
\mathfrak{g}(H)
-module

V^{(\lambda)}
is even.

Lemma 20.1
.

Let
\lambda^{\prime}
be the

\sigma
-highest weight of
V
.

(a)

Either
\lambda^{\prime}=\lambda-\epsilon_{1}

and the space
V^{(\lambda^{\prime})}
is odd,

(b)

or
\lambda^{\prime}=\lambda-2\epsilon_{1}
,

V^{(\lambda^{\prime})}
is even and

V^{(\lambda^{\prime})}\simeq{\mathrm{Tens}}(\lambda^{\prime},\delta,u)

as a
\mathfrak{g}(H)
-module.

Proof.

By hypothesis

V^{(\lambda+n\epsilon_{1})}=0
for
n>0
.
It is clear that
\lambda^{\prime}=\lambda-k\epsilon_{1}
,
where
k
is the biggest integer such that

V^{(\lambda-k\epsilon_{1})}\neq 0
.

Obviously,
V^{(\lambda-k\epsilon_{1})}

has parity
k\,\mathrm{mod}\,2
. Thus Assertion (a) holds
whenever
\lambda^{\prime}=\lambda-\epsilon_{1}
.

We now assume that
k\neq 1
.
We have
[\zeta_{1},\eta_{1}]=D
, hence

\zeta_{1}\eta_{1}|_{V^{(\lambda)}}=D|_{V^{(\lambda)}}\neq 0,

which implies that

V^{(\lambda-\epsilon_{1})}\neq 0
.
Thus we have
k\geq 2
. Since

\lambda^{\prime}+\alpha_{1}\not\leq\lambda,

we deduce that
e_{1}\cdot V^{(\lambda^{\prime})}=0
,
which implies that
\lambda^{\prime}(h_{1})\geq 0
, that is

\lambda(h_{1})\geq k\geq 2.

It follows that the weight

\mu:=\lambda-\omega
, where

\omega=\epsilon_{1}+\cdots+\epsilon_{m}
, is dominant.

First we assume
m\geq 3
. With the notations of
Section
15.1
, we consider the simple finite dimensional
\mathrm{L}^{\bf(1)}
-module

S:=S(\mu,\delta+\frac{m}{2})
and we set

M:=\mathcal{F}(S,u).

When
m=2
we use the notations of Section

14.1
and we consider the
simple
\hat{\mathrm{L}}^{\bf(1)}
-module

S:=S(\mu,\delta+1)
and we set

M:=\mathcal{F}(S,u).

By Proposition
4.7
,

\lambda
is the highest weight of the
\mathrm{L}
-module

M
and
there is an isomorphism of
\mathfrak{g}(H)
-modules

M^{(\lambda)}\simeq{\mathrm{Tens}}(\lambda,\delta,u).

Since
S
is a simple
\mathfrak{so}(2m)
-module with highest weight
\mu
, its

\sigma
-highest weight is also
\mu
.
Hence by proposition
4.7
the
\sigma
-highest
weight of
M
is

\mu+\sigma(\omega)=\lambda-2\epsilon_{1}

and there is an isomorphism of
\mathfrak{g}(H)
-modules

M^{(\lambda-2\epsilon_{1})}\simeq{\mathrm{Tens}}(\lambda-2\epsilon_{1},\delta,u).

Since
V
is a subquotient of
M
, it follows that
\lambda^{\prime}=\lambda-2\epsilon_{1}
and

V^{(\lambda^{\prime})}\simeq{\mathrm{Tens}}(\lambda^{\prime},\delta,u)

as a
\mathfrak{g}(H)
-module.
∎

Lemma 20.2
.

Let
V
be a cuspidal
\mathrm{L}
-module with highest weight
\lambda
.
Then
V
is of second type if and only if

\lambda_{1}=1\text{ and }V^{(\lambda-2\epsilon_{1})}\neq 0.

Proof.

The highest weight of
\sigma_{*}V
is

\sigma\lambda^{\prime}
.

If
\lambda^{\prime}=\lambda-\epsilon_{1}
the highest component of
\sigma_{*}V

is odd by Lemma
20.1
, thus

V\not\simeq\sigma_{*}V.

Otherwise
\lambda^{\prime}=\lambda-2\epsilon_{1}
, hence

\sigma(\lambda^{\prime})=\lambda+2(1-\lambda_{1})\epsilon_{1}.

Thus
\lambda_{1}=1
if
V
is of second type. Conversely assume that
\lambda_{1}=1
.
By Theorem
6
,
V
is isomorphic to

V(\lambda,\delta,u)
for some
\delta,u\in\mathbb{C}
.
We observe that
\lambda=\sigma(\lambda^{\prime})
is the highest weight of
\sigma_{*}V
. Moreover by Lemma
20.1
,

(\sigma_{*}V)^{(\lambda)}\simeq{\mathrm{Tens}}(\lambda,\delta,u)

as a
\mathfrak{g}(H)
-module, thus
\sigma_{*}V
is isomorphic to
V
.
∎

21
Classification of cuspidal modules over the

twisted superconformal algebras
\mathrm{K}^{(2)}(2m)

Our final result concerns the classification
of cuspidal modules over the
twisted superconformal algebras
\mathrm{K}^{(2)}(2m)

for
m\geq 2

Recall that

\mathrm{K}^{(2)}(2m)
is the subalgebra
\mathrm{K}(2m)^{\sigma}
of fixed points under some involution
\sigma
of
\mathrm{K}(2m)
.
By definition, a
\mathrm{K}(2m)
-module
M
endowed with an involution
\sigma
satisfying

{\sigma}(x.m)=\sigma(x){\sigma}(m),\hskip 5.69054pt\forall x\in\mathrm{K}(2m)\hbox{ and }m\in M

is called an
(\sigma,\mathrm{K}(2m))
-module.

The notion of a
(\sigma,\widehat{\mathrm{K}(4)})
-module is similarly defined.

Obviously a cuspidal
\mathrm{K}(2m)
-module of second type carries a structure of
(\sigma,\mathrm{K}(2m))
-module.
More precisely it carries two such structures,
since the involution

\sigma:M\to M
is defined up to a sign.

Recall that the type of any cuspidal

\mathrm{K}(2m)
-module or
\widehat{\mathrm{K}(4)}
-module have been determined by Theorem
18
, thus the next two theorems provide an explicit classification
of all cuspidal modules over the twisted superconformal algebras.

Theorem 19
.

Let
m\geq 3
. A cuspidal
\mathrm{K}^{(2)}(2m)
-module is

(a)

either isomorphic to a
\mathrm{K}(2m)
-module of first kind, or

(b)

to the component
V^{\sigma}
of a

\mathrm{K}(2m)
-module
V
of second kind.

The case
m=2
is special.
The involution

\sigma
can be lifted to
\widehat{\mathrm{K}(4)}

and we also have

\mathrm{K}^{(2)}(4)=\widehat{\mathrm{K}(4)}^{\sigma},

where we use the same notation
\sigma
for
the involution of
\mathrm{K}(4)
and for its lift.
Though
\mathrm{K}^{(2)}(4)
has a trivial center, its cuspidal modules inherit the central extension of
\mathrm{K}(4)
, as it is shown in the next theorem.

Theorem 20
.

A cuspidal
\mathrm{K}^{(2)}(4)
-module is

(a)

either isomorphic to a
\widehat{\mathrm{K}(4)}
-module of first kind, or

(b)

it is isomorphic to
V^{\sigma}
for some

\widehat{\mathrm{K}(4)}
-module
V
of second kind.

The proof of both Theorems is based on a doubling construction. That is there is another cuspidal

\mathrm{K}^{(2)}(2m)
-module
\tilde{V}
such that

V\oplus\tilde{V}
carries a structure of

(\sigma,\mathrm{L})
-module, where
\mathrm{L}=\mathrm{K}(2m)
if
m\geq 3

or
\mathrm{L}=\widehat{\mathrm{K}(4)}
otherwise.
The proof is based on Lemma
21.2
, which shows
why the case
\mathrm{K}^{(2)}(4)
is special: roughly speaking
the naive definition of the conformal
algebra
\mathrm{Conf}(\mathrm{K}^{(2)}(4))
is defective. However it is corrected by using
\widehat{\mathrm{K}(4)}
instead of
\mathrm{K}(4)
.

A technical lemma

Let
m\geq 2
. Set
\mathrm{L}=\widehat{\mathrm{K}(4)}
if
m=2
or

\mathrm{L}=\mathrm{K}(2m)
otherwise.

Set
\xi=\zeta_{1}+\eta_{1}
. Then

e=\zeta_{2}\xi,h=\zeta_{2}\eta_{2},f=\xi\eta_{2}

is a
\mathfrak{sl}(2)
-triple in
\mathrm{L}_{0}
.
The eigenvalues of
\mathrm{ad}(h)
are
0
and
\pm 2
, and
we write

\mathrm{L}=\mathrm{L}^{(2)}\oplus\mathrm{L}^{(0)}\oplus\mathrm{L}^{(-2)}

the corresponding eigenspace decomposition.

Lemma 21.1
.

For all integers
n,m
, we have

[h(t^{n_{1}}),[h(t^{n_{2}}),\mathrm{L}^{(0)}]]=0.

Proof.

First assume that
\mathrm{L}=\mathrm{K}(2m)
with
m\geq 3
. We have

\mathrm{L}={\mathrm{Grass}}(2m)\otimes\mathbb{C}[t,t^{-1}]\text{ and }\mathrm{L}^{(0)}={\mathrm{Grass}}(2m)^{(0)}\otimes\mathbb{C}[t,t^{-1}].

Using the explicit formulas for the bracket from Section

2.5
, we observe that

[h(t^{n_{2}}),a(t{n_{3}})]=[h,a](t^{n_{2}+n_{3}})\,\mathrm{mod}\,h{\mathrm{Grass}}(2m)\otimes\mathbb{C}[t,t^{-1}],

for any
a\in{\mathrm{Grass}}(2m)
and
n_{3}\in\mathbb{Z}
. We deduce that

[h(t^{n_{2}}),\mathrm{L}^{(0)}]\subset h{\mathrm{Grass}}(2m)\otimes\mathbb{C}[t,t^{-1}]\text{ and }

[h(t^{n_{1}}),[h(t^{n_{2}}),\mathrm{L}^{(0)}]]\subset h^{2}{\mathrm{Grass}}(2m)\otimes\mathbb{C}[t,t^{-1}]=0,

which proves the lemma for
m\neq 3
.

For
\mathrm{L}=\widehat{\mathrm{K}(4)}
we have

\mathrm{L}=\widetilde{{\mathrm{Grass}}(4)}\otimes\mathbb{C}[t,t^{-1}].

Consider the decomposition

\mathrm{L}=\oplus_{0\leq k\leq 4}\,\mathrm{L}_{k}
, where

\mathrm{L}_{k}=\widetilde{{\mathrm{Grass}}(4)}\otimes\mathbb{C}[t,t^{-1}].

It is easy to observe that

[h(t^{n_{2}}),\mathrm{L}_{k}^{(0)}]\subset\mathrm{L}_{k+2}^{(0)},

therefore

[h(t^{n_{1}}),[h(t^{n_{2}}),\mathrm{L}^{(0)}]]=[h(t^{n_{1}}),[h(t^{n_{2}}),\mathrm{Vir}]]=0.

∎

The conformal algebra
\mathrm{Conf}(\mathrm{K}^{(2)}(2m))

Let
m\geq 2
. Set
\mathrm{L}=\widehat{\mathrm{K}(4)}
if
m=2
or

\mathrm{L}=\mathrm{K}(2m)
if
m\geq 3
. By definition

\mathrm{K}^{(2)}(2m)=\mathrm{L}^{\sigma}

where
\sigma
is the involution defined by

\sigma(\zeta_{1}(t^{n}))=(-1)^{n}\eta_{1}(t^{n}),\,\sigma(\eta_{1}(t^{n}))=(-1)^{n}\zeta_{1}(t^{n})

\sigma(\zeta_{l}(t^{n}))=(-1)^{n}\zeta_{l}(t^{n}),\,\sigma(\eta_{l}(t^{n}))=(-1)^{n}\eta_{l}(t^{n})\,\,\text{ for }2\leq l\leq m.

For
a\in(L_{0})^{\sigma}
set
\tau_{a}=0
and for

a\in(L_{0})^{-\sigma}
set
\tau_{a}=1
.
For
a\in(L_{0})^{\sigma}\cup(L_{0})^{-\sigma}
, set

a[z]=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}a(t^{2n+\tau_{a}})\,z^{2n+\tau_{a}}.

This
\mathrm{L}^{\sigma}
-valued distribution
a[z]
should not be confused with the
\mathrm{L}
-valued distribution
a(z)
.
By definition,
\mathrm{Conf}(L^{\sigma})
is the conformal
algebra generated by the distributions

a[z]
, for
a\in(L_{0})^{\sigma}\cup(L_{0})^{-\sigma}
.

Indeed we have
a[z]=\frac{1}{2}(a(z)+\sigma(a)(-z)
,
thus
a[z]
is not in
\mathrm{Conf}(\mathrm{L})
. It is clear that
any two distributions
\phi_{1}[z]
and
\phi_{2}[z]

in
\mathrm{Conf}(L^{\sigma})
are
mutually
semi-local
, that is

(z_{1}^{2}-z_{2}^{2})^{N}\Bigl[\phi_{1}[z],\phi_{2}[z]\Bigr]=0\text{ for some }N>>0.

We observe that the previously defined

\mathfrak{sl}(2)
-triple
\{e,h,f\}
lies in
\mathrm{L}^{\sigma}
.
For
\phi[z]=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\phi_{n}z^{n}
, we have

[h[z],\phi[z]]_{0}=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}[h,\phi_{n}]z^{n}.

Hence
h
acts over
\mathrm{Conf}(\mathrm{L}^{\sigma})
and it admits an eigenspace decomposition

\mathrm{Conf}(\mathrm{L}^{\sigma})=\mathrm{Conf}(\mathrm{L}^{\sigma})^{(2)}\oplus\mathrm{Conf}(\mathrm{L}^{\sigma})^{(0)}\oplus\mathrm{Conf}(\mathrm{L}^{\sigma})^{(-2)}.

Lemma 21.2
.

The algebra
\mathrm{Conf}(\mathrm{L}^{\sigma})
is generated by

\mathrm{Conf}(\mathrm{L}^{\sigma})^{(2)}\oplus\mathrm{Conf}(\mathrm{L}^{\sigma})^{(-2)}.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{C}\subset\mathrm{Conf}(\mathrm{L}^{\sigma})
be the
subalgebra generated by

\mathrm{Conf}(\mathrm{L}^{\sigma})^{(2)}\oplus\mathrm{Conf}(\mathrm{L}^{\sigma})^{(-2)}.

There is a basis of the set of odd variables
of the form
\{\zeta_{2},\eta_{2},\xi_{1},\cdots\xi_{2m-2}\}

such that

(a)

\sigma(\xi_{1})=-\xi_{1}
and
\sigma
fixes all the other variables,

(b)

[\zeta_{2},\eta_{2}]=1
,
\xi_{i}^{2}=1
and all other brackets are zero.

First assume
m\geq 3
. The elements

\omega
and
\zeta_{2}\eta_{2}\omega
, where
\omega

runs over
\mathbb{C}[\xi_{1},\cdots,\xi_{2m-2}]
, span

\mathrm{L}_{0}^{(0)}\simeq{\mathrm{Grass}}(2m)^{(0)}
. Similarly the elements

\zeta_{2}\omega
and
\eta_{2}\omega
generate
respectively
\mathrm{L}_{0}^{(2)}
and
\mathrm{L}_{0}^{(-2)}
.
We now prove that the distributions

\omega[z]
and
\zeta_{2}\eta_{2}\omega[z]
belong to

\mathcal{C}
.

We have

[\zeta_{2},\eta_{2}\omega(t^{n})]+[\eta_{2},\zeta_{2}\omega(t^{n})]=2\omega(t^{n}),

thus

2\omega[z]=[\zeta_{2},\eta_{2}\omega[z]]+[\eta_{2},\zeta_{2}\omega[z]]
which proves that

\omega[z]
belongs to
\mathcal{C}
.

Assume now that
\omega
has degree
k\neq 2

and
\sigma(\omega)=\pm(\omega)
. Then

[\zeta_{2},\eta_{2}\omega(t^{n+2})]-[\zeta_{2}(t^{2}),\eta_{2}\omega(t^{n})]=(k-2)\zeta_{2}\eta_{2}\omega(t^{n}),

which implies that

[\zeta_{2},\eta_{2}\omega[z]]-[\zeta_{2}(t^{2}),\eta_{2}\omega[z]]=(k-2)\zeta_{2}\eta_{2}\omega[z].

Thus
\zeta_{2}\eta_{2}\omega[z]
belongs to

\mathcal{C}
.

When
\omega
has degree
2
, we can assume that

\omega=\xi_{i}\xi_{j}
for some
i\neq j
. Choose
an index
k\neq 1,i\text{ or }j
. Obviously, we have

[\xi_{k},\xi_{k}\zeta_{2}\eta_{2}\omega[z]]=\zeta_{2}\eta_{2}\omega[z].

It has been already proved that

\xi_{k}[z]
and
\xi_{k}\zeta_{2}\eta_{2}\omega[z]
belong to

\mathcal{C}
, thus
\zeta_{2}\eta_{2}\omega[z]
belong to

\mathcal{C}
, which proves the claim for

m\geq 3
.

For
m=2
, we have
\mathrm{L}_{0}=\widetilde{{\mathrm{Grass}}}(4)
.
Let
\theta\in\widetilde{{\mathrm{Grass}}}(4)^{(0)}
be homogenous of degree
k
. The same proof shows that

\theta[z]
belongs to
\mathcal{C}
if
k\neq 4
.
Recall that
c
generates
\widetilde{{\mathrm{Grass}}_{4}}(4)
. The formula

2[\xi_{2},\xi_{1}\zeta_{2}\eta_{2}[z]]={\mathrm{Tr}}(\xi_{2}\xi_{1}\zeta_{2}\eta_{2})c[z]

shows that
c[z]
also belongs to
\mathcal{C}
, which completes the proof.
∎

The double of
V^{(\ell)}

Let
V
be a cuspidal
\mathrm{K}^{(2)}(2m)
-module. Since each
V_{n}
is finite dimensional,
the operator
h=\zeta_{2}\eta_{2}
acts diagonally with integer eigenvalues.
Let

V=\oplus_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}V^{(n)}

be the corresponding eigenspace
decomposition of
V
under
h
.

Lemma 21.3
.

The operator
h|_{V}
admits
only finitely many eigenvalues.

Moreover

(a)

its highest eigenvalue
\ell
is a positive integer and

(b)

the operator
h(t^{2})|_{V^{(\ell)}}
is invertible.

Proof.

Set
\mathfrak{g}=\mathrm{Vir}\ltimes h\otimes\mathbb{C}[t^{2},t^{-2}]
.
Since
V
is a
\mathfrak{g}
-module of growth one,

h|_{V}
admits only finitely many eigenvalues by Corollary
5.14
.

By elementary
\mathfrak{sl}(2)
-theory, the highest eigenvalue is a nonnegative integer
\ell
. By simplicity of the
superalgebra
\mathrm{L}^{\sigma}
, the action of
\mathfrak{sl}(2)
is not trivial, therefore
\ell>0
.

Moreover
h(t^{2})|_{V^{(\ell)}}
is invertible by Corollary
5.13
.
∎

Set
\frac{h(t^{2})}{\ell}=t^{2}
, that is we identify the commutative algebra

\mathbb{C}[\frac{h(t^{2})}{\ell}]
with the subalgebra

\mathbb{C}[t^{2}]\subset\mathbb{C}[t,t^{-1}]
.
Write as
v_{1}(1),\cdots v_{p}(1)
a basis of
V_{0}^{(\ell)}
and

v_{p+1}(t),\cdots v_{p+q}(t)
a basis of

V_{1}^{(\ell)}
, where
p=\dim\,V_{0}^{(l)}
and

q=\dim\,V_{1}^{(l)}
.
Define the shifts
\tau_{i}
as

\tau_{i}=0\text{ if }i\leq p\text{ and }\tau_{i}=1\text{ if }i>p.

Thus the set

\{v_{i}(t^{\tau_{i}})\mid 1\leq i\leq p+q\}

is a basis of
V_{0}^{(l)}\oplus V_{1}^{(l)}
.
At this stage, the expression
v_{i}(t^{\tau_{i}})
are merely
formal notations.

By Lemma
21.3
,
V^{(\ell)}
is a
\mathbb{C}[t^{2},t^{-2}]
-module, so we can define

v_{i}(t^{2n+\tau_{i}}):=t^{2n}v_{i}(t^{\tau_{i}})\text{ for }n\in\mathbb{Z}.

Then the set

\{v_{i}(t^{n})\mid 1\leq i\leq p+q\text{ and }n\equiv\tau_{i}\,\mathrm{mod}\,2\}

is a basis of
V
.

Let
a\in(L_{0})^{\sigma}\cup(L_{0})^{-\sigma}
, where

(L_{0})^{-\sigma}
is the set of
a\in\mathrm{L}_{0}
which are fixed by
-\sigma
. Set
\tau_{a}=0
if
a\in(L_{0})^{\sigma}
and

\tau_{a}=1
otherwise. Then
a(t^{n})
belongs to

\mathrm{L}^{\sigma}
if
n\equiv\tau_{a}\,\mathrm{mod}\,2
.
For
1\leq i\leq p+q
set

D_{i}=\{(n,m)\mathbb{Z}^{2}\mid n\equiv\tau_{a}\text{ and }m\equiv\tau_{i}\,\mathrm{mod}\,2\},

We define the functions

\gamma^{i,j}_{a}:D_{i}\to\mathbb{C},\,(n,m)\mapsto\gamma^{i,j}_{a}(n,m)

by the formula

a(t^{n}).v_{i}(t^{m})=\sum_{j}\,\gamma^{i,j}_{a}(n,m)\,v_{j}(t^{n+m}).

In the formula, it is assumed that
n\equiv\tau_{a}\,\mathrm{mod}\,2
,

m\equiv\tau_{i}\,\mathrm{mod}\,2
. Moreover we have

\gamma^{i,j}_{a}(n,m)=0
if
\tau_{a}+\tau_{i}+\tau_{j}
is odd.

Lemma 21.4
.

For
i,j\in\{1,\cdots,p+q\}
, the function

\gamma^{i,j}_{a}:D_{i}\to\mathbb{C}

is a polynomial.

Proof.

To avoid triviality, we assume that

\tau_{a}+\tau_{i}+\tau_{j}
is even.

Consider the formal distribution

v_{i}[z]=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}v_{i}(t^{2n+\tau_{i}})\,z^{2n+\tau_{i}}
.
We observe that

A:(\mathrm{L}^{\sigma})^{(-2)}
and
B:=V^{(\ell)}
are modules over the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}:=\mathrm{Vir}\ltimes h\otimes\mathbb{C}[t^{2},t^{-2}]

and the action provides a morphism of
\mathfrak{g}
-modules

\pi:A\otimes B\to C

where
C=V^{(\ell-2)}
.

It follows from Theorem
17
that

v_{i}[z]
is
mutually semi-local
with any distribution

\phi[z]
in
\mathrm{Conf}(\mathrm{L}^{\sigma})^{(-2)}
, that is

(z_{1}^{2}-z_{2}^{2})^{N}\phi[z_{1}]v_{i}[z_{2}]=0\text{ for some }N>>0.

Moreover it is obvious that
\phi[z_{1}].v_{i}[z_{2}]=0
for

\phi[z_{1}]\in\mathrm{Conf}(\mathrm{L}^{\sigma})^{(2)}
.

By Lemma
21.2
, the algebra
\mathrm{Conf}(\mathrm{L}^{\sigma})
is
generated by
\mathrm{Conf}(\mathrm{L}^{\sigma})^{(\pm 2)}
. By Dong Lemma,
v_{i}[z]
is mutually semi-local with any
distribution in
\mathrm{Conf}(\mathrm{L}^{\sigma})
. Thus

(z_{1}^{2}-z_{2}^{2})^{N+1}a[z_{1}].v_{i}[z_{2}]=0\text{ for some }N>>0.

We deduce that for a fixed integer
m
the function

k\mapsto\gamma^{i,j}_{a}(2k+\tau_{a},m-(2k+\tau_{a}))

(26)

is a polynomial of degree
\leq N
in the variable
k
.

Let
n\equiv\tau_{a}\,\mathrm{mod}\,2
be an arbitrary integer.
By Lemma
21.1
, we have

[t^{2},[t^{2},a(t^{n})]]=0

hence
a(t^{n})
acts on the
\mathbb{C}[t^{2},t^{-2}]
-module

V^{(\ell)}
as a differential operator of order
\leq 1
.
Thus the function

k\mapsto\gamma^{i,j}_{a}(n,2k+\tau_{i})

(27)

is a polynomial of degree
\leq 1
in the variable
k
.

Thus by Formulas
26
and
27
the function

\gamma^{i,j}_{a}
is a polynomial on its domain of definition
D_{i}
.
∎

Set
W^{(\ell)}=\mathbb{C}[t,t^{-}1]\otimes_{\mathbb{C}[t^{2},t^{-2}]}\,V^{(\ell)}
.
The space
W^{(\ell)}
has a natural grading. As a

\mathbb{C}[t^{2},t^{-2}]
-module it decomposes as

W^{(\ell)}=V^{(\ell)}\oplus\tilde{V}^{(\ell)},

where
\tilde{V}^{(\ell)}=t.V^{(\ell)}
.

Lemma 21.5
.

The space

W^{(\ell)}
carries a structure
of
(\sigma,\mathrm{L}^{(0)})
-module such that

(a)

(W^{(\ell)}))^{\sigma}=V^{(\ell)}
as an

(\mathrm{L}^{(0)})^{\sigma}
-module, and

(b)

(W^{(\ell)})^{-\sigma}=\tilde{V}^{(\ell)}
.

Proof.

The action of
\sigma
is easy to define: it acts as
1
on
V^{(\ell)}
and as
-1
on
\tilde{V}^{(\ell)}
.

By definition,
W^{(\ell)}
has basis

\{v_{i}(t^{n})\mid 1\leq i\leq p+q\},

where
v_{i}(t^{n})=t^{n-\tau_{i}}v_{i}(\tau_{i})
.

Let
a\in\in(L_{0})^{\sigma}\cup(L_{0})^{-\sigma}
.
By Lemma
21.4
there are polynomial functions

\tilde{\gamma}^{i,j}_{a}:\mathbb{Z}^{2}\to\mathbb{C}

which extend the function
\gamma^{i,j}_{a}:D_{i}\to\mathbb{C}
.
Then we define the action of
a(t^{n})
on
W^{(\ell)}

by

a(t^{n}).v_{i}(t^{m})=\sum_{j}\,\tilde{\gamma}^{i,j}_{a}(n,m)\,v_{j}(t^{n+m}).

By the principle of extension of polynomial identies,
the formula provides a structure of
\mathrm{L}^{(0)}
-module on the space
W^{(\ell)}
.
∎

The double of
V

Let
\mathrm{L}
be as before. Let
P\subset\mathrm{L}

and be the parabolic
subalgebra

P=C_{\mathrm{L}}(h)\oplus\mathrm{L}^{(2)}.

We consider the
C_{\mathrm{L}}(h)
-module
W^{(\ell)}
as a
P
-module with a trivial action of
\mathrm{L}^{(2)}
. The subspaces
V^{(\ell)},\tilde{V}^{(\ell)}\subset W^{(\ell)}
are
P^{\sigma}
-module, where
P^{\sigma}=P\cap\mathrm{L}^{\sigma}
.

The generalized Verma modules

M=\mathrm{Ind}_{P}^{\mathrm{L}}\,W^{(\ell)}\text{, }N=\mathrm{Ind}_{P^{\sigma}}^{\mathrm{L}^{\sigma}}\,V^{(\ell)}\text{ and }\tilde{N}=\mathrm{Ind}_{P^{\sigma}}^{\mathrm{L}^{\sigma}}\,\tilde{V}^{(\ell)}

admit eigenspace decompositions

M=\oplus_{k\geq 0}\,M^{(\ell-2k)}\text{, }N=\oplus_{k\geq 0}\,N^{(\ell-2k)}\text{ and }\tilde{N}=\oplus_{k\geq 0}\,\tilde{N}^{(\ell-2k)}.

We have
M^{(\ell)}=W^{(\ell)}
,
N^{(\ell)}=V^{(\ell)}
and
\tilde{N}^{(\ell)}=\tilde{V}^{(\ell)}
. Set

W:=M/Z(M),

where

Z(M)
is the largest

\mathrm{L}
-module contained in

\oplus_{k>0}\,M^{(\ell-2k)}.

Similarly, let
Z(N)
and
Z(\tilde{N})
be the largest

\mathrm{L}^{\sigma}
-modules contained in

\oplus_{k>0}\,N^{(\ell-2k)}\text{ and }\oplus_{k>0}\,\tilde{N}^{(\ell-2k)}.

Obviously we have
V=N/Z(N)
. Set

\tilde{V}=\tilde{N}/Z(\tilde{N}).

Fix an integer
k\geq 1
and elements

a_{1},\cdots,a_{k}\in\mathrm{L}^{(+2)}
,

b_{1},\cdots,b_{k}\in\mathrm{L}^{(-2)}
.
For any

(m_{0},\cdots,m_{2k})\in\mathbb{Z}^{2k+1}
, we have

a_{1}(t^{m_{1}})\cdots a_{k}(t^{m_{k}})b_{1}(t^{m_{k+1}})\cdots b_{k}(t^{m_{2k}})W_{m_{0}}\subset W_{m_{0}+\cdots+m_{2k}}.

So there are structure constants

\theta^{i,j}(m_{0},\cdots,m_{2k})
for
i,j\leq p+q

such that

a_{1}(t^{m_{1}})\cdots a_{k}(t^{m_{d}k})b_{1}(t^{m_{k+1}})\cdots b_{k}(t^{m_{2k}})v_{i}(t^{m_{0}})

=\sum_{j=1}^{p+q}\,\theta^{i,j}(m_{0},\cdots,m_{2k})v_{j}(t^{m_{0}+\cdots+m_{2k}}).

Lemma 21.6
.

The structure constants

\theta^{i,j}(m_{0},\cdots,m_{2k})
are polynomials in

m_{0},\cdots,m_{2k}\in\mathbb{Z}^{2k+1}
.

Moreover their degrees are bounded by a constant

N(k)
which only depends on
k
.

Proof.

Set
I=U(\mathrm{L})\mathrm{L}^{(2)}\cap U(\mathrm{L})^{(0)}
. By Poincaré-Birkhoff-Witt Theorem,
we have

C_{U(\mathrm{L})}(h)=U(\mathrm{L}^{(0)})\oplus I.

The Harish-Chandra homomorphism

\pi:C_{U(\mathrm{L})}(h)\to U(\mathrm{L}^{(0)})

is the projection on the first factor.
Using the explicit formulas of section
2.5
, it is clear that the mutidistribution

\pi\Bigl(a_{1}(z_{1})\cdots a_{k}(z_{k})b_{1}(z_{k+1})\cdots b_{k}(z_{2k})\Bigr)

lies in the algebra of multidistributions of

\mathrm{L}^{(0)}
. Thus the structure constants
\theta^{i,j}
are polynomial by Lemma
21.6
.

Moreover the multidistributions

\sum_{m\in\mathbb{Z}^{2k+1}}a_{1}(t^{m_{1}})\cdots a_{k}(t^{m_{k}})b_{1}(t^{m_{k+1}})\cdots b_{k}(t^{m_{2k}})v_{i}(t^{m_{0}})\,\,z_{0}^{m_{0}}z_{1}^{m_{1}}\cdots z_{2k}^{m_{2k}}

generate a finite dimension space, thus the degrees
of the polynomials

\theta^{i,j}(m_{0},\cdots,m_{2k})

are uniformly bounded.
∎

Lemma 21.7
.

We have

(a)

W^{(\ell-2k)}=0
if
k>\ell

(b)

the dimensions
\dim\,W^{(\ell-2k)}_{n}
are finite and uniformly bounded,

(c)

H_{0}((\mathrm{L}^{\sigma})^{(-2)},W)\simeq W^{(\ell)}
,

(d)

H^{0}((\mathrm{L}^{\sigma})^{(2)},W)=W^{(\ell)}
.

Proof.

We will repeatedly use that

\Bigl(\mathrm{L}^{(2)}\Bigr)^{k}.v\neq 0

for any nonzero vector
v\in W^{(\ell-2k)}
.
Choose bases
B_{\pm}
of
\mathrm{L}^{(\pm 2)}
which consist of elements in
\mathrm{L}^{\sigma}\cup\mathrm{L}^{-\sigma}
. For

b\in B_{\pm}
we write
\tau(b)=0
if
\sigma(b)=b
and

\tau(b)=1
otherwise. Also set
\tau(i)=0
if
i\leq p
and

\tau(i)=1
otherwise.

Since
V
is cuspidal, we have
V^{(\ell-2k)}=0
if

k>\ell
. Therefore for
k>\ell
, all polynomials

\theta^{i,j}(m_{0},\cdots,m_{2k})
vanish on the Zariski-dense subset

\{(m_{0},\cdots,m_{2k})\mid m_{0}\equiv\tau(i),m_{1}\equiv\tau(a_{1}),\cdots,m_{2k}\equiv\tau(b_{k})\,\mathrm{mod}\,2\}

so all
\theta^{i,j}
are identically zero. Thus

a_{1}(t^{m_{1}})\cdots a_{k}(t^{m_{k}})b_{1}(t^{m_{k+1}})\cdots b_{k}(t^{m_{2k}})W^{(\ell)}=0

for all
a_{i}\in\mathrm{L}^{(2)}
and
b_{i}\in\mathrm{L}^{(-2)}
. It follows that

W^{(\ell-2k)}=(\mathrm{L}^{(-2)})^{k}W^{(\ell)}=0,

which proves Assertion (a).

For a
k
-uples
{\bf a}:=(a_{1},\cdots,a_{k})\in(B_{+})^{k}
,

{\bf b}:=(b_{1},\cdots,b_{k})\in(B_{-})^{k}
and an integer
i\leq p+q
,
consider the multidistributions

X_{\bf a}(z_{k+1},\cdots,z_{2k})=\sum_{m\in\mathbb{Z}^{k}}a_{1}(t^{m_{1}})\cdots a_{k}(t^{m_{k}})\,\,z_{k+1}^{m_{1}}\cdots z_{2k}^{m_{k}}\text{ and }

Y_{\bf b}^{i}(z_{0},\cdots,z_{k})=\sum_{m\in\mathbb{Z}^{k+1}}b_{1}(t^{m_{1}})\cdots b_{k}(t^{m_{k}})v_{i}(t^{m_{0}})\,\,z_{0}^{m_{0}}z_{1}^{m_{1}}\cdots z_{k}^{m_{k}}.

Assume given
{\bf b}
and
i
. Since the structure constants

\theta^{i,j}
are polynomials,
there exist an integer
N
such that

(z_{i}-z_{j})^{N}X_{\bf a}(z_{k+1},\cdots,z_{2k})Y_{\bf b}^{i}(z_{0},\cdots,z_{k})=0

for any
{\bf a}\in B_{+}^{k}
and any

0\leq i,j\leq k
. Thus

\Bigl(\mathrm{L}^{(2)}\Bigr)^{k}(z_{i}-z_{j})^{N}Y_{\bf b}^{i}(z_{0},\cdots,z_{k})=0

which implies that the multidistribution

Y_{\bf b}^{i}(z_{0},\cdots,z_{k})
is local.
Since
W^{(\ell-2k)}
is generated by the modes of a finite set of local multidistributions, it follows from
Corollary
9.1
that
W^{(\ell-2k)}
is a

\mathbb{Z}
-graded space of growth one, which proves Assertion (b).

Let
N
be an arbitrary integer.
The set of all
(k+1)
-uples

(m_{0},\cdots,m_{k})
of integers such that

m_{0}+\cdots+m_{k}=N\text{ and }m_{1}\equiv\tau(b_{1}),\cdots,m_{k}\equiv\tau(b_{k})

is Zariski-dense in the hyperplane

\{(x_{0},\cdots,x_{k})\in\mathbb{C}^{1+k}\mid x_{0}+\cdots+x_{k}=N\}.

It follows from Corollary
9.1
(b) that the space of modes of
Y_{\bf b}^{i}(z_{0},\cdots,z_{k})
is generated by the elements

b_{1}(t^{m_{1}})\cdots b_{k}(t^{m_{k}})v_{i}(t^{m_{0}})

where
m_{j}\equiv\tau({b_{j}})\,\mathrm{mod}\,2
for
1\leq j\leq k
.
Thus
W
is generated by
W^{(\ell)}
as a

(\mathrm{L}^{\sigma})^{(-2)}
-module, which proves Assertion (c).

Replacing
\mathrm{L}^{(-2)}
by
\mathrm{L}^{(2)}
and considering the dual graded of
W^{*}
instead of
W
, we deduce
Assertion (d) from Assertion (c).
∎

Lemma 21.8
.

The
\mathrm{L}^{\sigma}
-module
\tilde{V}
is cuspidal and

W=V\oplus\tilde{V}.

Proof.

The
\mathrm{L}^{\sigma}
-submodule generated by

V^{(\ell)}
is a quotient of

N=\mathrm{Ind}_{P^{\sigma}}^{\mathrm{L}^{\sigma}}\,V^{(\ell)}
. By Lemma
21.7
the
image of
Z(N)
in
W
is zero, hence

U(\mathrm{L}^{\sigma})V^{(\ell)}\simeq V
. Similarly
we have

U(\mathrm{L}^{\sigma})\tilde{V}^{(\ell)}\simeq\tilde{V},

which proves the lemma.
∎

Proof of Theorems
19
and
20

Proof of Theorem
19
and
20
.

Set
\mathrm{L}=\mathrm{K}(2m)
if
m\geq 3
and
\mathrm{L}=\widehat{\mathrm{K}(4)}

otherwise.

Let
V
be a cuspidal
\mathrm{K}^{(2)}(2m)
-module.
By Lemma
21.8
, there exists another
\mathrm{K}^{(2)}(2m)
-module
\tilde{V}
such that

(a)

W:=V\oplus\tilde{V}
carries a structure of

(\sigma,\mathrm{L})
-module extending the given

\mathrm{K}^{(2)}(2m)
-action, and

(b)

the involution
\sigma
acts as
1
on
V
and
-1
on
\tilde{V}
.

If
M
is a simple
\mathrm{L}
-module,
then
W
is a cuspidal
\mathrm{L}
-module of second type and
V=W^{\sigma}
. Thus
V
satisfies Assertion (b).

Otherwise
W
is a
\mathrm{L}
-module of lenght
2
. Let

W^{\prime}\subset W
be a simple
\mathrm{L}
-submodule. We have

\mathrm{L}^{-\sigma}\tilde{V}\subset V,

where

\mathrm{L}^{-\sigma}=\{g\in\mathrm{L}\mid\sigma(g)=-g\}.

We deduce that
W^{\prime}\cap\tilde{V}=0
. Thus the first factor projection

\pi:W\to V

induces a isomorphism of
\mathrm{K}^{(2)}(2m)
-modules

W^{\prime}\simeq V
, which shows that
V
is isomorphic to
the
\mathrm{L}
-module
W^{\prime}
. Since
\mathrm{L}
-modules of second type are not irreducible as a
\mathrm{K}^{(2)}(2m)
-module,

W^{\prime}
is a module of first type. Thus
V
satisfies Assertion (a). ∎
